Monday 10th October, 2022

Biology & Life History 1


Giulia Bressan
CoNISMa; University of Padova

Shark tale: a study of tail morphology and ecology.
1.    Giulia Bressan; CoNISMa; University of Padova.
2.    Monica Barone; Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nation, Fisheries Resources Consultant (FAO).
3.    Fabrizio Serena; Institute for Marine Biological Resources and Biotechnologies (IRBIM); National Research Council
      (CNR).
4.    Carlotta Mazzoldi; University of Padova.

The earliest Chondrichthyes appeared 400 million years ago and have maintained their overall body shape essentially
unchanged since their origins. This long evolutionary history has brought them to develop body and fin characteristics and an
efficient swimming mode that allow them to occupy different habitats successfully. According to recent hydrodynamics studies,
compared to the other fins, the tail is the one that plays a crucial role in overall locomotor functions and its shape appears to be
correlated with the habitat occupied by the shark. Previous works have investigated this correlation analysing museum
specimens or scientific illustrations, while those that have focused on live animals were able to study only few individuals of few
species. Using photos taken from freshly fished sharks here, we investigated the diversity of the form of caudal fins in 37
species belonging to 3 orders to verify the relationships between the variability of shape and occupied habitat. Geometric
morphometric analysis was used to study the shape of the tail through the positioning of 5 landmarks. Moreover, the aspect
ratios of the caudal fins were calculated. Information regarding the habitat and bathymetric range occupied by each species
was obtained from scientific database. Relations between the occupied habitat and caudal fin shape were investigated taking
into account phylogenetic relationships.
This study contributes to the understanding of the relation between morphology and ecology in the light of the evolution of
sharks.

Keywords: sharks caudal fins; morphology; ecology
SI Abstract Ref: 261




Cristina Rodriguez-Cabello
Spanish Institute of Oceanography (IEO). Oceanographic Centre of Santander

Fitting a biphasic growth model to the elasmobranch Scyliorhinus canicula (L. 1758) in the Cantabrian
Sea (NE Atlantic).
1.    Cristina Rodríguez-Cabello. Spanish Institute of Oceanography (IEO).
2.    Larissa Modica. Spanish Institute of Oceanography (IEO).
3.    Fráncisco Sánchez. Spanish Institute of Oceanography (IEO).

A number of different models have been developed to describe growth (e.g., von Bertalanffy, Gompertz, Logistic), however no
single growth model provides the best fit for describing a population growth. This study presents the results obtained by fitting a
biphasic growth model to describe lifetime growth of Scyliorhinus canicula population in the Cantabrian Sea (NE Atlantic). Data
used for all these models was based on tag-recapture database updated from a tagging program conducted in the study area
since 1993. A total of 14091 lesser spotted dogfish have been tagged during this period, 7638 males and 6453 females
respectively from which 496 were recaptured. Parameters obtained from Lester growth model (LGM) provided a juvenile annual
growth rate (h= 57.4 ±1.2 mm) and reproductive investment (g~ 0.20). Besides, after conversion, the following von Bertalanffy
growth parameters (VBGC), for sexes combined, valid only for post reproductory lifetime, were: Linf=84.5 cm; k=0.083, t0=-
2.25. Growth models (VB), such as Gompertz or logistic were compared and differences were discussed taking into account
both life history traits and fishing pressure acting on the population studied. Finally mortality estimates (M) based on previous
growth models were calculated. Parameters derived from growth models are used to estimate important life history traits.

Keywords: Scyliorhinus canicula; growth models; tag-recapture; biphasic growth; Cantabrian Sea
SI Abstract Ref: 260




                                                                    Sharks International 2022                           Page       4
Dr Joni Pini-Fitzsimmons
School of Natural Sciences, Faculty of Science and Engineering, Macquarie University

First evidence of sound production in wild stingrays
1.    Dr Lachlan C. Fetterplace, Department of Aquatic Resources, Institute of Coastal Research, Swedish University of
      Agricultural Sciences & Fish Thinkers Research Group
2.    J. Javier Delgado Esteban, Whale Nation Studio
3.    John Gaskell, Reef Catchments & Sharks and Rays Australia
4.    Dr Barbara E. Wueringer, School of Natural Sciences, Faculty of Science and Engineering, Macquarie University & Sharks
      and Rays Australia

Sharks, rays, and skates - the elasmobranchii - are currently not known to actively produce sounds in the wild. Here, we
present multiple records of voluntary sound production in the wild, by two species of stingray, namely the mangrove whipray
Urogymnus granulatus and the cowtail stingray Pastinachus ater. Sounds recorded from both species are characterised by a
series of very short and loud clicks and appear to serve the purpose of agonistic displays. In all recorded observations, a ray
commenced producing sounds in response to an observer approaching closely, and ceased sound production when the
distance between the ray and observer increased. Our study illustrates that elasmobranchs can produce sounds, and that more
research into this ability is required.

Keywords: batoidea; sound production; communication; agonistic display; predator avoidance
SI Abstract Ref: 214




Fidji Berio
ISEM, CNRS, IRD, EPHE, Univ. Montpellier, Montpellier, Place Eugène Bataillon, 34090 Montpellier, France

Hatching in an aquarium: first insights from successful breeding of the spotted ratfish (Hydrolagus
colliei)
1.  Fidji, Berio; ISEM, CNRS, IRD, EPHE, Univ. Montpellier, Montpellier, Place Eugène Bataillon, 34090 Montpellier, France.
2.  Raphaël, Charron; Planet Ocean Montpellier, Allée Ulysse Odysseum, CS 79562, 34960 Montpellier Cedex 2, France.
3.  Jean-Marie, Dagouret; Planet Ocean Montpellier, Allée Ulysse Odysseum, CS 79562, 34960 Montpellier Cedex 2, France.
4.  Florent, De Gasperis; Planet Ocean Montpellier, Allée Ulysse Odysseum, CS 79562, 34960 Montpellier Cedex 2, France.
5.  Aurore, Éon; Planet Ocean Montpellier, Allée Ulysse Odysseum, CS 79562, 34960 Montpellier Cedex 2, France.
6.  Lola, Lambert; Planet Ocean Montpellier, Allée Ulysse Odysseum, CS 79562, 34960 Montpellier Cedex 2, France.
7.  Emmanuel, Meunier; VETOFISH, 7 Rue de la Salamandre, 13220 Chateauneuf-les-Martigues, France.
8.  Morrigane, Simonet; Planet Ocean Montpellier, Allée Ulysse Odysseum, CS 79562, 34960 Montpellier Cedex 2, France.
9.  Nathalie, Verschraegen; Planet Ocean Montpellier, Allée Ulysse Odysseum, CS 79562, 34960 Montpellier Cedex 2,
    France.
10. Nicolas, Hirel; Planet Ocean Montpellier, Allée Ulysse Odysseum, CS 79562, 34960 Montpellier Cedex 2, France.

Among the numerous aquatic species displayed in aquaria worldwide, chimaeroid fishes play a special role. Not only these
scarce and delicate species are very attractive to the visitor’s education but they still have unsuspected ecological adaptations
to deep-sea environments. Knowledge of these adaptations and life traits of deep-sea species are key elements to their
conservation, even for species that are not directly endangered by anthropic activities. The basic nutritional and environmental
needs of spotted ratfish (Hydrolagus colliei) are known, however, husbandry and breeding of this species still require crucial
information to be successfully achieved. Successful breeding was first reported in Japan in early 2000 with barely one year of
survival. In Europe, the EUAC Ratfish Monitoring Program reported mating behaviors between captive H. colliei specimens as
well as egg-laying for more than two decades. However, few hatchings only were reported between 2008 and 2015, with
subsequent poor survival rates. Since 2018, Planet Ocean Montpellier (POM, France) aquarists observed an increased number
of egg cases in tanks, inducing a greater number of hatchings in 2019. To date, the oldest H. colliei specimens born in captivity
at POM are more than three years old. Since 2019, egg cases and juveniles are closely monitored and embryonic development
is regularly recorded. This presentation will focus on H. colliei husbandry and breeding at POM, in an attempt to reconcile
observations from aquarists and research. It will include environmental and developmental data that have been collected for 16
years on embryos, juveniles, and mature specimens.

Keywords: chimaera; breeding; aquarium
SI Abstract Ref: 44

Threatened Species - Spotlight on Angel Sharks, Sawfish & Rhinorays 1



David Jimenez Alvarado
IU-EcoAqua, University of Las Palmas de Gran Canaria.

New mechanism for the identification of nursery areas of the Critically Endangered Angelshark (Squatina
squatina) in the Canary Islands archipelago.
1.    David Jiménez Alvarado; IU-EcoAqua, University of Las Palmas de Gran Canaria & Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands
2.    Eva Meyers; Zoological Research Museum Alexander Koenig & Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands
3.    Michael Sealey; Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands
4.    Maria Belén Caro; Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands
5.    Hector Toledo; Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands
6.    Charlotte Pike; Zoological Society of London & Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands
7.    Joanna Barker; Zoological Society of London & Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands


The Angelshark (Squatina squatina) is listed as Critically Endangered by the IUCN and the Spanish Catalogue of Endangered
Species. It has suffered a decline across Europe of at least 80% during the last century, mainly attributed to overfishing and
habitat degradation. However, a uniquely large stronghold for this species remains in an island archipelago in the middle of the
East Atlantic Ocean – the Canary Islands.
In the Canary Islands this species has an idyllic place to develop its life cycle, with no trawling or directed fishing activity, a
variety of environmental conditions and abundant prey. A key part of their life cycle occurs in nursery areas, where the offspring
are born and spend their first life stages. Since 2015, the Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands (ASP:CI) has conducted different
studies focused in these areas, designed to assess nursery are criteria established by Heupel et al. 2007. ASP:CI have then
developed a point-based mechanism to compare different sites and identify those that are confirmed, potential and possible
nursery areas.
To date, ASP:CI have discovered seven confirmed nursery areas, six potential nursery areas and 42 sites where juveniles are
present but need further study. Further, this talk will describe some of the environmental and biological drivers of juvenile Angel
shark distribution across the archipelago.
ASP:CI nursery area research was funded by Shark Conservation Fund, Disney Conservation Fund, Save our Seas
Foundation, The Biodiversity Consultancy, BIAZA, German Elasmobranch Group and WWF Netherlands.


Keywords: Angelshark; Squatina squatina; nursery area; Squatinidae; Canary Islands
SI Abstract Ref: 366




Mia Groeneveld
Department of Genetics, Stellenbosch University, South Africa

Towards resolving genetic differences and population distributions of rhino rays from the Southwest
Indian Ocean region
1.    Mia J, Groeneveld; Department of Genetics, Stellenbosch University, South Africa
2.    Aletta E, Bester- van der Merwe; Department of Genetics, Stellenbosch University, South Africa
3.    Aidan, Price; Department of Genetics, Stellenbosch University, South Africa
4.    Juliana D, Klein; Department of Genetics, Stellenbosch University, South Africa
5.    Michaela, van Staden; Department of Genetics, Stellenbosch University, South Africa
6.    Mark E, Bond; Department of Biological Sciences, Florida International University, Florida, USA
7.    David A, Ebert; Pacific Shark Research Center, Moss Landing Marine Laboratories, Moss Landing, USA and South
      African Institute for Aquatic Biodiversity
8.    Jeremy, Kiszka; Department of Biological Sciences, Florida International University, Florida, USA
9.    John, Nevill; Environment Seychelles, Mahé, Seychelles
10.   Angus, Macdonald; School of Life Sciences, University of KwaZulu-Natal, South Africa
11.   Bruce, Mann; Oceanographic Research Institute, Durban, South Africa
12.   Rhett, Bennett; Wildlife Conservation Society
13.   Katie S, Gledhill; Fish Ecology Lab, University of Technology Sydney, Broadway, Sydney, Australia

Rhino rays or wedgefishes (Rhynchobatus spp) are threatened by illegal trade globally and in the Southwest Indian Ocean
(SWIO) due to their high-value fins on the international shark-fin market. Rhynchobatus australiae and Rhynchobatus
djiddensis are classified as Critically Endangered on the International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN) Red List of
Threatened Species, yet little is known about the distribution of genetic diversity and populations across the SWIO region. This
hinders assessments of population trends that are required for effective conservation strategies. Previous molecular species
identification confirmed all samples from South Africa and Mozambique to be R. djiddensis while those from Madagascar,
Tanzania and Seychelles were identified as R. australiae. Here we investigated the genetic diversity and connectivity within and
between species and sampling locations using sequence data from three mitochondrial gene regions (cytochrome oxidase I,
nicotinamide adenine dehydrogenase subunit 2, mitochondrial control region) and 9 nuclear microsatellite markers developed
from high-throughput sequencing data. We also developed molecular resources to aid in accurate species identification and



                                                                    Sharks International 2022                          Page      6
subsequent genetic analysis, including complete annotated mitogenomes of R. djiddensis and R. australiae and optimized
eDNA (environmental DNA) metabarcoding assays based on the 12S and 16S ribosomal RNA gene regions. This knowledge
could further the genetic characterization of populations of these and related species and promote the taxonomic resolution of
the Rhynchobatus species complex.

Keywords: Ion Torrent sequencing; microsatellites; mitochondrial DNA; mitogenomes; wedgefish
SI Abstract Ref: 4




Héctor Toledo Padilla
Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands

The value of scientific data, citizen science, and collaboration with stakeholders in developing a
Recovery Plan
1.    Héctor Toledo; Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands
2.    Eva Meyers; Zoological Research Museum Alexander Koenig & Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands
3.    Joanna Barker; Zoological Society of London & Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands
4.    David Jiménez-Alvarado; Universidad de Las Palmas de Gran Canaria & Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands
5.    Charlotte Pike; Zoological Society of London & Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands
6.    Michael Sealey; Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands
7.    Belén Caro; Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands

The Canary Islands have been identified as a unique stronghold for the Angelshark, Squatina squatina, because it can be seen
relatively frequently by divers and fishers. The species is highly susceptible to accidental capture in fisheries and habitat
degradation, due to its benthic ecology and coastal location. This has caused the species to be listed as Critically Endangered
on the IUCN Red List, and action is required for its protection.
In 2019, Angelsharks were listed in the Spanish Endangered Species List for Canary Islands waters, under “In danger of
extinction” (highest category within this legislation). In accordance with this listing, it was required that a Recovery Plan be
developed, including appropriate measures for the management and conservation of Angelsharks.
Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands (ASP:CI) ran a series of in-person and online surveys to collate information on Angelshark
interactions, perceptions and possible measures to improve their protection with three main user groups: divers, recreational
fishers and commercial fishers. This information was used alongside ASP:CI scientific data and a collaborative workshop with
stakeholder representatives to identify the location of conservation areas and co-design specific conservation measures in
order to safeguard the future of Angelsharks in their uniquely large stronghold. This presentation will outline our key findings
and the importance of using a participatory approach to design conservation measures for a Critically Endangered species.
This work, led by ASP:CI, was funded by Shark Conservation Fund and through a contract with the Canary Islands
Government, co-financed by the FEDER 2014-2020 Operational Program.

Keywords: Angelshark; Canary Islands; recovery plan; co-design; conservation measures
SI Abstract Ref: 287




Renata Daldin Leite
Universidade Federal do Paraná (UFPR)

Dichlorodiphenyldichloroethylene (DDE), a legacy agrochemical metabolite, in the Critically Endangered
Brazilian guitarfish Pseudobatos horkelii from Southeastern Brazil
1.    Renata Daldin, Leite; Universidade Federal do Paraná (UFPR)
2.    Ana Paula Chaves, Silva; Universidade de São Paulo (USP)
3.    Eloísa Pinheiro, Giareta; Universidade Federal do Paraná (UFPR)
4.    Natascha, Wosnick; Universidade Federal do Paraná (UFPR)
5.    Natalia ,Guimarães; Universidade Federal do Rio de Janeiro (UFRJ)
6.    Amanda Pontes, Lopes; Fundação Oswaldo Cruz (Fiocruz)
7.    Francielli Casanova, Monteiro; Pontifícia Universidade Católica do Rio de Janeiro (PUC-Rio)
8.    Carlos, Massone; Pontifícia Universidade Católica do Rio de Janeiro (PUC-Rio)
9.    Renato, Carreira; Pontifícia Universidade Católica do Rio de Janeiro (PUC-Rio)
10.   Marcelo, Vianna; Universidade Federal do Rio de Janeiro (UFRJ)
11.   Rachel Ann, Hauser-Davis; Fundação Oswaldo Cruz (Fiocruz)


Dichlorodiphenyltrichloroethane (DDT) is a broad-spectrum pesticide banned in Brazil since 1998. One of its main metabolites,
dichlorodiphenyldichloroethylene (DDE), is highly persistent, fat-soluble and extremely toxic to aquatic organisms. Scarce
studies in elasmobranchs are available, and none have been performed for the Brazilian guitarfish Pseudobatos horkelii.
Herein, DDE concentrations were determined in the livers of 22 Brazilian guitarfish individuals, five non-pregnant adult females,
two pregnant females, 10 embryos (eight females, two males) and five adult males and correlated to the hepatosomatic index
(HSI), gonadosomatic index (GSI) and condition factor (CF) for all groups. Maternal transfer was confirmed, as embryos
contained detectable amounts of DDE, albeit lower than in all adult groups. Higher concentrations were found in adult males
compared to non-pregnant adult females and embryos, and intermediate values were observed for adult pregnant females. The




                                                                   Sharks International 2022                         Page        7
higher values observed in males may reflect the maternal contaminant offloading processes through female ovulation and
pregnancy processes, resulting in lower DDE concentrations, as noted for other elasmobranchs. The positive correlations noted
between DDE and the IHS and negative correlations between DDE concentrations and the IGS for all groups (including
embryos) indicate potentially deleterious systemic health effects for the liver and reproductive systems, respectively, beginning
in the womb and indicating serious concerns for the species. Finally, negative correlations between DDE concentrations and
the FC indicate that this pesticide metabolite may result in potential general health effects. This is the first study to assess DDE
concentrations and maternal offloading processes in this Critically Endangered species.


Keywords: legacy agrochemicals; organochlorine pesticide; maternal contaminant offloading; DDE; morphometric indices
SI Abstract Ref: 279




Ryan J. Nevatte
School of Natural Sciences, Macquarie University, North Ryde, New South Wales, Australia

Population structure of commercially fished sawsharks in southern Australia
1.    Jane E. Williamson; School of Natural Sciences, Macquarie University, North Ryde, New South Wales, Australia
2.    Barbara E. Wueringer; School of Natural Sciences, Macquarie University, North Ryde, New South Wales, Australia;
      Sharks and Rays Australia, PO Box 575, Bungalow, Queensland, Australia
3.    Michael R. Gillings; School of Natural Sciences, Macquarie University, North Ryde, New South Wales, Australia

Sawsharks are demersal sharks characterised by a distinctive saw-like rostrum with a pair of long barbels on the ventral
surface. In Australia, two sawshark species – the common (Pristiophorus cirratus) and southern (P. nudipinnis) sawshark – are
frequent by-catch in commercial fisheries and sold for human consumption. Although harvesting is currently considered
sustainable, much of the biology of these sharks is poorly understood. In particular, it is unknown whether these species display
genetically distinct populations throughout their ranges. Such information is necessary for effective management decisions in
fisheries.
We examined whether P. cirratus and P. nudipinnis display genetically distinct populations in the waters of southern Australia.
Analysis of two mitochondrial genes (Cytochrome-b and ND5) indicated that the two species are structured differently, with P.
cirratus consisting of two populations and P. nudipinnis consisting of one population. Tests for population expansion also
highlighted differences between the species. Signatures of population expansion were detected in P. nudipinnis whilst P.
cirratus showed an overall signature of stable population size, with evidence of expansion in one mitochondrial lineage. The
observed population structure and expansion signatures are likely due to oceanographic changes in southern Australia during
the Pleistocene. These results provide important information for management of sawsharks in commercial fisheries and
demonstrate that population structure in closely related species within the same region can differ, and therefore requires
investigation at the species level.

Keywords: population genetics; fisheries; Pristiophorus; biogeography
SI Abstract Ref: 251




                                                                    Sharks International 2022                          Page       8
Threatened Species - Spotlight on Angel Sharks, Sawfish & Rhinorays 2


Cat Gordon
The Shark Trust

Conservation planning for angel sharks in the Mediterranean
Cat A Gordon, Ali R Hood

Angel sharks (Squatinidae) are amongst the most threatened families of chondrichthyan species, with 13 of the 23 described
species in a threat category. Reliance often falls on reports of incidental catch, citizen science, social media, and interviews
with fishers to increase knowledge of distribution. Over recent years, in the wake of the Eastern Atlantic and Mediterranean
Angel Shark Conservation Strategy and inception of the Angel Shark Conservation Network, many more records have been
unearthed from the Northeast Atlantic and Mediterranean.
The Mediterranean Angel Sharks: Regional Action Plan sets out a roadmap for the three Critically Endangered species
encountered in this region - Sawback Angelshark (Squatina aculeata), Smoothback Angelshark (S. oculata), and Angelshark
(S. squatina). SubRegional Action Plans have since been developed for the Aegean Sea, Northern Levant Sea, Cyprus,
Southern Ionian Sea and Tunisia, with a view to facilitating further coordinated action by engaging regional stakeholders (e.g.
governments, industry) to better protect angel sharks.
Varying levels of management and compliance in each region means key policy actions are adapted according to requirement.
In working with regional partners, focus is on advancing policy developments, implementing existing regulations, and improving
fisher awareness/engagement to reduce bycatch mortality. Collaborative action is essential to build capacity for angel shark
conservation.
Through the development of SubRegional Action Plans, along with the global angel shark ID guide, it’s hoped that a spotlight
will be shone on this threatened family and highlight the importance of catch monitoring and management measures to prevent
other species from following declining trends.


Keywords: angel sharks; Squatinidae; implementation; conservation planning; Mediterranean
SI Abstract Ref: 207




Rossana Maguino
ecOceánica

Fisheries of the cryptic angelshark (Squatina spp.) in northern Peru
1.    Rossana Maguiño Napurí: ecOceánica
2.    Daniella Biffi: ecOceánica, Texas Christian University
3.    Keny Kanagusuku: Universidad Científica del Sur
4.    Alejandra Mendoza: ecOceánica
5.    Renán Flores García: Instituto Tecnológico Contralmirante Manuel Villar Olivera
6.    María Isabel Sanjinez Medina: Instituto Tecnológico Contralmirante Manuel Villar Olivera
7.    Gerson Talledo Peña: Universidad Nacional de Tumbes
8.    Dean Williams: Texas Christian University
9.    Shaleyla Kelez Sara: ecOceánica

Worldwide, angel sharks (family Squatinidae) are the third most threatened family of chondrichthyans. In the Southeast Pacific,
two species are recorded: Squatina californica and Squatina armata. In Peru, between 1996 and 2001, the sixth most fished
shark species was the S. californica. The catch of S. californica in Peru has declined by more than 90% between 2001 and
2020. A recent study conducted in Peru, confirmed the presence of S. californica and S. armata, and reported a potential third
undescribed cryptic species (Squatina sp.). This study aimed to characterize the biology and fisheries of Squatina spp. in
northern Peru. Five landing sites were evaluated between 2017 and 2019 (552 days). A total of 1,565 angelsharks were
recorded (average total length 84.8 cm [SD=13.0, range: 27.7-101 cm], 56% female). Ninety-seven percent of the landings
were from Puerto Pizarro (PP), the most northern site. Ninety-nine percent of the individuals landed in PP (n = 337) came from
fishing areas in Ecuador. Tissue samples of 110 individuals were barcoded using the mitochondrial cytochrome c oxidase I
(COI) gene. Sixteen samples were identified as S. californica and 94 as S. sp. Most of the S. sp. samples were from Puerto
Pizarro (n = 78). The results are concerning since direct capture of sharks has been prohibited in Ecuador since 2007.
Moreover, the study highlights the need for accurate landing reports in Peru given the steep decline of the total catch of
angelsharks.

Keywords: Ecuador; DNA barcoding; illegal; bycatch; pregnant females
SI Abstract Ref: 181




                                                                   Sharks International 2022                        Page       9
Ryan Daly
1. Oceanographic Research Institute 2. South African Institute for Aquatic Biodiversity

Long-term catch trends and risk assessment of the Critically Endangered white-spotted wedgefish
(Rhynchobatus djiddensis) from South Africa.
1.    Ryan, Daly; Oceanographic Research Institute & South African Institute for Aquatic Biodiversity.
2.    Denham, Parker; Department of Environment, Forestry and Fisheries & University of Cape Town.
3.    Geremy, Cliff; KwaZulu–Natal Sharks Board & University of KwaZulu–Natal.
4.    Gareth, Jordaan; Oceanographic Research Institute.
5.    Nkabi, Nomfundo; KwaZulu–Natal Sharks Board.
6.    Rhett Bennett; South African Institute for Aquatic Biodiversity & Wildlife Conservation Society.
7.    Bruce, Mann; Oceanographic Research Institute.


The white-spotted wedgefish (Rhynchobatus djiddensis) is a Critically Endangered shark-like ray in the family Rhinidae.
Throughout its Western Indian Ocean distribution, it is targeted for its valuable meat and fins and is reported to have undergone
major population declines. This study used two independent long-term (37 and 40 years) time-series catch data from
competitive shore angling and shark nets to investigate the size composition and catch per unit effort (CPUE) and conduct a
risk assessment for the population on the east coast of South Africa. From 1977 to 2017 the competitive shore fishery captured
7,703 individual R. djiddensis, whilst shark nets in the same region captured 2,856 individuals from1981 to 2017. Although the
mean annual sizes of net-caught individuals were above the size of reported sexual maturity, there was little evidence to
suggest that any individuals captured were reproductively active. Standardized CPUE from the competitive shore fishery
declined substantially between 1977 and 2017 and shark net catches exhibited a significant (p< 0.05) fourfold decline in annual
nominal CPUE from1981 to 2017. Ultimately, a risk assessment showed a 65.1% decline in abundance over a period of three
generation lengths, which indicates that the sampled population of R. djiddensis in South Africa should be classified as
Endangered. It is critical that the drivers of this population decline are identified and addressed to improve the conservation
outcomes for this extremely vulnerable species.

Keywords: wedgefish; Critically Endangered; West Indian Ocean Conservation
SI Abstract Ref: 65




Lucy Mead
Institute of Zoology, Zoological Society of London; Queen Mary University of London

Spatio-temporal and sex-specific patterns of residency and space-use in adult Angelsharks (Squatina
squatina) in Europe’s largest marine reserve
1.    Lucy Mead; Institute of Zoology, Zoological Society of London
2.    David Jiménez Alvarado; Universidad de Las Palmas de Gran Canaria
3.    Eva Meyers; Zoological Research Museum Alexander Koenig
4.    Joanna Barker; Zoological Society of London
5.    Michael Sealey; Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands
6.    Maria Belén Caro; Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands
7.    Hector Toledo; Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands
8.    Charlotte Pike; Zoological Society of London
9.    Matthew Gollock; Zoological Society of London
10.   Adam Piper; Institute of Zoology, Zoological Society of London
11.   Gail Schofield; Queen Mary University of London
12.   David M.P. Jacoby; Lancaster University

Understanding of species distribution and space-use is an essential prerequisite for effective marine conservation and
management, and with a third of all chondrichthyan species now threatened with extinction, a chronic lack of data on basic
ecology compounds extinction risk for many species. Squatina squatina (hereafter Angelshark), a bottom-dwelling coastal
shark, is currently listed as Critically Endangered on the IUCN Red List of Threatened Species. Despite recent conservation
attention, there remains a significant lack of ecological data related to this species, and understanding of Angelshark behaviour,
distribution and movement remains extremely limited. In the present study, acoustic telemetry was used to collect detailed data
on movement, residency and space-use in adult Angelsharks within the La Graciosa Marine Reserve in the Canary Islands,
with the aim of addressing key knowledge gaps and informing conservation policy. Between July 2018 and April 2022, 104
adult Angelsharks (72 females, 32 males) were tagged with acoustic transmitters and detected across 12 receivers. Significant
sexual variation was found in space-use and seasonal presence in the study area, with results suggesting some localised sex-
segregation outside of Winter months, when presence of both males and females peaked. While detections were concentrated
in the shallow waters between La Graciosa and Lanzarote (up to 24m depth), notable use of an offshore location (86m depth)
indicated utilisation of deep-water habitat, not previously observed in this species. The present findings have implications for
Angelshark conservation, particularly in relation to sex-specific space-use and use of deep waters across the study area and
more widely.

Keywords: Angelshark; Squatina squatina; acoustic telemetry; spatial ecology; sex segregation

Joanna Barker
Zoological Society of London; Angel Shark Project; Project SIARC

Angel Shark Project: Wales – understanding the distribution, ecology and habitat use of Critically
Endangered Angelsharks (Squatina squatina) in the northernmost part of their range.
1.    Joanna Barker; Zoological Society of London | Angel Shark Project
2.    Jake Davies; Zoological Society of London | Natural Resources Wales
3.    Ben Wray; Natural Resources Wales
4.    Nick Dunn; Imperial College London | Zoological Society of London
5.    Isabel Apetroaie; Imperial College London
6.    David Curnick; Zoological Society of London
7.    Alice Chamberlain; Zoological Society of London
8.    Charlotte Pike; Zoological Society of London
9.    Sarah Davies; Zoological Society of London | Natural Resources Wales
10.   Eva Meyers; Zoological Research Museum Alexander Koenig | Angel Shark Project
11.   David Jiménez Alvarado; Universidad de Las Palmas de Gran Canaria | Angel Shark Project
12.   Monika Goralczyk; Imperial College London
13.   Surshti Patel; Zoological Society of London
14.   John O’Connor; Welsh Federation of Sea Anglers
15.   Jim Evans; Welsh Fishermen’s Association
16.   Rowland Sharp; Natural Resources Wales
17.   Matthew Gollock; Zoological Society of London
18.   Fenella Wood; University of Aberdeen
19.   James Rosindell; Imperial College London
20.   Charlie Bartlett; Fisher
21.   Brett Garner; Fisher
22.   Dafydd Jones; Fisher
23.   Declan Quigley; Sea Fisheries Protection Authority

The Critically Endangered Angelshark (Squatina squatina) is part of one of the most threatened families of elasmobranch
(sharks, skates & rays) in the world. Despite a predicted 58% decline in their geographic extent over the last 50 years, a
population remains off the coast of Wales, in the northernmost part of their range. Angel Shark Project: Wales (ASP:W) was
launched in 2018 with the aim to safeguard Angelsharks in Wales with fishers and coastal communities, using heritage,
education and research.
Over the last five years, we have gathered 2,231 Angelshark records dating back to 1812 using a multidisciplinary approach via
fisher engagement, archival research, digital platforms and a community roadshow across Wales. A combination of quantitative
analysis, qualitative analysis and species distribution modelling have been used to interrogate the data and investigate
Angelshark distribution and ecology in Wales.

We will present key insights from these analyses, specifically the spatial and environmental trends in Angelshark distribution
and what biological parameters may be driving these. We will also highlight the importance of integrating community voices and
local ecological knowledge within the study of rare species, and how the results contribute towards delivering the Wales
Angelshark Action Plan.
ASP:W is funded by On the Edge, Welsh Government’s Nature Networks Fund and National Lottery Heritage Fund, with in kind
support from Natural Resources Wales.

Keywords: Angelshark; ecology; distribution; multidisciplinary; LEK

Movements & Habitat Use 1


Zachary Riskin
Large Marine Vertebrates Research Institute Philippines

Tails from the sea: pelagic thresher shark presence at a coastal seamount in the Ticao-Burias Pass
protected seascape
1.    Zachary Riskin, Large Marine Vertebrates Research Institute Philippines
2.    Alessandro Ponzo, Large Marine Vertebrates Research Institute Philippines
3.    Joshua Rambahiniarison, Large Marine Vertebrates Research Institute Philippines
4.    Martina Spakowski, Large Marine Vertebrates Research Institute Philippines

Pelagic species presence at Manta Bowl in the TBPPS was investigated using remote underwater video surveys. Globally
renowned as a reef manta ray (Mobula alfredi) cleaning station, this coastal seamount also hosts other endangered and
protected species of megafauna including whale sharks (Rhincodon typus), oceanic manta rays (Mobula birostris), scalloped
hammerhead sharks (Sphyrna lewini), and pelagic thresher sharks (Alopias pelagicus). This study discusses the presence of
pelagic thresher sharks at Manta Bowl. Pelagic thresher sharks are classified as Endangered on the IUCN Red List and are
vulnerable to overexploitation. This study took place between August 2018 and August 2019, with 335 out of 384 days (87.2%)
monitored. There was pelagic thresher shark presence on 120 of these days (35.8% of total days monitored) with a total of 211
thresher shark encounters determined over the study period. The study found that 43.6% of thresher shark encounters took
place in the late morning, and seasonal peak was observed during the cool dry months of the Amihan season (52.1% of
encounters were recorded between December and February). Despite the listing of pelagic thresher sharks in the CITES
appendix II in 2016 and its subsequent implementation in 2017, both targeted and non-targeted catch of the species continues
unmonitored and unmanaged. Considering the declaration of the protected status of the Ticao Burias Pass Protected Seascape
in 2018, this seamount represents a key habitat for the conservation of this species. As such, fishery management interventions
are urgently needed.

Keywords: pelagic thresher sharks; coastal seamount; RUV surveys
SI Abstract Ref: 19




Jarius Bradley
CIBIO-InBIO,
Patterns of residency and space use of arctic skates (Amblyraja hyperborea) across a developing Arctic
fishery ground
1.    Jarius Bradley; University of Delaware
2.    Nigel Hussey; University of Windsor
3.    Aaron Carlisle; University of Delaware

Understanding residency patterns and space use of a species is key to implementation of sustainable and productive
conservation management practices. Characterization of movement patterns of species within an ecosystem can provide
insight into why animals use particular habitats, such as for feeding or reproduction, or how movements are regulated by
various biotic or abiotic factors. Furthermore, by describing the fine scale movements of a species within an ecosystem,
patterns of habitat use, movement corridors, and focal areas can be identified, at both the population or individual levels. Due to
the logistical challenges of working in the Arctic, these types of studies have historically been limited compared to temperate or
tropical climates. The Arctic Skate (Amblyraja hyperborea) is a high latitude, deep water species that is commonly caught as
bycatch in both Canadian commercial and indigenous community fisheries. The life history characteristics of skates makes
them vulnerable to overexploitation, and careful management is required. In this study, we used acoustic telemetry consisting
of 81 receivers to study patterns of movement and residency of Arctic skates in Scott Inlet, Canada, the location of a
developing community fishery for Greenland halibut. Our goal is to characterize residency patterns and space utilization of 52
skates across 6 years throughout the community fishing ground. The intent is to elucidate both how the skates distribute
themselves within the region as well as identify how sex, size, and reproduction may impact these dynamics. Through
understanding these movement metrics, bycatch of Arctic skate can be reduced and appropriate management formulated.

Keywords: fish conservation; ecosystem-based fisheries management; population dynamics; communities and ecosystems;
elasmobranch ecology
SI Abstract Ref: 71




                                                                    Sharks International 2022                          Page 12
Walter Pisco
Instituto Politécnico Nacional (CICIMAR); MIGRAMAR

Habitat suitability of whale shark Rhincodon typus in the Eastern Tropical Pacific Ocean
1.    Lic. Walter Pisco: Instituo Politecnico Nacional y MIGRAMAR}
2.    PhD. César Peñaherrera: MIGRAMAR
3.    PhD. Alex Hearn
4.    PhD. Héctor Guzman

Whale shark (Rhincodon typus) are listed as endangered by the International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN). The
species is found globally in tropical and warm-temperate regions with high productivity. In the Eastern Tropical Pacific (ETP) it
can be found in coastal and offshore waters from the Gulf of California (Mexico) to the coast of Perú, including offshore islands,
where its presence has been associated with primary productivity systems and oceanic fronts present in the region. This study
aims to describe the effect of sea surface temperature, current velocity, chlorophyll-a and bathymetry on the habitat suitability
of whale sharks at important aggregation sites in the ETP. A total of 93 individuals were tagged using satellite transmitters
during expeditions carried out between 2011 and 2012 along ETP. A total of 2399 occurrence data were obtained, which were
then used to create species niche models for the ETP using Maxent model. Sea surface temperature explained 42% of the
suitable habitat, followed by chlorophyll-a and current velocity with 33.5% and 20.2% respectively. Finally, bathymetry
accounted for 3.9% of the model. The habitat suitability index was high in areas close to the equatorial strip and change
seasonally. The models indicate that suitable habitat is related to areas where non-directed fisheries can affect the shark, as
well as other threats such as entanglements and ship strikes. This information contributes to the knowledge that should be
considered in management measures for the conservation of the species.

Keywords: climate preferences; niche; distribution; fundamental niche; habitat mapping
SI Abstract Ref: 120




Olaia Cacho Movellan
CICIMAR-IPN

Shark abundance and the effect of recreational diving in the Revillagigedo Archipelago
Olaia Cacho Movellan; CICIMAR-IPN and Pelagios KAKUNJÁ

The Revillagigedo Archipelago is a region of great ecological importance, and it is considered a hub shark conservation. The
presence of nine species of sharks in the area attracted diving activity within the park since 1980´s. The present study aims to
determine the abundance of sharks using two methodologies: visual censuses (UVC) and baited underwater video stations
(BRUVS), as well as to evaluate the effect of recreational diving on sharks. Relative abundances of various shark species per
dive site and per sampling year (2015-2019) were calculated for UVCs and constrained by MaxN for BRUVS (2014).
Contingency tables were made to determine significant differences in the abundance of the five most abundant shark species
associated to site and year. Triaenodon obesus was the most abundant shark with 90% occurrence in 2018, followed by
Sphyrna lewini 80% in 2015. We found significant differences (p<0.05) in the abundances related to the site and year of
sampling. On the other hand, six species were recorded in the BRUVS, but only two were the most abundant: Carcharhinus
albimarginatus and Carcharhinus falciformis. Roca Partida was the most sampled place with a total of 270 dives, followed by El
Boiler with 139 dives. We found a higher probability of observing larger groups of sharks with smaller groups of divers,
however, this was not significant. Therefore, the Revillagigedo archipelago serves as a refuge for different species of sharks,
with Roca Partida being the site with the greatest abundance.

Keywords: visual censuses; baited underwater video stations; generalized additive models
SI Abstract Ref: 14




Natalie Klinard
Integrated Fisheries Lab, Department of Biology, Dalhousie University

Exploring the seasonality of coastal reef shark populations worldwide
1.    Natalie Klinard; Integrated Fisheries Lab, Department of Biology, Dalhousie University
2.    Christopher Mull; Integrated Fisheries Lab, Department of Biology, Dalhousie University
3.    M. Aaron MacNeil; Integrated Fisheries Lab, Department of Biology, Dalhousie University

Reef sharks have experienced widespread declines across much of the world’s coral reefs as a result of decades of
overexploitation. Large knowledge gaps regarding the abundance of reef sharks worldwide have limited effective restoration
and management of coastal shark populations. Although recent research has assessed the global status of reef sharks, it
presents a snapshot in time with little indication of temporal variability in abundance. Identifying how seasonal changes in the
environment influence abundance is important to ensure sampling is conducted at appropriate temporal scales to accurately
quantify abundance, assess the role of reef sharks within communities and ecosystems, and ultimately implement suitable



                                                                    Sharks International 2022                         Page 13
monitoring and management strategies. To explore seasonality in coastal reef shark populations worldwide, we used data from
baited remote underwater video systems (BRUVS) in 371 sampling locations as part of a dedicated global survey across 58
countries, states, and territories. We developed a set of Bayesian hierarchical models that incorporate a continuous seasonal
function to develop a predictive model of reef shark seasonality in abundance globally. Determining how seasonality influences
reef shark abundance worldwide provides context to global catch records and abundance measures, allowing us to refine and
standardize abundance estimates, and aiding in determining the most appropriate time of year for sampling.

Keywords: FinPrint; BRUVS; abundance; seasonality; coral reefs

Jeremy Vaudo
Guy Harvey Research Institute, Nova Southeastern University

Long-term movements of silky sharks, Carcharhinus falciformis, in the Eastern Tropical Pacific Ocean
1.    Jeremy Vaudo; Guy Harvey Research Institute, Nova Southeastern University and Save Our Seas Foundation Shark
      Research Center, Nova Southeastern University
2.    Pelayo Salinas de León; Save Our Seas Foundation Shark Research Center, Nova Southeastern University and Charles
      Darwin Foundation
3.    Ryan Logan; Guy Harvey Research Institute, Nova Southeastern University and Save Our Seas Foundation Shark
      Research Center, Nova Southeastern University
4.    Bradley Wetherbee; University of Rhode Island and Guy Harvey Research Institute, Nova Southeastern University
5.    Mahmood Shivji; Guy Harvey Research Institute and Save Our Seas Foundation Shark Research Center, Nova
      Southeastern University

Despite the silky shark, Carcharhinus falciformis, being ubiquitous in tropical and sub-tropical pelagic waters and the second
most common species found in the fin trade, little is known about its long-term horizontal movements. We tagged 40 silky
sharks (7 males and 33 females) within the Galapagos Marine Reserve (GMR) with SPOT satellite tags during February and
July 2021. Sharks were tracked for up to 374 days, with 25 sharks tracked for over 200 days and 18 sharks for over 300 days.
All but one shark left the GMR, however, sharks were observed within the reserve during all months of the study and individuals
were often in the reserve for months at a time. The number of sharks present within the reserve per month tended to decrease
over the course of the year, but increased during February and March 2022, suggesting the Galapagos Islands may serve as a
seasonal aggregation site. Female sharks leaving the reserve often displayed directional western movement traveling up to
~4000 km from the reserve. Despite large longitudinal movements, these movements were markedly latitudinally constrained,
with most shark locations falling between the equator and 5˚ N latitude. Male sharks did not make long directional movements.
Overall, for a wide-ranging species, silky sharks spent a great deal of time within the protected waters of the GMR, but their
seasonal movements still put them at risk from commercial fisheries operating in the region, evidenced by the capture of three
of the tagged sharks to date.

Keywords: satellite telemetry; habitat use; tracking; pelagic

Fisheries & Trade 1


Rachel Bustamante
Earth Law Center

Rights of nature approach to Shark conservation: a Panama case study
Rachel, Bustamante, Earth Law Center

On February 24, 2022 Panama’s President, Laurentino Cortizo, signed Law 287, “that recognizes the rights of Nature and the
obligations of the state related to these rights”. This signifies a shift to a proactive conservation approach, recognizing the
intrinsic worth and inherent Rights of Nature, while offering an opportunity to fulfill gaps in national legislation. By extension, this
law recognizes the rights of Sharks and their habitats.
This talk analyzes the threats depleting Shark populations while presenting ample evidence to justify enhanced conservation to
sustain the ecological role of this species. Panama, one of the most biodiverse countries worldwide, was selected as a case
study due to the country’s demonstrated efforts to environmental conservation, including the country’s legal recognition of the
Rights of Nature (having now been enacted in law and policy in more than thirty-five countries worldwide), as well as the
observed high presence of industrial and artisanal fishing that threatens several species with extinction. This case can act as a
microcosm of the conflicting economic interests that prevent enhancing Shark protection, while highlighting imaginative
opportunities for holistic, rights-based and equitable solutions. For example, opportunities will be explored to implement Law
287 through supplementary conservation policy based upon best available science to conserve and restore Sharks, thereby
helping safeguard the capacity of marine sustenance, coastal economies, and the future health of the Ocean against climate
impacts, and in fact, to help retain the country’s very name meaning—“abundance of fish”.

Keywords: Rights of Nature; Rights-based approaches; Shark Conservation Policy; Panama

Devanshi Kasana
Florida International University

Tracking shark fisheries and meat trade in Central America
1.    Devanshi Kasana; Florida International University
2.    Hector Daniel Martinez; Belize
3.    Jessica Quinlan; Florida International University
4.    Elisa Areano; Fundación Mundo Azul
5.    Julio Francisco Sánchez Jiménez; Fundación Mundo Azul
6.    Ana Paula Barbosa Martins; Dalhousie University
7.    Christopher Mull; Dalhousie University
8.    Elizabeth Babcock; University of Miami
9.    M Aaron MacNeil; Dalhousie University
10.   Demian Chapman; Mote Marine Laboratory

Global demand for shark products continues to drive overexploitation in many nations. Understanding the trade and
consumption of shark-derived products is currently dominated by literature focusing on shark fins while markets for shark meat
on global, regional, and domestic scales remain poorly understood. However, the trade in shark meat continues to surpass fins
contributing to food security and livelihoods in many coastal areas of the world. To understand the global trade in shark meat
and the fisheries supplying these markets, it is crucial to build baselines for catch and consumption at national scales. We
investigate one important supply chain in Central America, which is predominantly driven by the demand for shark meat. Shark
meat is infrequently consumed in Belize yet shark fishing occurs there to supply an export market in neighbouring Guatemala,
where seasonal demand for shark meat peaks during the Catholic Lenten season. We disaggregated landings and trade data
in Belize to the species level using multiple approaches and found trade from Belize to Guatemala involves at least 19 shark
species, including several listed on the Convention on International Trade of Endangered Species (CITES). We will present
species-specific baselines for production and consumption in both nations based on DNA barcoding of markets. This
information will bridge critical knowledge gaps in understanding the regional dynamics of shark meat trade in Central America.
Understanding this trade network has implications for the management of domestic fisheries in these nations, the
implementation of international trade regulations, and the conservation of species supplying this demand.

Keywords: shark meat; Central America; trade; consumption; CITES

Nidhi Gloria D'Costa
Recent Graduate from Marine Affairs Program, Dalhousie University, Halifax, NS B3H 4R2, Canada; Research
Assistant at Worm Lab, Department of Biology, Dalhousie University, Halifax, NS B3H 4R2, Canada

Identifying priority areas for shark conservation through the lens of global anti-finning legislations
1.    Nidhi, D'Costa; Marine Affairs Program, Dalhousie University, Halifax, NS B3H 4R2, Canada.
2.    Laurenne, Schiller; School of Public Policy and Administration, Carleton University, 1125 Colonel by Dr, Ottawa, ON K1S
      5B6, Canada.
3.    Leonardo, Feitosa; Bren School of Environmental Science and Management, University of California, Santa Barbara,
      93117, United States.
4.    Sara, Orofino; Environmental Markets Lab, University of California, Santa Barbara, 93117, United States.
5.    Echelle, Burns; Environmental Markets Lab, University of California, Santa Barbara, 93117, United States.
6.    Darcy, Bradley; Environmental Markets Lab, University of California, Santa Barbara, 93117, United States.
7.    Boris, Worm; Department of Biology, Dalhousie University, Halifax, NS B3H 4R2, Canada.

Shark conservation is being hampered by increased fishing pressure and regulatory shortcomings in many regions, resulting in
population losses for several species. A growing market for high-value shark fins, has fueled much of the demand for shark
products since the 1990s. National and international policy has tried to end the wasteful practice of shark finning by requiring
sharks to be landed whole and with their fins naturally attached (FNA), or even prohibiting shark fishing entirely in so-called
shark sanctuaries. This study examines anti-finning regulations across 278 exclusive economic zones (EEZs) and four regional
fisheries management organizations (RFMOs). Results show that as of 2020 only 36.7% (n=102) of EEZs were covered by a
FNA ban and only around 10% (n=28) of EEZs had a complete ban on shark fishing. Some of the largest shark fishing
countries (e.g., Pakistan, Iran) had weak legislation. Thirteen expert interviews were undertaken to contextualize and cross-
reference these analyses. Over the last two decades, all respondents indicated a declining (n=11) or constant (n=2) trend in
shark finning, with rising use of whole sharks and growing markets for shark meat and other goods. Overall, these findings
point to places where existing procedures should be strengthened and present regulations can be improved to reduce shark
finning and, eventually, shark mortality, at both the RFMO and national levels.

Keywords: finning; anti-finning bans; shark sanctuaries; EEZs; tRFMOs
SI Abstract Ref: 142




Sarah J. Nicholson
Toronto Metropolitan University (formerly Ryerson University)

We are unwittingly wearing shark: an analysis of squalene origin in finished cosmetics.
Sarah J. Nicholson; Toronto Metropolitan University

Anthropogenic activities, including exploitative fishing to harvest shark liver oil for cosmetic use, pose a significant threat to
sharks. While trade records and routes for shark fins and meat are relatively well documented, little is known about the use and
distribution of other commodities like shark liver oil. We do know shark liver oil is the richest natural source for squalene, that
the squalene market was valued at $129 million USD in 2020, and that the cosmetic industry is squalene’s principal end use
market. Endpoint market monitoring is crucial for improving our understanding of the shark trade, and tracking compliance with
trade laws and sourcing policies. Carbon ratio analysis of δ 13 C using gas chromatography - combustion - isotope ratio mass
spectrometry (GC-C-IRMS) has been shown to successfully determine squalene’s source even in finished cosmetic samples of
mixed squalene origin. A 2015 study used this method to test skin creams, and found that 20% contained shark squalene. Here
we complete a broader investigation of squalane’s use and composition in different types of cosmetic products. Using GC-C-
IRMS, we found shark squalene in 26% of cosmetic samples, and in 31% of these cases, squalene was of a mixed plant/shark
origin. Half of the top 20 global beauty brands contain shark squalane, 89% of products containing shark squalene identify as
cruelty-free, and 37% identify as vegan. This research points to the urgent need for species-specific analysis of the shark
squalene in trade, and greater transparency and oversight in the cosmetic industry.



Keywords: squalene; squalane; shark liver oil; cosmetics; makeup

Chelsey Young
NOAA Fisheries Service

Conserving the threatened oceanic whitetip shark (Carcharhinus longimanus) in the U.S. Pacific Islands
and beyond
1.    Chelsey Young; NOAA Fisheries Service, Pacific Islands Regional Office
2.    David O'Brien; NOAA Fisheries Service, Pacific Islands Regional Office
3.    Asuka Ishizaki; NOAA Fisheries Service, Western Pacific Regional Fishery Management Council
4.    Mark Fitchett; NOAA Fisheries Service, Western Pacific Regional Fishery Management Council
5.    John Carlson; NOAA Fisheries Service, Southeast Fisheries Science Center

The oceanic whitetip shark (Carcharhinus longimanus) was described historically as one of the most abundant pelagic shark
species in tropical seas worldwide. Currently, the species is classified as Critically Endangered under the IUCN Red List and
listed as threatened globally under the U.S. Endangered Species Act due to significant population declines and threats of
overfishing and the international shark fin trade. In the Western and Central Pacific, the species has declined by more than
90% since 1995, with bycatch-related mortality in pelagic longlines representing the most significant source of mortality. In
efforts to conserve the oceanic whitetip shark in U.S. Pacific waters, NOAA Fisheries has taken targeted steps under domestic
authorities to end overfishing and recover the species. These steps include developing a draft recovery plan highlighting
necessary recovery actions for the Western and Central Pacific population. It also includes developing regulations and
international recommendations to end overfishing, including an industry-led initiative to require a gear change in Hawaii’s
pelagic longline fishery from wire leaders to monofilament and minimize trailing gear to increase survivorship of hooked oceanic
whitetip sharks. While these efforts are expected to help end overfishing and contribute to the recovery of oceanic whitetip
sharks in the region, the species is highly migratory, occurs in the waters of 50+ countries, and is caught incidentally in
numerous fisheries throughout the region, with the United States representing only ~5% of the total catch. Therefore,
international collaboration will be critical to successfully conserving the oceanic whitetip shark beyond U.S. waters.

Keywords: oceanic whitetip shark; threatened species; conservation; fisheries management

Ana Barbosa Martins
Dalhousie University

Uncovering the Shark Meat Trade in Brazil
1.    Ana Barbosa Martins; Dalhousie University
2.    João Braullio L. Sales; Universidade Federal do Pará
3.    Jonathan S. Ready; Universidade Federal do Pará
4.    Jorge Nunes; Universidade Federal do Maranhão
5.    Natascha Wosnick; Universidade Federal do Paraná
6.    Rachel Hauser-Davis; Fundação Oswaldo Cruz
7.    Paulo dos Santos; Universidade Estadual Paulista
8.    Jones Santander-Neto; Instituto Federal de Educação, Ciência e Tecnologia do Espírito Santo
9.    Marcio de Araujo Freire; Universidade Federal do Rio Grande
10.   Luis Gustavo Cardoso; Universidade Federal do Rio Grande
11.   Cezar Fernandes; Universidade Federal do Delta do Parnaíba
12.   Lucas Werner; Associação de Tubarões da Costa - RN
13.   Cláudio L. S. Sampaio; Universidade Federal de Alagoas
14.   Luis Rodrigues-Filho; Universidade Federal do Pará
15.   Christopher Mull; Dalhousie University
16.   Elizabeth Babcock; University of Miami
17.   Aaron MacNeil; Dalhousie University

Understanding the trade in shark and ray meat is challenging given the overall lack of data, with uncertainty surrounding total
volume and species composition of catches, domestic consumption, imports, and exports. Additionally, marketing of shark and
ray meat under different or broad generic names (e.g., cação, arraia) distorts fisheries statistics and consumer transparency.
This challenge is particularly acute in Brazil, a top fishing nation and the largest importer of shark and ray meat in the world,
resulting in high production and demand, as well as diverse and widespread markets, both national and international.
Recognizing Brazil’s major role in the meat trade and the potentially increasing threats to shark and ray populations, the
primary focus of this study is to uncover the shark and ray meat trade along the coast of Brazil. We conduct surveys at
important landing sites for sharks and rays, fish markets, and supermarkets across 10 coastal states. Various methods are
applied for data collection, such as photographs, samples of biological material for DNA sequencing, and interviews with
stakeholders. This information is critical for a comprehensive understanding of the use of shark and ray meat for livelihoods,
and the impacts of catch and trade of shark and ray meat along the Brazilian coast, helping to identify species at potential risk
of extinction, contributing to CITES listings, and promoting sustainable fisheries strategies.

Keywords: Elasmobrachii; Fisheries; Shark Trade; ;

Leonardo Manir Feitosa
Bren School of Environmental Science and Management, University of California, Santa Barbara, 93117, USA

Refining the distribution and assessing the extinction risk of Sphyrna tudes
1.    Leonardo, Manir Feitosa; Bren School of Environmental Science and Management, University of California, Santa
      Barbara, 93117, USA.
2.    Christopher, Mull; Department of Biology, Dalhousie University, Halifax, NS B3H 4R2, Canada.
3.    Ana Paula, Barbosa Martins; Department of Biology, Dalhousie University, Halifax, NS B3H 4R2, Canada.

Sphyrna tudes is a small-bodied, poorly understood species of hammerhead shark. In 2019 S. tudes was listed as Critically
Endangered by the IUCN, with an estimated population reduction of 80% or more across its range in less than 40 years. The
rapid decline of S. tudes can be attributed to overfishing, mostly as bycatch in gillnet fisheries targeting the Acoupa weakfish
and shrimp trawling, as well as coastal habitat degradation across its limited geographical and depth range. Unfortunately, the
distribution of S. tudes has been mistakenly reported over decades, mainly due to errors in species identification or inaccurate
species distribution models. Here, we refine S. tudes distribution by cross-validating species distribution models (SDMs) with a
comprehensive literature review and individual record checking, and assess its spatial extinction risk through Bayesian Additive
Regression Trees (BART). Our SDM results demonstrate that, while suitable habitat extends from southeastern Brazil to the
Gulf of Mexico, the most important region in the world for this species is likely to be Northern South America. Given that
suitable habitat is constrained to coastal areas and dissolved oxygen was the most important variable for the SDMs, we
employed the BART with two possible scenarios: (1) fishing pressure reduced to zero and (2) mangrove areas increased by
100%. Our analyses demonstrate that S. tudes has a smaller suitable range than previously considered with intensive fishing
pressure, and its capture in fisheries need to significantly decrease to avoid extinction.

Keywords: small-eye hammerhead shark; Critically Endangered; species distribution models; Bayesian Additive Regression
Trees; Western Atlantic Ocean

Jaime Penadés-Suay
Associació LAMNA; Universitat de València

How geography and specificity determine tapeworm communities of blue sharks (Prionace glauca)
1.    Jaime, Penadés-Suay; Associacio LAMNA; Universitat de València
2.    Ana, Jarque-Rico; Universitat de València
3.    Jesús, Tomás; Universitat de València
4.    Francisco Javier, Aznar; Universitat de València

Over the last two decades there has been an effort to unveil community patterns of elasmobranch cestodes at a
macroecological scale, but analyses at infracommunity level are still scarce. In this work the helminth community parasitizing 37
blue sharks (Prionace glauca), both juveniles and adults, is described and compared with previous studies, performing a global
assessment of the relative role of the ecological and evolutionary factors in structuring their local tapeworm assemblages.
Shark came from two different non previously surveyed regions: Northeast Atlantic (Galicia, Spain) and Western Mediterranean
(Valencia, Spain). Helminths from nine different taxa were identified, of which four presented adult specimens and have already
been describe infecting blue sharks: Platybothrium auriculatum, Prosobothrium armigerum, Anthobothrium caseyi and Molicola
horridus. The five other taxa could not be identified at species level because of their poor condition. All six previous studies
providing infracommunity data about blue sharks encountered a group of four cestode species, thus described here as the
‘core’ community parasitizing blue sharks worldwide. Other cestode infections in blue sharks are discussed and a checklist of
adult cestodes infecting the spiral valve of blue sharks is provided. At a global geographic scale, tapeworm communities of blue
sharks were composed by 10 cestode taxa, 7 of which are exclusive to this host species and share two fundamental traits: their
cosmopolitan distribution and their belonging to cestode genera that typically infects carcharhinids. Showing that the influence
of ecological factors upon their tapeworm communities is highly constrained by a strong host specificity.

Keywords: parasitology; blue sharks; Cestodes; global distribution; especificity

Bryan R. Franks

It's about that time: Examining migratory behavior and potential departure cues for white sharks
(Carcharodon carcharias) in the western North Atlantic Ocean
1.    Bryan, Franks; Marine Science Research Institute, Jacksonville University, Jacksonville, FL, USA
2.    John, Tyminski; OCEARCH, Park City, UT, USA
3.    Nigel, Hussey; Integrative Biology, University of Windsor, Windsor, ON, Canada
4.    Camrin, Braun; Biology Department, Woods Hole Oceanographic Institution, Woods Hole, MA, USA
5.    Alisa, Newton; OCEARCH, Park City, UT, USA; ZooQuatic Laboratory, LLC, Baltimore, MD, USA
6.    Christopher, Fischer; OCEARCH, Park City, UT, USA
7.    Brett, McBride; OCEARCH, Park City, UT, USA
8.    Simon, Thorrold; Biology Department, Woods Hole Oceanographic Institution, Woods Hole, MA, USA
9.    Robert, Hueter; OCEARCH, Park City, UT, USA; Center for Shark Research, Mote Marine Laboratory, Sarasota, FL, USA

Describing temporal patterns of space use for migratory marine species can provide insights into the biology and ecology of
these animals including their role in ecosystem function. Identifying potential triggers for migration, over multi-year time scales
can provide a deeper understanding of the physiological and ecological underpinnings of behavior. To examine migration timing
and identify biotic and abiotic factors associated with migratory behavior in white sharks in the WNA, we used data from 65
sharks tagged with a combination of satellite and acoustic tags. Tag-based horizontal and vertical movement data from 2012-
2022 were used to discern temporal patterns of space use for individual sharks. Dates of departure from and arrival to
residency areas were compared to a number of environmental factors (e.g. absolute temperature, delta temperature over time,
photoperiod, moon phase) to determine their influence on migration timing. While there was substantial individual variability in
migratory routes and timing, abiotic factors including temperature appear to be significant drivers of departure timing from
residency areas. Ongoing analyses are exploring the presence and scale of intra-annual variability among individuals along
with inter-annual variability within individuals tracked for longer than one year. By providing information on multiple individuals
over a broad spatiotemporal scale, these findings provide key information to better understand white shark migration and space
use in the region. With changing ocean conditions, elucidating the effects of these changes on oceanic predators and their
migratory behavior will be critical to understanding the comprehensive impacts to marine ecosystems.

Keywords: migration; telemetry; ecology
SI Abstract Ref: 269




Francesco Garzon
MarAlliance; University of Exeter

Spatial ecology of the first recorded oceanic manta ray population in the Eastern Atlantic Ocean
1.    Francesco, Garzon; MarAlliance, University of Exeter
2.    Zeddy, Seymour; MarAlliance
3.    Rachel, Graham; MarAlliance, University of Exeter

The oceanic manta ray (Mobula birostris) is a circumglobally distributed species, but little to no information exists for many
parts of their range. The eastern Atlantic Ocean is a particularly data-deficient area, where manta ray presence has seldom
been reported and no description of the distribution, movement, and connectivity of populations within the region exists. This
study makes use of satellite telemetry conducted in Cabo Verde, coupled with ecological niche modelling, to provide the first
description of an oceanic manta ray population in the eastern Atlantic ocean and the most likely distribution of the species in
this region.
Repeated surveys between 2015 and 2019 and data from 11 satellite-tracked individuals revealed a reliable and continued
presence of manta rays in the archipelago, although the number of sightings was considerably lower than those reported for
some of the major aggregation sites for the species and no individual was ever re-sighted. Ecological niche modelling suggests
connections between the Gulf of Guinea and Cabo Verde, and revealed stable pockets of habitat within Cabo Verde.
The role of Cabo Verde in the life-history of oceanic manta rays in the region remains to be elucidated, though the low
abundance and re-sighting frequency, coupled with modelling suggesting seasonal expansion and contraction of suitable
habitat, suggests that Cabo Verde might be a key stop-over on a potentially longer movement corridor along the eastern
Atlantic Ocean.

Keywords: manta ray; species distribution modelling; eastern central Atlantic; satellite tracking

Fisheries & Trade 2


Kristen Koyama, Chelsey Young, John Carlson
National Marine Fisheries Service

Developing a recovery plan for the threatened oceanic whitetip shark under the U.S. Endangered Species
Act: metrics, actions, and challenges
1.    Kristen Koyama; NOAA Fisheries, Office of Protected Resources
2.    Chelsey Young; NOAA Fisheries, Pacific Islands Regional Office
3.    John Carlson; NOAA Fisheries, Southeast Fisheries Science Center
4.    Adrienne Lohe; NOAA Fisheries, Office of Protected Resources

The oceanic whitetip shark (Carcharhinus longimanus) is a globally distributed, highly migratory, pelagic shark species that was
once considered one of the most abundant sharks in tropical seas globally. Due to significant population declines resulting from
threats of overfishing and the international shark fin trade, the oceanic whitetip shark was listed as threatened throughout its
range under the U.S. Endangered Species Act (ESA) in January 2018. As required by the ESA, the U.S. National Marine
Fisheries Service (NOAA Fisheries) is developing a draft recovery plan for the oceanic whitetip shark that serves as a roadmap
to recovery and outlines the path and tasks needed to restore the species to the point where it no longer requires the
protections of the ESA. The draft recovery plan identifies both demographic and threats-based criteria to ensure population
viability and reduction of threats to the species, as well as comprehensive management, research, and outreach actions
needed to successfully reach those criteria. While NOAA Fisheries has the responsibility to develop recovery plans, successful
implementation of the oceanic whitetip shark recovery plan will require participation and cooperation from numerous
government agencies, stakeholders, international organizations, and non-governmental organizations around the world. This
presentation will highlight key elements of the draft recovery plan and opportunities for public and stakeholder involvement in
the recovery planning and implementation process.

Keywords: oceanic whitetip shark; threatened species; Endangered Species Act; recovery; fisheries
SI Abstract Ref: 190




Brendan Shea
Department of Fish and Wildlife Conservation, Virginia Tech, Blacksburg, VA, 24061, USA; Beneath the Waves,
Inc., PO Box 126, Herndon, VA, 20172, USA

Using Big Data to test the effects of spatial protection in remote island shark sanctuaries
1.    Brendan, Shea; Virginia Tech / Beneath the Waves.
2.    Austin, Gallagher; Beneath the Waves.
3.    Lindsay, Bomgardner; Virginia Tech.
4.    Francesco, Ferretti; Virginia Tech.

Sharks were historically abundant throughout oceanic regions; however, today many populations are threatened with extinction.
Recent conservation efforts include the creation of large-scale marine protected areas (LMPAs), with a major expectation of
LMPAs being the potential to conserve highly migratory species like sharks. In the last 15 years, 17 nations or territories have
established ‘shark sanctuaries’, whereby the targeting or retention of sharks is prohibited within territorial waters. Yet, the
conservation impacts of such sanctuaries remain unclear. Migratory species may spend limited time in protected areas, and
while targeting sharks is prohibited, commercial fishing continues in many sanctuaries. Though sharks caught in sanctuaries
are mandated to be released, at-vessel and post-release mortality rates can be significant, particularly in longline fisheries. We
combined data from regional fisheries management organizations and satellite remote sensing sources to model longline
fishing effort and generate estimates of annual shark catch and mortality in eight Western Pacific shark sanctuaries. We
developed a predictive model that estimates the number of longline hooks deployed using satellite-derived vessel positioning
data, then generated standardized catch-per-unit-effort rates for 11 species and genera using fisheries observer data and
quantified total shark catch. Finally, we predicted total mortality using species-specific at-vessel and post-release mortality rates
gleaned from the literature. We find that longline bycatch remains a significant yet undercounted source of mortality for sharks
within sanctuaries. Given the data gaps in regional stock assessments for many pelagic species, we discuss the implications of
our findings for stock management across the wider fishing region.

Keywords: shark sanctuary; marine protected area; bycatch; big data; fisheries
SI Abstract Ref: 217




                                                                     Sharks International 2022                          Page 20
Danny Poulding
The Shark Trust

The role of The Shark Trust Fisheries Advisories in promoting compliance and good practice within
elasmobranch fisheries.
1.    Danny, Poulding; The Shark Trust
2.    Ali, Hood; The Shark Trust

Within NE Atlantic waters, the legislative framework in which elasmobranch fisheries operate can be both diverse and complex.
Commercial fishers, recreational anglers, processors and fisheries regulators can find the relevant legislation and regulation
difficult to interpret and implement. Lack of understanding of such regulations may result in low levels of support within the
fishing industry and subsequent reduced levels of compliance, thereby undermining the effectiveness of any management
measures. Additionally, species within elasmobranch fisheries can often be similar in appearance and difficult to accurately
identify. A history of aggregated landings in some commercial elasmobranch fisheries has also contributed to unreliable catch
and discard data. Improving elasmobranch fisheries data is of prime importance in achieving sustainably managed stocks. In
both recreational and commercial fisheries, inappropriate handling of elasmobranch catches prior to release can also contribute
to increased post release mortality, further undermining those management measures designed to reduce fishing mortality. For
over 10 years, The Shark Trust has produced Fisheries Advisories for the fishing industry with the aims of protecting vulnerable
elasmobranch species and promoting sustainable elasmobranch fisheries. The Fisheries Advisories are used in numerous
sectors of the fishing industry and by multiple government agencies, in the UK and EU, and have been translated into seven
languages. In order to determine their effectiveness, The Shark Trust Fishery Advisories are critically assessed using survey
and interviews with various end users from within the fishing industry and fisheries regulation. Future development
opportunities for the Fisheries Advisories are discussed.


Keywords: fisheries; guidance; identification; legislation; discard-reporting

1.    Antonia Klöcker, IMR
2.    Dr Kristina Ø Kvile, NIVA
3.    Dr Mikko Vihtakari, IMR
4.    Dr Claudia Junge, IMR

The endangered basking shark (Cetorhinus maximus) is experiencing pressure from fisheries, coastal development, and
climate change. Yet, fundamental information about its spatio-temporal habitat use is lacking at the edges of its northern range,
increasing the vulnerability to human activities. Species distribution models provide a powerful tool to investigate how
environmental factors influence shark occurrence over annual and sub-annual time scales. Here, I will present preliminary
results for such relationships in basking sharks along the Norwegian coast, based on observations collected as part of a
national Citizen Science project called “Dugnad for havet”. In my talk, I will highlight predicted hotspots of basking shark

occurrence of obtained distributional hotspots with current and future human activities and highlight areas of elevated scientific
and management importance at the northern edge of the species’ distribution in the light of competing management priorities
and a changing climate.
