Evaluating models of advice for Thornback ray
1.    Marta, Ferraro; Groningen University; Wageningen Marine Research.
2.    Jurgen, Batsleer; Wageningen Marine Research.
3.    Katinka, Bleeker; Wageningen Marine Research

Elasmobranch are characterized by specific life history traits that make them vulnerable to fishing and other anthropogenic
factors. In the North Sea, rays and skates are mainly caught as bycatch, and have low commercial value. Studies are working
toward defining a picture of the status of their populations and addressing the mortality inferred by fisheries. Currently
population status is derived from a trend based analysis of scientific survey time series. Rajidae stocks fall in ICES category 3,
including data-deficient stocks which lack analytical assessments and whose advice is given under a precautionary approach
since 2012. Recently, new empirical approaches linked to life-history traits and fishing history have been developed. In this
study, different approaches applied within ICES have been compared for the landings advice of thornback ray, Raja clavata.
This is the main commercially important Rajidae species. Among them are the two trend-based rules “2 over 3” and “2 over 5”,
currently employed for the advice of the Rajidae complex, and the empirical rules rfb and rb. This study aims at providing an
overview of the implications of the main data-deficient models for the advice of Thornback ray, exploring the possibilities for
conservation of Rajidae species in the North Sea.

Interdisciplinary science to Remember Sharks on Holbox Island
1.    Nadia T. Rubio-Cisneros Laboratorio de Biología de la Conservación y Desarrollo Sustentable de la Facultad de Ciencias
      Biológicas, Universidad Autónoma de Nuevo León, Monterrey, Nuevo Leon, Mexico; Mar Sustentable Ciencia y
      Conservación, A.C. Monterrey, Nuevo Leon, Mexico; Center for Marine Biodiversity and Conservation, Scripps Institution
      of Oceanography, University of California San Diego, United States;
2.    Ilse Martínez School of Environmental Studies, University of Victoria, Victoria, BC, Canada
3.    Juan Carlos Perez Departamento de Ciencias de la Sustentabilidad, El Colegio de la Frontera Sur, Lerma, Campeche,
      Mexico; Jeffrey Glover Department of Anthropology, Georgia State University;
4.    José Ignacio González Laboratorio de Biología de la Conservación y Desarrollo Sustentable de la Facultad de Ciencias
      Biológicas, Universidad Autónoma de Nuevo León, Monterrey, Nuevo Leon, Mexico


The millennial coastal exploitation by humans has modified and depleted coastal food webs throughout the globe. Coastal
shark fisheries increased worldwide in the late 20th century due to global demand for shark products. In Quintana Roo, coastal
environments harbored diverse and numerous coastal sharks for millennia. This interdisciplinary research documents the
presence and exploitation of sharks on Holbox Island by integrating archaeological data, fishers' Local Ecological Knowledge
(LEK) surveys with a spatial component, open interviews, and literature analyses. Studying LEK of top predators in islands that
are contemporary touristic hotspots for global tourism and face environmental threats provides an opportunity to examine the
long-term availability and exploitation of top predators as a fishery resource and as a cultural and biodiversity component.
Seventy-eight LEK fishers' surveys allowed documenting over 15 shark species that were common in the Holbox region from
families Carcharhinidae, Shpyrnidae, Lamnidae, and Ginglymostomatidae. Archaeological shark records also denote the high
presence of shark families in the area. LEK fishers' surveys also documented the exploitation of sawfishes. The geospatial
component of the survey allowed identifying 20 sites that were important for sharks near the coastal area of Holbox. Results
contribute to understanding shark-human interactions through time and generate baseline knowledge that can help fisheries
and biodiversity management. The above is relevant since the local economy relies on healthy coastal landscapes and
biodiversity. Our methodology and results can help other Islands that face growing tourism like Holbox and have insufficient
data on fishing activities and landscape biodiversity.

Keywords: interdisciplinary science; Local Ecological Knowledge; sharks; islands; tourism

Assessing habitat use of three tropical coastal stingrays (Hypanus spp.) through stable isotopes and
vertebral microchemistry
1.    Aristóteles, Philippe, Queiroz; Programa de Pós-Graduação em Biologia Animal, Departamento de Zoologia, Universidade
      Federal de Pernambuco, 50670-901, Recife, Pernambuco, Brazil.
2.    Leonardo, Manir Feitosa; Bren School of Environmental Science and Management, University of California, Santa
      Barbara, 93117, USA.
3.    Francisco, Marcante Santana; Laboratório de Dinâmica de Populações Aquáticas (DAQUA), Unidade Acadêmica de
      Serra Talhada (UAST), Universidade Federal Rural de Pernambuco, 56909-535, Serra Talhada, Pernambuco, Brazil.
4.    Rosangela, Lessa; Laboratório de Dinâmica de Populações Marinhas (DIMAR), Departamento de Pesca e Aquicultura,
      Universidade Federal Rural de Pernambuco, 52171-900, Recife, Pernambuco, Brazil.

Understanding habitat use patterns on a species-specific basis is crucial information for more effective fisheries management.
In this study, we employed two techniques – stable isotopes of muscle tissue and vertebrae microchemistry – to analyze and
compare the habitat use of three co-occurring species of coastal Myliobatiformes stingrays in different stages of their life cycles.
We sampled a total of 37 individuals comprising Hypanus guttatus (n = 14), H. marinae (n = 11), and H. berthalutzae (n = 12).
We estimated the proportional contribution of marine and estuarine sources to the diet using SIMMR, a Bayesian stable
isotopic mixing model, while the vertebrae microchemistry data were analyzed through permutational analysis of variance
(PERMANOVA). Overall, both analyses clearly demonstrate strong differences in habitat use between species. Both SIMMR
and microchemistry results revealed that H. guttatus displays several entry and exit movements in estuarine areas, while H.
marianae has a high specificity for its coral reef habitats, and the prevalent use of deeper waters (between 50 and 100 m deep)
by adults of H. berthalutzae. Within species, PERMANOVA results yielded significant differences in habitat use between sexes
for H. guttatus, and H. berthalutzae, while H. marianae displayed no differences between sexes or life stages. This is the first
study to ever use data from stable isotopes and vertebrae microchemistry in elasmobranchs, which demonstrate to be accurate
descriptors of the distinct environments these species occupy. Furthermore, they provide a more holistic understanding of the
habitat use of these species across different time scales.

Sofia Green
Galapagos Whale Shark Project

Decade-long whale shark tracking from the Galapagos: identifying region specific anthropogenic risks in
the Eastern Pacific
Sofía M Green, Alex Hearn, Jonathan R Green, Leo Zurita, Bryan Vallejo

Region specific anthropogenic impacts on whale sharks in the areas around the Galapagos and the Eastern Pacific have not
yet been assessed. During the past decade we have gathered data on whale shark movements achieved through active
tracking through satellite tagging and passive tracking through photo identification efforts. Between 2011-2021 we have tagged
106 whale sharks with 129 tags, 124 of which gave enough data for analysis. We have also identified 522 individuals through
the global catalogue of whale sharks (sharkbook.ai). These data are helping us decipher the movement ecology of whale
sharks in the region, which anthropogenic impacts affect them most, and where in the ETP they are most heavily impacted. For
this purpose we are examining the impact of purse-seine fisheries, vessel strikes, and the effects of El Niño/La Niña ocean
phenomena on whale shark movements as a proxy for climate change impact. Fishing and marine traffic hotspots are identified
using open source AIS data and measured through spatial density analysis and spatial overlap with the movement patterns of
whale sharks in the area using QGIS and R. Further connectivity and population indices are reviewed by applying models
based on the lagged identification rate to the Galapagos photo-ID dataset. This study is complementary to the recent
publication by Womersley et al. 2022 which analysed vessel traffic impact around the globe and identified the Eastern Pacific
as the area where whale sharks had the highest overlap with fishing vessels.

Keywords: anthropogenic impact; movement ecology; telemetry; photo identification; spatial analysis

James Ketchum
Pelagios Kakunjá

Long-term movement patterns of tiger sharks around isolated oceanic islands
1.    James T. Ketchum; Pelagios Kakunjá
2.    Mauricio Hoyos-Padilla; Pelagios Kakunjá
3.    Alex Hearn, Universidad San Francisco de Quito
4.    Randall Arauz, Centro de Rescate de Especies Marinas Amenazadas
5.    Yannis P. Papastamatiou, Florida International University
6.    Chris Fischer, OCEARCH
7.    Miguel Gómez-García, Pelagios Kakunjá
8.    Peter Klimley, Biotelemetry Consultants

The Eastern Tropical Pacific (ETP) is a large expanse of ocean exceptionally rich in biodiversity with abundant sharks and
other marine predators, particularly around the oceanic islands of this region. However, shark populations are declining, and
little is known about their ecology, migratory movements, and spatial dynamics. Hence, our main goal was to study an apex
predator in the ETP from their northernmost refuges (Marías, Revillagigedo) to their southern abodes (Galapagos). We tagged
35 tiger sharks, Galeocerdo cuvier, with coded acoustic transmitters (V16) and 13 sharks with satellite tags (SPOT), in Marías,
Revillagigedo, Cocos and Galapagos islands from 2010 to 2018. Tiger sharks resided for longer periods of time and remained
at the islands for weeks, months and even years, especially in the warmer months, before performing large-scale movements.
Some of the large-scale movements were between the Revillagigedo and the Gulf of California, and between the Galapagos
and 200 km south of Malpelo and the northern coast of Ecuador. Our results show that most tiger sharks were resident and
moved over short distances within a home range of less than 100 km around different oceanic islands in the ETP, whereas few
individuals moved exceptionally large distances of 2000 km or more. Such movements have strong implications in the design of
protective zones around isolated oceanic islands and in the description of movement corridors for mobile apex predators in the
ETP.

Keywords: movements; satellite telemetry; acoustic telemetry; migratory corridors; Eastern Tropical Pacific

Diel vertical migrations of blonde skate (Raja brachyura) and spotted skate (R. montagui) in the

2. Jurgen, Batsleer; Wageningen Marine Research
3. Jan Jaap, Poos; Wageningen University Research; Wageningen Marine Research
4. Hendrik, Winter; Wageningen Marine Research

Horizontal and vertical movements of marine organisms are reflective of habitat preference and behavioural patterns in activity.

Movements & Habitat Use 4


Mark Erdmann
Conservation International Aotearoa

First insights into the movement ecology of Aotearoa’s oceanic manta rays Mobula birostris
1.    Mark Erdmann, Conservation International Aotearoa
2.    Edy Setyawan, University of Auckland
3.    Lydia Green, Manta Watch New Zealand
4.    Clinton Duffy, New Zealand Dept of Conservation
5.    Scott Tindale, Tindale Marine Research Charitable Trust

Unlike reef manta rays, the movement ecology of endangered oceanic manta rays (Mobula birostris) is poorly understood. In
Aotearoa New Zealand, M. birostris are known to occur off the North Island during at least the spring and summer months, but
their distribution and habitat use is largely unknown. Here we provide preliminary observations on the movements and diving of
M. birostris in northern New Zealand using satellite telemetry. A total of eight adult M. birostris were tagged with SPLASH10F
GPS Fastloc satellite tags (n=7) and miniPAT archival satellite tags (n=1) off the northeast coast of the North Island of New
Zealand in 2019 and 2021. Tagging duration ranged from 9-199 days, with minimum distances covered by each individual
ranging from 249-4,447 km. Analysis of these tracks suggest that oceanic mantas off northeastern New Zealand spend the
majority of their time (>84%) in surface waters (<50m depth), with occasional dives to the seafloor in 70-120m depth.
Importantly, six of the tagged individuals migrated northwards out of NZ coastal waters during the month of March as water
temperatures cooled, with three of them reaching Fiji or Tonga before their tags released. Of these 6 migrating individuals, five
made repeated dives (2-6 dives/individual) below 1000m, which we postulate may be to aid in navigation during northward
migration. Our study provides first insights into the movement ecology of M. birostris in New Zealand and suggests targeted
future research directions to better understand the movements and habitat use of this important yet poorly-known endangered
species.

Keywords: manta ray; satellite telemetry; New Zealand

Hannah Martin
MarAlliance

A baseline assessment of sharks and rays in Coiba National Park using baited remote underwater videos
(BRUVs)
Hannah, Martin; MarAlliance. Julie, Berry; Coiba Dive Centre. Rachel T., Graham; MarAlliance.

Baseline assessments provide information on species assemblages that can inform conservation actions through identifying
biodiversity hotspots or investigating the influence of different environmental factors on species abundance and richness. Coiba
National Park and its Special Zone of Marine Protection in Panama situated in the Eastern Tropical Pacific is a unique area of
marine endemism and high biodiversity. Several studies have been conducted in the area on the assemblage and ecology of
corals, cetaceans and selected shark and turtle species along with the socio-economic impacts of fisheries; however, the
assemblages of sharks and rays have yet to be quantified. Using baited remote underwater videos (BRUVs), we created a
baseline assessment of these two groups and investigated the influence of protection status and seasonality on species
richness and abundance. Three shark species and eight ray species were observed on the BRUVs (n=203 deployments).
Species abundance and richness were found to be higher within the Coiba marine protected area (MPA) compared to surveys
outside of the park’s boundaries. Species abundance and richness were not found to be significantly different between the wet
and dry seasons. The results from this study will help to inform conservation strategies and support greater management
effectiveness in the Coiba National Park and the surrounding area. With a well-enforced and successful MPA, Coiba can not
only provide an example of effective marine protection but it also has the potential to attract more tourists to the area, providing
an essential economic boost post-COVID.

Keywords: Panama; conservation; Marine Protected Area; Eastern Tropical Pacific; management

Sebastian Kraft
Centre of Marine Sciences (CCMAR), University of the Algarve

Seasonal movement patterns of the common stingray (Dasyatis pastinaca) in and out of a temperate
coastal marine protected area
1.    Sebastian, Kraft; Centre of Marine Sciences (CCMAR), University of the Algarve, Campus de Gambelas, Faro, Portugal
2.    David, Abecasis; Centre of Marine Sciences (CCMAR), University of the Algarve, Campus de Gambelas, Faro, Portugal

Marine protected areas (MPAs) are regarded as a useful tool to protect elasmobranchs. However, these species are generally
not a primary target when designing MPAs and how they benefit from these remains to be better understood. To this end,
acoustic telemetry has shown to be a very fitting tool. The Arrábida marine park is a temperate coastal MPA off the Portuguese
West coast, near two important fishing ports, Sesimbra and Setúbal. The common stingray (Dasyatis pastinaca) is a medium-
sized viviparous ray found mostly in shallow waters from Norway to Senegal and the Mediterranean. This species is caught
throughout its range and is currently classified as vulnerable by the IUCN. To study their space use and residency in this MPA,
28 individuals were tagged with acoustic transmitters and monitored for an average of 625 days and up to over 1000. Average
residency was low (Residency Index=0.211) ranging from RI=0.002 to RI=0.588. Their presence in the marine park was
markedly seasonal, highest during the coldest months and with virtually all individuals leaving during the warmer months of the
year. During their absence, individuals were detected in the nearby Sado estuary, where they likely remained for reproductive
purposes as suggested by the literature on Dasyatis. This pattern was repeated at least twice by seven individuals during the
entire monitoring period. These results show common stingrays are not protected year-round by this marine park, which they
leave during the reproductive season. We discuss additional measures that could be considered to improve their protection.

Keywords: acoustic telemetry; Dasyatidae; residency; movement; conservation
SI Abstract Ref: 311




Jonathan D. Mitchell
The University of Western Australia, Queensland Government, Department of Agriculture and Fisheries

Using a multi-disciplinary approach to manage shark bycatch and depredation at an offshore marine
park
1.    Jonathan D. Mitchell; The University of Western Australia and Queensland Government, Department of Agriculture and
      Fisheries
2.    Victoria Camilieri-Asch; The University of Western Australia and Queensland University of Technology
3.    Fabrice R.A. Jaine; Integrated Marine Observing System (IMOS) Animal Tracking Facility, Sydney Institute of Marine
      Science and Macquarie University
4.    Victor M. Peddemors; New South Wales Department of Primary Industries, Fisheries
5.    Tim J. Langlois; The University of Western Australia

Shark bycatch and depredation occurs frequently in the offshore marine parks surrounding Lord Howe Island, Australia. This
causes loss of bait, fishing gear and catch for fishers and sharks can be injured or killed when bycaught. To understand this
shark-fisher conflict and identify ways to mitigate it, we applied a multi-disciplinary approach including acoustic telemetry,
vessel tracking, logbook data and surveys of local fisher knowledge. Galapagos sharks (Carcharhinus galapagensis) were
tagged with acoustic transmitters and detected within a receiver array and charter fishing vessel activity was determined using
Vessel Monitoring System data. Kernel density analyses assessed the spatial and temporal overlap between shark detections
and fishing vessel presence, identifying several areas where higher fishing effort overlapped with greater shark detections,
predominantly at shelf edges. Substantially higher residency values were recorded for three individuals at a location where fish
waste was regularly discarded, suggesting a change in movement patterns resulting from food provision. Statistical models
indicated that detection rates were higher in locations with higher fishing activity, greater bathymetric complexity and during
summer. Logbook data showed a mean annual catch of 1,114 sharks with >95% released. Fishers reported methods that they
currently use to reduce shark interactions, including fishing deeper than 100m, switching from rod and reel to handlines and
electric reels, using jigs and lures instead of bait and rotating fishing locations. Diversifying target species also reduced shark
interactions. The novel multi-disciplinary approach used in this case study should be adopted elsewhere to help characterise
and manage shark-fisher conflicts.

Keywords: shark-fisher conflict; marine parks; Galapagos shark; acoustic telemetry; vessel tracking
SI Abstract Ref: 16




                                                                    Sharks International 2022                          Page       3
Ellie Moulinie
Save Our Seas Foundation D'Arros Research Centre (SOSF-DRC), Rue Philippe Plantamour 20, 1201 Genéve,
Switzerland

MantaCam: One year continuously monitoring reef manta rays at a key aggregation site in Seychelles
1.    Henriette Grimmel; Save Our Seas Foundation D'Arros Research Centre (SOSF-DRC), Rue Philippe Plantamour 20, 1201
      Genéve, Switzerland
2.    Lauren Peel; (a) Pendoley Environmental Pty Ltd, 12a Pitt Way, Booragoon, WA 6154, Australia, (b) Save Our Seas
      Foundation D’Arros Research Centre (SOSF-DRC), Rue Philippe Plantamour 20, 1201 Genéve, Switzerland, (c) The
      Manta Trust, Catemwood House, Norwood Land, Corscombe, Dorset DT2 0NT, UK
3.    Ellie Moulinie; Save Our Seas Foundation D'Arros Research Centre (SOSF-DRC), Rue Philippe Plantamour 20, 1201
      Genéve, Switzerland
4.    Dillys Pouponeau; Save Our Seas Foundation D'Arros Research Centre (SOSF-DRC), Rue Philippe Plantamour 20, 1201
      Genéve, Switzerland
5.    Robert Bullock; Save Our Seas Foundation D'Arros Research Centre (SOSF-DRC), Rue Philippe Plantamour 20, 1201
      Genéve, Switzerland

High-resolution, long-term datasets are critical in documenting ecosystem and population changes, as well as for differentiating
natural changes from those caused by humans. Mobilising the resources necessary to collect such data for vulnerable shark
and ray populations, however, remains a major challenge in elasmobranch conservation and research. Here, we used two low-
cost, high-resolution camera rigs (MantaCams) to monitor the presence and residency of reef manta rays (Mobula alfredi)
within a marine protected area in Seychelles, near-continuously for a year (April 2021 to April 2022). The MantaCams were set-
up to capture images every ten seconds at a key manta aggregation site offshore of D’Arros Island and were sequentially
deployed every few days to maximise site coverage throughout the year. The analysis of 1,326,926 MantaCam images from
133 deployments resulted in 913 sightings of 152 individuals (74 males, 76 females, 2 unknown), including the identification of
43 new mantas. MantaCams also observed nine adult females that were in various stages of pregnancy, as well as various
instances of courtship behavior. To-date, the sighting records from the MantaCams comprise 34 % of all records collected
since the establishment of the Seychelles Manta Ray Project in 2013, and were associated with the identification of more new
individuals at this site than had been recorded in any year prior for the whole country. Collectively, this highlights the value of
remote camera deployments to long-term, marine monitoring programs for vulnerable fauna that display high site fidelity.

Keywords: Mobula alfredi; MantaCam; Seychelles; site fidelity; population monitoring

Muhammad Moazzam
WWF-Pakistan

Conservation of sharks in Pakistan: a dilemma
Muhammad Moazzam

Pakistan has an important shark fisheries contribution annually about 12,000 m. tons. Although there is no aimed fishery for
sharks in Pakistan but sharks are caught as bycatch of gillnet, longline and trawl fisheries. Tuna gillnet fishery is major
contributor of pelagic sharks in Pakistan whereas sting rays and rhino sharks are mainly harvested through bottom-set gillnets
in coastal and offshore waters. At present, wings of stingrays are exported from Pakistan to southeast Asia which is in addition
to shark fins which is traditionally being exported to Hong Kong for almost two centuries. Although there are a number of laws
which are enacted to manage shark in Pakistan both at federal and provincial levels. Pakistan is a signatory of the CITES
Agreements and as such no sharks (or their fins) included on various appendices can be exported, however, shark fins
including those in the Appendix-II are exported from Pakistan. Provincial Wildlife law of Sindh puts a blanket ban on catching
sharks, however, the concerned Department has no infrastructure for the implementation on this law. In addition, Provincial
Fisheries Departments have put ban on catching and marketing of CITES listed sharks and all rhino sharks, however, there is
no control on catching and marketing of these species. There is no two opinions that shark resources are dwindling in Pakistan
and despite adequate legislative cover, there is no effective implementation mechanism It is feared that major depletion of
sharks species or even their extinction.


Keywords: shark; Pakistan; legislative control of sharks; shark fins; CITES

Bhagyalekshmi Venugopal
University of Kerala, India

Fisheries, livelihood and conservation of rays along South Indian coast
1.    Bhagyalekshmi Venugopal
2.    BijuKumar, A.

India is one of the leading elasmobranch fishing nations which is driven by the bycatch and small-scale targeted fishing. The
continuous decline in shark landing and increased demand of sea-food has instigated the demand of other chondrichthyans
such as rays, guitarfishes and skates in the country. The surveys and reports of these batoids landed along the coast are
based on commercially significant and conservation prior species. When batoids are extremely susceptible to population
decline from overexploitation, a species-specific list of batoids exploited in the fishery is inevitable. We recorded 45 species of
batoids (under genus Benthobatis, Narcine, Torpedo, Tetronarce, Narke, Dipturus, Orbiraja, Acroteriobatus, Rhinobatos,
Glaucostegus, Rhynchobatus, Rhina, Gymnura, Plesiobatis, Brevitrygon, Himantura, Pateobatis, Pteroplatytrygon, Maculabatis,
Urogymnus, Neotrygon, Taeniurops, Aetobatus, Rhinoptera and Mobula) from the major and minor harbours and landing
centre’s along the coast of 4 leading maritime states in south Indian, irrespective of fishery value. The analysis of landing
shows that batoid fishery in the country is still driven by highly threatened species. The electric rays, skates, small guitar fishes
and stingrays with no fishery value are equally exploited as the bulk catch of these species with low turnover in fishery get
auctioned for high amounts by the small-scale factories producing fish manure and animal feed. Consequently, the fisherman
retains the non-commercial bycatch during trawling. The study draws attention towards reconsidering the fisheries regulation
and conservation acts in India.

Keywords: Elasmobranchs; Batoids; threatened species; commercial fishing; non-commercial species

Irene Kingma
Dutch Elasmobranch Society

What have EU policy changes delivered for shark and ray conservation in Europe?
1.    Irene Kingma; Dutch Elasmobranch Society
2.    Paddy Walker; Dutch Elasmobranch Society

Two decades ago the situation for sharks and rays in European waters was pretty grim. There were no limits for fisheries on
elasmobranchs and endangered species did not have protection. Many species were in decline or had populations and
historically low levels.
A concerted effort by conservationist around Europe has led to a substantial increase in protective management measures
aimed at improving the status of sharks and rays in EU waters, but are we there yet? Have these changes in the policy
landscape yielded a significant result in the ocean?




                                                                     Sharks International 2022                          Page       5
We present an overview of EU internal laws and regulations that apply to elasmobranch management with a validation of the
level of change that has been achieved at a species level with an in depth analysis of several measures to pin point where the
key to effective conservation of elasmobranchs lies. We differentiate between management of skates and rays, which are
traditionally harvested commercially, and sharks which, with a few exceptions, tend to by bycatch species in EU fisheries.
We end with clear policy recommendations and follow up actions for legislators and, who knows, maybe even a song!


Keywords: policy; fisheries; Europe; North-East Atlantic; EU

Fahmi
Research Centre for Oceanography BRIN – Indonesia

A review of target shark fisheries in the Lesser Sunda Islands: do they support the sustainable shark
fishing program in Indonesia?
1.    Fahmi; Research Centre for Oceanography BRIN – Indonesia
2.    Selvia Oktaviyani; Research Centre for Oceanography BRIN – Indonesia
3.    Wanwan Kurniawan; Research Centre for Oceanography BRIN – Indonesia
4.    Benaya Simeon; Sustainable Fisheries Management Scientific Forum of West Nusa Tenggara, Indonesia
1.    5, Soraya Gigentika; Sustainable Fisheries Management Scientific Forum of West Nusa Tenggara, Indonesia

The shark fishery in Tanjungluar Lombok is one of the largest target shark fisheries in Indonesia. Fishers from this location
mainly use surface longlines to catch pelagic sharks in the eastern Indian Ocean and adjacent waters. We used 20-year
landing data to identify a possible decline in shark production due to the fishery. A daily basis catch data collection has been
applied at Tanjungluar since 2014. This method provided more robust data and showed a stable catch pattern rather than using
the sampling-based method applied earlier. In general, the total catch of sharks caught by the surface longline fisheries varied
from around 300 individuals per month in the low season to more than 1000 individuals in the peak season. Two CITES-listed
species, Carcharhinus falciformis, and Sphyrna lewini were dominant in the shark catch composition within the last two
decades. The silky shark contributed around 20-60% of the total shark catch every month and showed an upward trend in the
catch from 2005 to 2021. Interestingly, the implementation of either CITES mechanisms for the species or the national shark
management program seemed to have no or less impact on this target fishery. We discussed some possible factors that may
sustain the shark fishery.

Keywords: the eastern Indian Ocean; pelagic shark; Carcharhinus falciformis

Elisabeth Fahrni Mansur
Wildlife Conservation Society Bangladesh

CITES listings catalyze improved conservation prospects for sharks and rays in Bangladesh
1.    Elisabeth, Fahrni Mansur; Wildlife Conservation Society (WCS) Bangladesh
2.    Nadim, Parves; Wildlife Conservation Society (WCS) Bangladesh
3.    Mohammad, Shamsuddoha; Wildlife Conservation Society Bangladesh
4.    G M Masum, Billah; Wildlife Conservation Society Bangladesh
5.    Brian D., Smith; Wildlife Conservation Society Bangladesh

Unsustainable catches, driven in part by illegal trade, threaten 70% of the 52 shark and 85% of 71 ray species confirmed or
suspected to occur in Bangladesh. Reported landings of elasmobranchs from targeted and non-targeted small-scale and
industrial fisheries have declined by half from 6,234 tons over the past two decades, while official exports of dried shark fins
(combined with fish maw) spiked at over 2,000 tons between 2018-2020. There is growing concern about the harmful effects of
overfishing these apex predators on local livelihoods, food security, and the national economy.
CITES Appendix I and II listings of globally threatened sharks and rays have catalysed efforts to improve their conservation
management in Bangladesh. In 2021, the Bangladesh Wildlife Act was amended to strictly protect species assessed as
Critically Endangered or Endangered in the IUCN Red List and/or listed in CITES Appendix I, and to regulate possession and
trade of species assessed as Vulnerable and/or listed in CITES Appendix II. Non-Detriment Finding assessments for four
species/species groups, including 15 CITES Appendix II listed species, confirmed the need for improved interagency
collaboration, monitoring, and trade transparency. These NDFs also highlighted the need to reduce non-target catches and
domestic consumption and trade that will not necessarily be reduced through stricter regulations on international trade.
Improved prospects for conservation management of elasmobranchs initiated through collaborative research, policy
development, trainings, and educational outreach exemplify how CITES listings can catalyse institutional collaboration and
empower government and non-government stakeholders to implement evidence-based conservation plans.

Keywords: CITES; conservation planning; interagency collaborations; national policy development; inclusive communication

Lydia Green
1; Manta Watch New Zealand Charitable Trust, 2; Manta Trust

Overview of population demographics and habitat use of Mobula birostris in Aotearoa New Zealand.
1.    Lydia Green, Manta Watch New Zealand Charitable Trust
2.    Edy Setyawan, University of Auckland
3.    Mark Erdmann, Conservation International Aotearoa

Despite being fully protected in New Zealand waters since 2011, significant gaps remain in our knowledge of nearly all aspects
of the oceanic manta’s (Mobula birostris) life history and ecology in New Zealand. This lack of data has hindered
comprehensive conservation management planning for this globally threatened species; New Zealand’s Department of
Conservation currently lists the species as “Data Deficient” in terms of its threat classification. A focused effort to better
understand the New Zealand oceanic manta population over the past four years has resulted in a significant increase in
knowledge of this species’ population dynamics and habitat use. Combining citizen science and dedicated research efforts, 618
verified sightings spanning the time period of 1994 to 2022 have been collated from New Zealand waters, including 103 ventral
photographic IDs, comprising 71 females, 26 males and 6 individuals of unknown sex. Furthermore, preliminary findings from
intensive field work conducted in 2021-22 suggests that New Zealand supports a significant and healthy population of M.
birostris. Observations include extensive feeding aggregations of more than 50 individuals, sightings of heavily pregnant
females feeding alongside subadult males (disc widths of 590cm and 300cm respectively) and numerous accounts of active
courtship displays. Moreover, this population shows very low incidence of injuries (17 %). These initial findings support the
hypothesis that M. birostris are resident for at least part of the year in Aotearoa New Zealand and likely reproducing there and
suggest that this population may be critical in global conservation efforts to recover this endangered species.

Keywords: photo ID; endangered species; population dynamics; oceanic manta ray; New Zealand

Modelling the spatio-temporal distribution of devil rays in the western Indian ocean using observer data
from the EU tropical tuna purse seine fishery
1.  Gency L. Guirhem; AZTI, Marine Research, Basque Research and Technology Alliance (BRTA), Pasaia, Spain; Institute
    of Marine Fisheries and Oceanology, College of Fisheries and Ocean Sciences, University of the Philippines Visayas,
    Iloilo, Philippines.
2. Haritz Arrizabalaga; AZTI, Marine Research, Basque Research and Technology Alliance (BRTA), Pasaia, Spain.
3. Leire Lopetegui-Eguren; AZTI, Marine Research, Basque Research and Technology Alliance (BRTA), Pasaia, Spain.
4. Hilario Murua; International Sustainable Seafood Foundation, Washington, DC, USA.
5. Nerea Lezama-Ochoa; Institute of Marine Sciences, University of California Santa Cruz, Santa Cruz, USA; Mobula
    Conservation, La Paz, Mexico.
6. Shane P. Griffiths; Ecosystem and Bycatch Program, Inter-American Tropical Tuna Commission, La Jolla, CA, USA.
7. Jon Ruiz Gondra; AZTI, Marine Research, Basque Research and Technology Alliance (BRTA), Pasaia, Spain.
8. Philippe S. Sabarros; MARBEC, Univ Montpellier, CNRS, Ifremer, IRD, Sète, France; Institut de Recherche pour le
    Développement, Ob7, Sète, France.
9. Jose Carlos Baez; Instituto Español de Oceanografía, Centro Oceanogra´fico de Ma´laga, Fuengirola, Spain; Instituto
    Iberoamericano de Desarrollo Sostenible (IIDS), Universidad Autónoma de Chile, Temuco, Chile.
10. Maria José Juan-Jordá; AZTI, Marine Research, Basque Research and Technology Alliance (BRTA), Pasaia, Spain

The European Union and Seychelles tropical tuna purse seine fishery in the western Indian ocean incidentally captures three
species of devil rays, the spinetail devil ray (Mobula mobular), the sicklefin devil ray (M. tarapacana), and the bentfin devil ray
(M. thurstoni), all currently classified globally as Endangered by the International Union for the Conservation of Nature. We
used observer data collected on tropical tuna purse seiners in 2010-2020 to analyse the relationship between the
presence/absence of devil rays in the catches and the environmental conditions, the spatial and temporal variability of fishing
locations, and fishing operational variables. We found that higher probability of capture was predicted in areas with relatively
high chlorophyll-a concentrations, high sea surface temperature fronts, and low sea surface height. This may indicate that the
presence of this planktivore species might be associated with mesoscale features resulting in elevated biomass of zooplankton.
We also found higher probability of catching devil rays in sets on free-swimming schools of tuna than in sets on fish
aggregating devices and smaller tuna catch sets. The spatial predictions identified two important areas (the Mascarene Plateau
and central Indian Ridge) persistent throughout the year with higher probabilities of catching a devil ray, and two seasonal
important areas (the western Arabian Sea and Equatorial Region) with higher catch probabilities during the winter monsoon
regime (December-March). Information on the spatio-temporal and environmental preference of devil rays is crucial to support
more holistic spatial management and conservation strategies to further reduce the bycatch of these species.

Keywords: devil rays; Mobula; spinetail devil ray; bycatch; species distribution models

Hannah Calich
University of Western Australia

Physiological biomarkers provide insights into tiger shark movement
1.    Hannah J. Calich; University of Western Australia
2.    Ana M.M. Sequeira; Australian National University
3.    Bianca S. Rangel; University of São Paulo
4.    Shannon G. Moorhead; University of Miami
5.    Abigail Tinari; University of Miami
6.    Carolyn Cray; University of Miami
7.    Duncan J. Irschick; University of Massachusetts Amherst
8.    Emily R. Nelson; University of Miami
9.    Robert H. Michener; Boston University
10.   James A. Sulikowski; Arizona State University
11.   Charitha Pattiaratchi; University of Western Australia
12.   Austin J. Gallagher; Beneath the Waves
13.   Neil Hammerschlag; University of Miami

Physiological processes influence the movement behavior of animals. Understanding relationships between physiology and
behaviour can lead to breakthroughs in species’ movement ecology but linking ephemeral physiological conditions with
movement patterns has been an ongoing challenge. This is especially the case for migratory, free-roaming species notorious
for displaying highly variable movement over large spatiotemporal scales. Physiological and biological variables, termed
“biomarkers”, can describe underlying physiological processes that influence movement, such as energy storage and utilization,
growth, and development. Here, we aimed to determine the relative influence of physiology on intra-specific movement patterns
of a highly migratory apex predator, the tiger shark (Galeocerdo cuvier). We accomplished this by analyzing 50 measured
biomarkers for nutritional and energetic condition, metabolism, morphology, reproduction, and health, in addition to seasonality,
on the dispersion and scale of tiger shark movement across multiple temporal periods (7 – 25 days). Overall, nutrition-related
biomarkers had the largest relative influence on movement, followed by metabolic hormones, energy substrates, and
morphology. Reproductive state, health, and season had negligible influence on the movement patterns observed across the
temporal periods considered. These results emphasize the importance of tiger sharks acquiring, storing, and mobilizing energy
to support their movements, providing important knowledge about their movement ecology. Our work presents a novel
approach for addressing the challenge of linking movement patterns of free-roaming species to their physiology and has wide
applicability for future studies investigating the drivers of individual variation in movement of highly mobile species.

Keywords: movement ecology; physiology; satellite tag; boosted regression trees; Atlantic Ocean

Nakia Cullain
Dalhousie University; Marine Megafauna Foundation

Zavora Bay: A new critical area for reef manta rays within Africa’s largest identified population
1.    Michelle Carpenter; University of Cape Town
2.    Stephanie K. Venables; Marine Megafauna Foundation
3.    Andrea D. Marshall; Marine Megafauna Foundation
4.    Derek P. Tittensor; Dalhousie University
5.    Heike K. Lotze; Dalhousie University

Mozambique is a hotspot for threatened and data deficient species of sharks and rays, yet a lack of site-specific information
hinders the development of local and national species management plans. The largest identified population of reef manta rays
(Mobula alfredi) in Africa aggregate within a 350 km upwelling zone that extends from the Bazaruto Archipelago in the north, to
Zavora in the south. An 88% decline in sightings of M.alfredi along the Mozambican coastline represents one of the largest
declines on record. However, Zavora (a remote and less explored bay in the region), still has consistent and frequent sightings
of reef manta rays. Using sightings and photo-ID data from dive surveys conducted during 2009-2021, we investigate sightings
trends, habitat preference, and population demographics of M. alfredi in Zavora Bay. Our results indicate that compared to
other sites in Mozambique, abundance estimates for Zavora are still high, albeit on the decline. Our results also indicate a shift
from consistent year-round sightings to a seasonal aggregation from July-November, with complete abandonment of a
previously favoured cleaning station. The site and seasonal affinity of M. alfredi in Zavora Bay highlights the importance of
understanding fine-scale site use within the larger home range of this population to develop local management strategies. Our
findings provide evidence that Zavora Bay is a critical visitation site for reef manta rays in Mozambique and should be
considered a conservation priority area for this threatened species.

Keywords: threatened species; Mozambique; habitat preference; photo-ID; management

                                                                    Sharks International 2022                          Page      8
Benjamin Marsaly
School of Marine Science and Policy, University of Delaware

Movement patterns of the sicklefin lemon shark, Negaprion acutidens, around Tahiti and Moorea, French
Polynesia, South Pacific.
1.    Benjamin, Marsaly; School of Marine Science and Policy, University of Delaware, Lewes DE, USA.
2.    Pierpaolo, Brena; PSL Research University: EPHE-CNRS-UPVD USR 3278 CRIOBE, Perpignan, France.
3.    Eric, Clua; PSL Research University: EPHE-CNRS-UPVD USR 3278 CRIOBE, Perpignan, France.

The sicklefin lemon shark, Negaprion acutidens, is globally understudied compared to its sister species the Atlantic lemon
shark, N. brevirostris. Although it has been heavily targeted by artificial provisioning activities until 2017, its movements are still
unknown in the Society Islands, French Polynesia. Here, we used acoustic telemetry to study the residency and movement
patterns of the sicklefin lemon shark between June 2014 and July 2015. We deployed 21 receivers on the outer reefs of Tahiti
(n=11), Moorea (n=7), and Tetiaroa (n=3), an atoll located approximately 50 km north of Tahiti and Moorea. A total of 17
mature individuals were tagged internally by ingestion or externally using a speargun. Preliminary analyses indicate that sharks
were primarily detected around Moorea and at the Vallée Blanche, northeast of Tahiti, while no individuals were detected in
Tetiaroa. The number of detections is higher where shark-feeding occurred during the study period and decreases with the
distance from tagging location. Globally, the detections mostly occurred during the day, suggesting that adult sicklefin lemon
sharks use the outer reef during the day and move to deeper waters at night. Therefore, the sicklefin lemon shark seems to be
highly resident and could be impacted by artificial provisioning activities at a small spatial scale. Considering that artificial
provisioning is now forbidden in French Polynesia waters, such data is crucial to understand the long-term impacts of this
activity on sharks movements, and could pave the way for further studies focusing on dishabituation process regarding artificial
provisioning in shark species.

Keywords: spatial ecology; residency; acoustic telemetry

Sweta Beura
Zoological Survey of India

Integrative taxonomy, species composition, distribution and current status of deep-sea chondrichthyans
of India
1.    Kinattumkara Bineesh; Zoological Survey of India
2.    Sweta Beura; Zoological Survey of India

India is one of the leading chondrichthyan fishing nations. Most of the deep-sea chondrichthyans species are poorly known due
to their low market value and low targeted sampling for research activities. In India, deep-sea chondrichthyans are caught both
as targeted and bycatch, there is a lack of data on deep-sea chondrichthyans, despite their high market demand and catch.
The objective of this study was to characterize the catch composition of deep-sea chondrichthyans, using extensive trawl catch
data of commercial deep-sea shrimp fishery. The present paper is an attempt to provide new information on catch, integrative
taxonomy, species composition current distribution, fleet size, trade and usage of deepsea chondrichthyans in India. This study
based on the landing site surveys conducted during the years 2017-2021 at major fish landing centres along the west and east
coast of India and Andaman and Nicobar Islands. The present findings revealed 47 species under 19 families caught in
commercial and artisanal fisheries. Trawl surveys show that Cephaloscyllium silasi distribution restricted to off Kerala and
South Andaman Islands of India. Hexatrygon bickelli and Squalus hemipinnis were confirmed from Kerala and Andaman
Islands respectively. Highest diversity in the landings was observed at Kerala with several species recorded during the present
study was new to Indian waters. Fishery was dominated by species belonging to families Echinorhinidae, Centrophoridae, and
Rhinochimaeridae. Continued monitoring and collection of biological and taxonomical data from additional sources is
recommended for better management measures for sustainable utilization of deep-sea chondrichthyans from Indian waters.

Keywords: deep-sea Chondrichthyans; species composition; diversity; Indian EEZ; conservation

Alifa Bintha Haque
University of Oxford

Mitigating elasmobranch product trade: A market analysis for made-to-measure interventions
1.    Alifa Bintha Haque; Nature-based Solutions Initiative, Department of Zoology, University of Oxford, Zoology Research and
      Administration Building, 11a Mansfield Rd, Oxford OX1 3SZ, UK
2.    Rodrigo Oyanedel ; The Interdisciplinary Centre for Conservation Science, Department of Zoology, University of Oxford,
      Oxford, UK.
3.    Rachel D. Cavanagh; British Antarctic Survey, High Cross, Madingley Road, Cambridge, UK, CB3 0ET

The global trade in elasmobranch products contributes to dramatic declines in elasmobranch populations. Recognising realistic
and context-appropriate solutions to mitigate trade requires a thorough grasp of elasmobranch marketplaces that are
notoriously inadequate where products originate, predominantly in the global south. We assessed the market components of
the elasmobranch fin trade in the Bay of Bengal, Bangladesh, using a framework designed for wildlife markets utilising mixed-
method approaches. We found that markets are supply-driven and complex. Fins are supplied from protected and unprotected
species, targeted and by-caught, and traded with other fishery and elasmobranch products. We highlighted challenges due to
no reporting, limited monitoring and the difficulties in segregating species/products of different legal statuses. An unjust power
structure between low-access harvesters and high-access actors with finances, market information and social ties was evident.
The discriminative power exercising influenced unsustainable trade by high-access actors attaining excessive and unequal
benefits from the market and often impacting independent decision-making of low-access actors (e.g., adopting conservation
measures). We found ambiguous clarity about legality, growing distrust between fishers and fishery managers/policy-makers,
and confusion regarding the infrequent seizures resulting from limited awareness, acceptance and no collaborative approach
preceding law-making and enacting. These led to increased conflicts, corruption, non-compliance and unwillingness to report
catch. Actors’ behaviours are driven by diverse motivations, requiring a sophisticated regulation for reducing catch and trade,
with local and global benefits. We provided targeted management recommendations to mitigate unsustainable trade, e.g.,
sustainable catch quotas, better financial arrangements, improved access to incentives for low-access actors, and other market
solutions.

Keywords: Bay of Bengal; market-based solutions; wildlife market; wildlife trade; shark/ray

Andhika Prima Prasetyo
Centre Fisheries Research, Ministry for Marine Affairs and Fisheries, Indonesia

Shark-dust: high-throughput DNA sequencing of processing residues unveils rife trade in endangered
sharks and rays
1.    Andhika, Prasetyo; School of Science, Engineering and Environment, University of Salford, Salford, UK / Centre Fisheries
      Research, Ministry for Marine Affairs and Fisheries, Indonesia
2.    Joanna, Murray; Centre for Environment, Fisheries and Aquaculture Science, Lowestoft, UK
3.    Firdaus, Agung; Directorate for Conservation and Marine Biodiversity, Ministry for Marine Affairs and Fisheries, Indonesia
4.    Efin, Muttaqin; Wildlife Conservation Society Indonesia Program, Indonesia
5.    Naiara, Sales; Department of Biological Sciences, University of Chester, UK
6.    Allan, McDevitt; School of Science, Engineering and Environment, University of Salford, Salford, UK
7.    Stefano, Mariani; School of Biological and Environmental Sciences, Liverpool John Moores University, Liverpool, UK

Trade restrictions have been established to counteract the rapid global decline of sharks and rays. This has resulted from high
fishing pressure, by-catch and market demand for certain products (e.g. fins). Tackling the illegal trade of endangered species
poses enormous challenges for authorities, including taxonomic ambiguity, product variety, logistical issues for inspections and
trade flow complexity. Traditional genetic techniques have been developed, but these techniques are either time-consuming or
species-specific, and generally fail to provide a comprehensive picture of the biological complexity underlying trade patterns.
Here, we explore a powerful, alternative option, based on the high-throughput sequencing of trace DNA fragments retrieved
from dust and scraps left behind by trade activities. We collected 28 ‘shark-dust’ samples from seven processing plants across
Java, Indonesia, and generated shark and ray sequences through DNA metabarcoding. We also collected individual tissue
samples (175) in the same processing plants and identified the species by high-throughput barcoding (HTB) for comparison.
Over 74% of reads belonged to CITES-listed species i.e. hammerhead sharks (Sphyrna spp.), silky shark (Carcharhinus
falciformis), bigeye thresher shark (Alopias superciliosus) and the recently CITES-listed giant guitarfish (Glaucostegus typus). A
total of 75 shark and ray genera (representing half of the chondrichthyan orders) were identified, with 20 more taxa detected
using the dust samples than those identified using tissue samples. Furthermore, dust samples were shown to effectively
capture the species diversity being exploited. We argue that this ‘shark-dust’ approach is likely to become a powerful and cost-
effective monitoring tool wherever elasmobranchs are traded.

Keywords: trade monitoring; CITES; DNA metabarcoding; sharks and rays; Indonesia

Pierluigi Carbonara

Mitigation trials in South Adriatic pelagic longline: first results from the MedBycatch project
1.    Pierluigi, Carbonara: COISPA Tecnologia & Ricerca
2.    Giulia, Prato: WWF Italy
3.    Walter, Zupa: COISPA Tecnologia & Ricerca
4.    Simone, Niedermüller: WWF Mediterranean
5.    Andrea, Bellodi: Cagliari University
6.    Maria Cristina, Follesa: Cagliari University

In the Mediterranean surface longlines (SL) the shark and ray bycatch represents around 10-15% in biomass of
the total catch and the highest bycatch was found in the Alboran and Adriatic Sea. The blueshark (Prionace
glauca), considered by IUCN as Critically Endangered in the Mediterranean, represent over 70% of the
chondricthyan catch. In this context in South Adriatic pelagic longline fishery were tested two type of bycatch
mitigation trials: circular hook and chancing fishing strategy.
About the circular hook in a SL were used alternatively two type of hooks: circular (C-type), normal type (J-type).
In 5 fishing days an experimental SL was used and the CPUE, bycatch-rate and %Bycatch was calculated for the
C-hook and J-hook. The results showed that there were no significant differences in terms of CPUE, bycatch-rate
and %bycatch between the two types of hooks. While the bluesharks caught by C-type hook showed a
significantly better condition than the J-type.
In the fishing strategy used by fisherman the SL fishing mostly during the night. During two consecutive fishing
trips (FT) with at least 3 fishing days each, in one FT was used the standard fishing strategy (strategy 1: SL
fishing mostly during the night) and in the other the reverse fishing strategy (strategy2: SL fishing mostly during
the day) was used. The CPUE, bycatch-rate and %bycatch for the two fishing strategy showed that in the
strategy 2 any blue shark was caught, while for the target species a significant reduction about the 30% was
observed.

Keywords: bycatch; Prionace glauca; circular hook; fishing strategy

Marta Cambra
Escuela de Biología y Centro de Investigación en Ciencias del Mar y Limnología de la Universidad de Costa Rica

Ecological drivers shaping elasmobranch assemblages at Cocos Island, a near-pristine ecosystem
1.    Marta Cambra; Escuela de Biología y Centro de Investigación en Ciencias del Mar y Limnología de la Universidad de
      Costa Rica
2.    Isaac Chinchilla; Área de Conservación Marina Cocos del Sistema Nacional de Areas de Conservación de Costa Rica
3.    Geiner Golfín; Área de Conservación Marina Cocos del Sistema Nacional de Areas de Conservación de Costa Rica
4.    Mario Espinoza; Escuela de Biología y Centro de Investigación en Ciencias del Mar y Limnología de la Universidad de
      Costa Rica

Cocos Island is known to have one of the largest biomass levels of predatory fish in the tropics, yet our knowledge of the
drivers affecting elasmobranch abundance and distribution patterns is limited. This study examined the effect of spatial,
seasonal, and environmental drivers on elasmobranch assemblages at Cocos Island using baited remote underwater video
stations (BRUVS) between 2016 and 2020. Elasmobranchs were detected in 95% of the BRUVS, with stations having on
average 2.7 ± 1.6 species and 6.3 ± 6.5 ind/hour largely overcoming the mean relative abundance reported by BRUVS in other
protected reef systems. A total of 13 species comprised the elasmobranch assemblage of Cocos Island, which was clearly
divided between pelagic and benthic environments. Depth and habitat were identified as the major factors structuring
elasmobranch assemblages in benthic environments, although substrate cover, distance to the coast and season were also
significant predictors. Significant indicator species with stronger species-habitat associations were the silky shark Carcharhinus
falciformis dominating pelagic habitats, the white tip reef shark Triaenodon obesus and the black tip reef shark Carcharhinus
melanopterus dominating warm shallow areas with coral or rocky reefs, the scalloped hammerhead shark Sphyrna lewini
associated with pinnacles at greater distances from the coast and the silvertip shark Carcharhinus albimarginatus with
preference for deeper sandy areas in bays and inshore sites. This study represents the first attempt at describing ecological
patterns of elasmobranch assemblages removing human presence from the water and using a systematic research survey in
benthic and pelagic environments of Cocos Island.

Keywords: Costa Rica; baited remote underwater video stations; multivariate regression trees; distance based redundancy
analysis; generalized additive mixed models

Larissa Modica
Spanish Institute of Oceanography (IEO)

Elasmobranch diversity in the Aviles canyon system (NE Atlantic) and habitat modelling
1.    Larissa Modica: Spanish Institute of Oceanography (IEO C.O Santander)
2.    Cristina Rodríguez-Cabello: Spanish Institute of Oceanography (IEO C.O Santander)
3.    Francisco Sánchez; Spanish Institute of Oceanography (IEO C.O Santander)

Information on the distribution and habitat preferences of ecologically important species is essential for their management and
protection. The Avilés Canyon System (ACS) located in NE Atlantic Waters (Cantabrian Sea) was declared as Site of
Community Importance (SIC) within the Nature 2000 network based on Habitat Directive 92/43/CEE. Therefore several surveys
have been conducted to identify the presence of vulnerable habitats and species and to achieve a better understanding of the
ecosystem function.
Biomass data were gathered from two otter trawls datasets (2010 and 2011) within two different projects: LIFE+INDEMARES
and ERDEM. Data were over dispersed due to the species typical spatial preferences, (a lot of zeros together with some very
high values samples), thus zero-inflated GAM (Generalized Additive Models) with a negative binomial distribution were applied
to predict species biomasses in the study area for the four most abundant chondricthyans: Scyliorhinus canicula, Raja clavata,
Galeus melastomus, Chimaera monstrosa. A broad set of environmental variables were available to link with biomasses, but
after a linear regression with a Pearson correlation test to avoid overestimation of explained variance just six environmental
layers were used: depth, BPI broad, Q50 phi, S0, organic matter content (OM) of the sediments and the categorical variable
bottom type.
The variable that better explains their distribution is depth. The variances explained varied between 72.9% for G. melastomus
and 82.6% for S. canicula. All the species studied showed a clear bathymetric distributional pattern probably linked more to
feeding behavior and prey availability than to specific environmental conditions.


Keywords: elasmobranch diversity; Aviles Canyon System; habitat; distribution models; NE Atlantic

Johann MOURIER
Université de Corse Pasquale Paoli, UMS 3514 Plateforme Marine Stella Mare

Dive into the social life of elasmobranchs: Investigating social and collective behaviour in sharks and
rays

Social and collective behaviours as well as group formation are found across the animal kingdom including sharks and rays.
Animal aggregations may result from a common requirement for shared resources or favourable environmental conditions and
are not necessarily driven by social attraction between individuals. In contrast, social groups rely on active social preferences
between individuals. Although they have been considered mostly solitary organisms, improvements in analytical tools and the
technology used to study behaviours in the wild has led to the confirmation that many elasmobranch species were more social
than previously thought and to a better understanding of the form, structure, function and ecological significance of associations
and grouping in sharks and rays. Here, I present some case studies I have been conducting or involved in, illustrating research
on aggregations, social and collective behaviour in wild elasmobranchs. This includes species with gregarious or solitary life
styles. This overview aims at presenting the latest advancements in analytical techniques (especially social network analysis)
and field technology (e.g. photo-identification, acoustic telemetry, biologging, video recording or drone) used to study
elasmobranch aggregating patterns and social systems, and discussing the benefits and costs of grouping and socializing in
these taxa.

Keywords: social behaviour; collective behaviour; social networks; biologging; aggregations

Drivers of fine-scale vertical and horizontal movement of whale sharks at Ningaloo Reef, Western
Australia
1.  Ben, D'Antonio; Ocean Graduate School and the UWA Oceans Institute, University of Western Australia, Perth, WA,
    Australia & Institution & Australian Institute of Marine Science, Indian Ocean Marine Research Centre, Perth, WA,
    Australia
2. Luciana C Ferreira; Australian Institute of Marine Science, Indian Ocean Marine Research Centre, Perth, WA, Australia
3. Mark Meekan; Australian Institute of Marine Science, Indian Ocean Marine Research Centre, Perth, WA, Australia
4. Lilian Lieber; Queen's University Marine Laboratory, Portaferry, Northern Ireland
5. Paul G. Thomson; Ocean Graduate School and the UWA Oceans Institute, University of Western Australia, Perth, WA,
    Australia
6. Chari Pattiaratchi; Ocean Graduate School and the UWA Oceans Institute, University of Western Australia, Perth, WA,
    Australia
7. Patti Virtue; Institute for Marine and Antarctic Studies, University of Tasmania, Hobart, TAS, Australia
8. Andrew Brierley; Gatty Marine Laboratory, School of Biology, University of St Andrews, Fife KY16 8LB, Scotland, UK
9. Chloe M Power; Institute for Marine and Antarctic Studies, University of Tasmania, Hobart, TAS, Australia
10. Ana M. M. Sequeira; School of Biology and ANU Collage of Science, The Australian National University, Canberra,
    Australia
11. Michele Thums; Australian Institute of Marine Science, Indian Ocean Marine Research Centre, Perth, WA, Australia

Whale sharks (Rhincodon typus) are thought to seasonally aggregate in coastal waters to take advantage of foraging
opportunities provided by enhanced localised productivity. To test this hypothesis, we examined the links between the
movements of whale sharks and prey availability at Ningaloo Reef, Western Australia. In 2018, we sampled the water column
using fisheries echosounder (Simrad EK60) transects and dedicated target trawls via ship-based surveys to describe the
distribution, abundance and density of zooplankton prey in the vicinity of concurrently tracked whale sharks (using Fastloc-GPS
tags). Using generalised additive mixed models, we examined whale shark kernel utilisation density (horizontal movement) and
time-at-depth (calculated per 5 m depth bins) and (response variables) in relation to various prey measurements (n per m^3).
There was a positive relationship between whale shark horizontal movement and prey density. Sharks tended to move along
the 40-60 m depth contours, which were characterised by pronounced bathymetric features accumulating higher densities of
prey near the seabed. However, despite shark horizontal movements centred within areas of high prey density, there was no
relationship between shark time-at-depth and prey density. Sharks displayed consistent patterns of repeated descents and
ascents through the water column with individuals spending ~49% of time within the upper 5 m of the water column,
irrespective of vertical prey distribution. We suggest these vertical movements may reflect the difficulty of feeding on prey in the
lower water column compared to filter feeding near the sea surface.

Keywords: foraging ecology; movement ecology; hydroacoustics; whale sharks; marine megafauna

Habitat use and movement patterns of tiger sharks (Galeocerdo cuvier) in eastern Australian waters
1.    Rebecca Lipscombe; Southern Cross University
2.    Julia Spaet; University of Cambridge
3.    Anna Scott; Southern Cross University
4.    Chi Hin Lam; University of Massachusetts
5.    Craig Brand; New South Wales Department of Primary Industries
6.    Paul Butcher; New South Wales Department of Primary Industries/Southern Cross University

Understanding the movement of highly mobile marine predators is vital for effective conservation and management. Despite
being targeted in shark control programs, our knowledge of tiger shark (Galeocerdo cuvier) movement and habitat use off
eastern Australia is limited. To investigate the habitat use and movements in this region, 16 sharks (157–384 cm total length)
were tagged with MiniPAT pop-up satellite archival tags in 2018 and 2019. Eleven of these individuals were also fitted with
satellite-linked radio transmitting tags. After release, most sharks moved off the continental shelf and travelled north,
associating with seamounts as they moved toward Queensland. While at liberty, they transited through temperate, sub-tropical
and tropical waters, spending the majority of time in the upper 50 m of the water column and at temperatures between 22–25
˚C. Horizontal movement was focused in offshore waters beyond the continental slope. Increased movement over shelf waters
occurred mainly during the austral spring and summer when the East Australian Current encroaches on the continental shelf.
Broad latitudinal movement along the east coast of Australia was evident and highlights the connectivity between tropical and
warm temperate regions.

Keywords: archival tag; Carcharhinid; satellite tag; shark management; geolocation

Wade VanderWright
Simon Fraser University

Metabolic morphology and ecological lifestyle in sharks
1.    Wade, VanderWright; Simon Fraser University
2.    Jennifer, Bigman; NOAA
3.    Anthony, Iliou; Simon Fraser University
4.    Nick, Dulvy; Simon Fraser University

Metabolism underpins life histories, population dynamics, and extinction risk. Morphological traits that are linked to an
organism's metabolism may provide a first approximation to the ecological needs to support growth, survival, and reproduction.
Here we examine the relationship between respiratory physiology (i.e. gill slit height in proportion to total length) and activity
level (caudal fin aspect ratio) in nearly all extant shark species (n=456). Gill slit heights and caudal fin aspect ratios were
collected and measured from the field guide Sharks of the World (Ebert 2013). We used a Bayesian-phylogenetic inference
model that accounts for each species’ maximum size, primary habitat type, and evolutionary history. We found a strong positive
relationship between gill slit height and activity level. We also show that primary habitat had a strong effect on this relationship
with pelagic sharks exhibiting the highest gill slit heights and activity levels while deep water sharks showed the opposite trend.
These findings suggest that proxies for metabolic demand may be available via external and easy to measure morphological
traits and natural history. Insight into the metabolic demand of these animals may allow us to estimate vital life history traits that
could inform ecological risk assessments.

Keywords: metabolic theory of ecology; gill-oxygen limitation theory; macroecology; shrinking fishes

Morphometry and histology to assess the maturity stage of three endangered devil ray species
(Elasmobranchii: Mobulidae) from the Gulf of California
1.    Katherin, Soto-López; Escuela de Sistemas Biológicos e Innovación Tecnológica, Universidad Autónoma Benito Juárez
      de Oaxaca (ESBIT-UABJO), Oaxaca de Juárez, México; Instituto Politécnico Nacional, Centro Interdisciplinario de
      Ciencias Marinas, La Paz, Baja California Sur, México.
2.    Rosa Isabel, Ochoa-Báez; Instituto Politécnico Nacional, Centro Interdisciplinario de Ciencias Marinas, La Paz, Baja
      California Sur, México.
3.    Felipe, Galván-Magaña; Instituto Politécnico Nacional, Centro Interdisciplinario de Ciencias Marinas, La Paz, Baja

4.    Javier Noé, Serrano-López; Instituto Politécnico Nacional, Centro Interdisciplinario de Ciencias Marinas, La Paz, Baja

Devil rays of the genus Mobula are subject to fishing exploitation worldwide, the population's reductions are evidenced by a
decline in the number of catches of these rays, for this reason, they are considered vulnerable to overexploitation. Limited
biological knowledge on these species has forced the use of precautionary conservation measures in some countries where
intensive fishing occurs. There is few information about the reproductive biology of these species. This study aimed to describe
the reproductive activity of three endangered species of the genus Mobula (Mobula munkiana, Mobula thurstoni, and Mobula
mobular) in the Gulf of California. Reproductive organs were collected during 7 years of commercial fishing (2001–2007), and

estimated based on anatomical and histological characteristics of the specimens caught. According to the results, there is an
urgent need to apply strict conservation measures, such as fishery closures during the breeding season (May to September) or
the establishment of catch sizes after specimens have reached sexual maturity, mainly in the countries where these rays are
caught.

                                                                     Sharks International 2022                           Page 15
Mark Royer
Hawai'i Institute of Marine Biology

The Right Stuff: Scalloped hammerhead sharks are metabolically poised for intensive and potentially
anoxic deep dives
1.    Mark, Royer; Hawaiʻi Institute of Marine Biology
2.    Danielle, Garcia; University of Hawaiʻi at Mānoa
3.    Kevin, Weng; Virginia Institute of Marine Science
4.    Carl, Meyer; Hawaiʻi Institute of Marine Biology
5.    Kim, Holland; Hawaiʻi Institute of Marine Biology
6.    Jeff, Drazen; University of Hawaiʻi at Mānoa

Scalloped hammerhead sharks (Sphryna lewini) routinely perform rapid deep dives to forage on meso and bathypelagic prey.
These deep dives consist of intensive burst swimming followed by resting periods in the surface mixed layer. Swimming muscle
temperature profiles suggest S. lewini suppress their gill function as a means to reduce convective heat loss. Such intensive
swimming behavior coupled with supposedly reduced respiration raises questions concerning the aerobic and anaerobic
metabolic capacity of their white swimming muscle. Measuring the activity rates of key enzymes used in aerobic and anaerobic
metabolism provides an indirect indicator of the metabolic potential of a tissue. Here we measured the maximal activities of
aerobic enzymes citrate synthase (CS), malate dehydrogenase (MDH) and anaerobic enzymes pyruvate kinase (PK) and
lactate dehydrogenase (LDH) from white swimming muscle of S. lewini and were compared to those measured from other
coastal, deep-dwelling, and highly active endothermic shark species. Sphyrna lewini had significantly higher activity levels of
LDH, an anaerobic enzyme, and MDH, an aerobic enzyme. Results indicate the white muscle tissue of S. lewini is highly
anaerobic dependent for burst swimming but has a high potential for lactate buildup. High activity rates of MDH indicate the
potential for lactate to be rapidly reduced under aerobic conditions such as those experienced in the surface mixed layer
between dives. These physiological adaptations likely enable S. lewini to remain highly active while suppressing gill function
during deep dives and thereby exploit a very different ecological niche from sympatric shark species.

Keywords: scalloped hammerhead; deep diving; enzyme; lactate; metabolic poise

Aaron Judah
Department of Biology, Dalhousie University, Halifax, Nova Scotia, Canada

Functional integrity and vulnerability of shark and ray communities across the world’s coral reefs
1.    Aaron, Judah; Department of Biology, Dalhousie University, Halifax, Nova Scotia, Canada
2.    Matthew, McLean; Department of Biology, Dalhousie University, Halifax, Nova Scotia, Canada
3.    Christopher, Mull; Department of Biology, Dalhousie University, Halifax, Nova Scotia, Canada
4.    Ana, Barbosa Martins; Department of Biology, Dalhousie University, Halifax, Nova Scotia, Canada
5.    M. Aaron, MacNeil; Department of Biology, Dalhousie University, Halifax, Nova Scotia, Canada; Ocean Frontier Institute,
      Dalhousie University, Halifax, Nova Scotia, Canada

Sharks and rays support many ecological roles in coastal marine ecosystems, however widespread overfishing and habitat loss
have resulted in population declines. Despite the intrinsic vulnerability of shark and ray populations due to their life-history
characteristics, such as slow growth, low fecundity and late reproductive maturity, shark and ray functional community structure
from a trait-based approach has rarely been quantified. Here, we provide a global assessment of shark and ray functional
community structure among 230 tropical coral reefs in 42 nations across three ocean basins. We find that socio-economic
conditions mediate shark and ray functional diversity, functional originality (the uniqueness of the community based on their
traits) and functional dispersion (the overall breadth of trait combinations). Higher human gravity is strongly associated with
degradation of all functional biodiversity metrics, while the trait structure of highly populated areas favour benthic and
invertivorous sharks and rays. Large-bodied, pelagic and benthopelagic, deep diving, piscivorous and higher order carnivorous
species are less abundant under increased human impacts. Biogeographically, Atlantic assemblages are more functionally
original and diverse than Pacific assemblages, which are more represented by traits indicative of reef sharks rather than rays,
such as fusiform body shape, ram ventilation and pelagic or benthopelagic lifestyles. Closed marine protected areas support
substantially higher functional biodiversity than restricted and openly fished areas. Our findings illustrate the large-scale
functional degradation of shark communities on coral reefs but identify spatial management tools as powerful avenues for
protecting shark and ray functional integrity.

Keywords: coral reefs; functional traits; functional diversity; bruvs; conservation

Boryshpolets Serhii
Faculty of Fisheries and Protection of Waters, University of South Bohemia in České Budějovice, South
Bohemian Research Center of Aquaculture and Biodiversity of Hydrocenoses, Zátiší 728/II, Vodňany 389 25,
Czech Republic.

The role of viscosity in the sperm motility of elasmobranch species.
1.    Serhii Boryshpolets; Faculty of Fisheries and Protection of Waters, University of South Bohemia in České Budějovice.
2.    Borys Dzyuba; Faculty of Fisheries and Protection of Waters, University of South Bohemia in České Budějovice
3.    Pablo García-Salinas; Grupo de Acuicultura y Biodiversidad, Instituto de Ciencia y Tecnología Animal, Universitat
      Politècnica de València
4.    Victor Gallego; Grupo de Acuicultura y Biodiversidad, Instituto de Ciencia y Tecnología Animal, Universitat Politècnica de
      València
5.    Anatolii Sotnikov; Faculty of Fisheries and Protection of Waters, University of South Bohemia in České Budějovice
6.    Juan F. Asturiano; Grupo de Acuicultura y Biodiversidad, Instituto de Ciencia y Tecnología Animal, Universitat Politècnica
      de València

Elasmobranchii belongs to an old evolutionary class of vertebrates that diverged 450 mya. These species present a wide
diversity of reproductive strategies while preserving the ancient mode of internal fertilization. Moreover, many of them are
considered endangered and may be extinct soon. Understanding the principles of sperm motility and sperm physiology is
essential to developing assisted reproductive techniques for this species group. Additionally, it is also important from
evolutionary and general physiological perspectives as a part of reproduction studies.
Elasmobranchii species possess relatively big spermatozoa (compared to bony fishes), with an elongated spiral shape head
and tail similar to one currently existing (but later diverged) in birds, reptiles, and amphibians, which can be considered an
evolutionary ancient. These particular structures may be associated with the necessity to penetrate viscous ovarian fluid or the
jelly layer of eggs, suggesting environmental viscosity as the driving force which shapes big-sized spermatozoa head to the
spiral shape during evolution.
In the present study, we observed spermatozoa motion by regular and high-speed video microscopy to visualize sperm and
flagellar motion in three different Elasmobranchii species: the freshwater ray Potamotrygon motoro, the marine skate Raja
asterias and the shark Scyliorhinus canicula. We investigated the effect of surrounding media viscosity on spermatozoa motility
parameters and their ability to propagate, release from spermatozeugmata, and perform directional changes. Our results
suggest that the surrounding media's viscosity regulates spermatozoa motility and may affect its performance during
fertilization in the Elasmobranchii species.

Keywords: cartilaginous fishes; fish reproduction; spermatozeugmata
