Atlantine Boggio-Pasqua
Aix-Marseille University

Whitespotted eagle ray (Aetobatus narinari) age and growth in wild (in situ) versus aquarium-housed (ex
situ) individuals: implications for conservation and management
1. Atlantine, Boggio-Pasqua; Aix-Marseille University, France.
2. Kim, Bassos-Hull; Sharks and Rays Conservation Research Program, Mote Marine Laboratory, Sarasota, FL, USA.
3. William H., Aeberhard; Swiss Data Science Center, ETH Zürich, Switzerland.
4. Lisa A., Hoopes; Research and Conservation Department, Georgia Aquarium, Atlanta, GA, USA.
5. Denise A., Swider; Discovery Cove, Orlando, FL, USA.
6. Krystan A., Wilkinson; Sharks and Rays Conservation Research Program, Mote Marine Laboratory, Sarasota, FL, USA.
7. Manuel, Dureuil; Shark and Ray Conservation Centre, Dartmouth, Canada.

The whitespotted eagle ray (Aetobatus narinari), a large pelagic migratory ray with an endangered status on the IUCN red list,
displays slow growth and late maturity life history characteristics based on available literature. Historically, its age and growth
parameters have been reported through vertebral aging methods. However, the periodicity of vertebrate band pair formation,
which is used for aging, has not been validated for this species, making aging accuracy and thus the resulting growth
parameters uncertain. In this study, we used both a frequentist and Bayesian method of estimating sex-specific von Bertalanffy
growth parameters (DW_∞, k) in wild recaptured versus aquarium-housed rays. Additionally, we estimated growth from
repeated measurement data collected from aquarium-housed rays, to obtain growth parameters while allowing for individual
variability. Between 2009 and 2020, 590 whitespotted eagle rays were caught, measured, tagged and released along the
southwest coast of Florida. Thirty-four were recaptured between 5–1413 days at liberty. Nineteen additional rays were
collected, transported and maintained at Georgia Aquarium, Atlanta, with regular size and weight monitoring. Wild whitespotted
eagle rays were found to grow faster and mature earlier than previously thought, with Bayesian estimates of k = 0.28 year-1 in
females, and k = 0.30 year-1 in males. Aquarium-housed individuals seemed to grow slower and reach smaller sizes, although
data provided by the aquariums showed variable growth patterns depending on the facility. Life history parameters and growth
trajectories generated from this study offer valuable information to aid with future conservation management strategies of this
endangered species.

Keywords: eagle ray; life history; age and growth analysis; maturity; longevity

Threats Beyond Overfishing 1


Florencia Cerutti
Charles Darwin Foundation

Vulnerability of chondrichthyan fishes of the Eastern Tropical Pacific to climate change
Cerutti-Pereyra, F.Denkerd, L., Espinoza, M., Fallo-Mejía, P., Finucci, B., Galván-Magaña, F., Hacohen, A., Hearn, A., Hoyos-
Padilla, M., Ketchum, J., Moya, A., Navia, A., Pazmiño, D., Ramírez-Macías, D., Rummer, J., Salinas-de-León, P., Sosa-
Nishizaki, O., Stock, C., Chin, A.

Climate change is an environmental emergency threatening species and ecosystems globally and leading to a critical need to
understand how marine species will be affected. Vulnerability assessments provide a framework to assess climate impacts
combining species’ exposure to a stressor, their sensitivity, and their capacity to adapt. We used an Integrated Risk
Assessment to evaluate the vulnerability of 132 chondrichthyan species in the Eastern Tropical Pacific (ETP) to climate change
and identified eight climate change factors most relevant for the region. The overall vulnerability for 23% of ETP
chondrichthyans is High and 76% are Moderately vulnerable to climate change. Most of the Highly vulnerable species are
batoids (77%), and most (90%) are coastal species or linked to coastal ecosystems through nurseries. Ocean warming, in
combination with hypoxia and acidification, will have the most widespread effect on ETP chondrichthyans, but coastal species
and those with reliance on coastal habitats will also contend with changes sea level rise and salinity from freshwater inputs.
Vulnerability increases with synergies with other anthropogenic factors already occurring in the region, thus mitigating the
impacts of climate change on ETP chondrichthyans include addressing issues with overfishing and habitat destruction too.
Specific actions may be required for species at higher risk including protecting coastal bays and estuarine habitats, addressing
fisheries sustainability, and reducing mortality. This assessment also identified knowledge gaps concerning ETP
chondrichthyans and highlights the need of further understand the impacts of climate change on ETP habitats and processes.

Keywords: risk assessment; climate change; sharks; batoids; chimeras

Franco Cristiani
Universidad Nacional del Comahue, Bariloche, Rio Negro, Argentina; CESIMAR, CENPAT-CONICET, Puerto
Madryn, Chubut, Argentina

Immune and endocrine reproductive parameters of the chimaera, Callorhinchus callorhynchus,
inhabiting a human-impacted area in Patagonia, Argentina. Preliminary results.
1.    Franco, Cristiani; Universidad Nacional del Comahue, Bariloche, Rio Negro, Argentina; CESIMAR, CENPAT-CONICET,
      Puerto Madryn, Chubut, Argentina.
2.    Cynthia A., Awruch; CESIMAR, CENPAT-CONICET, Puerto Madryn, Chubut, Argentina; School of Natural Sciences,
      University of Tasmania, Tasmania, Australia.
3.    Gabriela M., Palacios; CESIMAR, CENPAT-CONICET, Puerto Madryn, Chubut, Argentina.

In an increasingly stressed marine environment due to anthropic activities, the assessment of wildlife health becomes
imperative. Marine organisms are exposed to a complex mix of pollutants which can compromise their wellbeing. Due to the
life-history characteristics, use of habitats and distribution, Chondrichthyans are prone to bioaccumulate and biomagnify
significant amounts of pollutants. Knowledge of immune and reproductive impairments on wild individuals can serve as an early
alert of potential conservation threats. In this study we present preliminary results on immune and reproductive parameters of
the elephant fish, Callorhinchus callorhynchus, inhabiting an anthropic impacted area. Elephant fish were captured by rod and
reel from shore between October 2021 and April 2022, in the Golfo Nuevo, Chubut, Argentina. Hematological parameters and
reproductive hormonal profiles are presented, constituting the first report for this species. Erythrocytes, thrombocytes and five
types of leucocytes (lymphocytes, monocytes, heterophils, eosinophils and neutrophils) were characterized by light microscopy.
Progesterone, estradiol and testosterone were measured in juveniles and adults of both sexes. This study provides baseline
physiological information for the species, contributing to an ongoing research on the responses of the immune and reproductive
systems within an ecotoxicological context.

Keywords: physiology; ecotoxicology; chimaera; immunology; reproduction

Maria Auxiliadora Sabando Plaza

Metal heads: Physiological and neurological effects of methylmercury on a small coastal elasmobranch,
Mustelus canis
1.    Maria Auxiliadora Sabando Plaza; University of Delaware
2.    Dr. Mi-Ling Li; University of Delaware
3.    Dr. Jon Cohen; University of Delaware
4.    Dr. Aaron Carlisle; University of Delaware

As mid to upper trophic level predators, elasmobranchs are well known to have elevated levels of mercury and its more toxic,
organic form methylmercury (MeHg). The lethality of methyl mercury across different taxa has been well researched, and its
sub lethal effects have only been investigated in depth at the scope of human health with no focus on fish that have the highest
mercury burden. However, the sub lethal effects of exposure to MeHg, which are known to impact animal behavior, cognitive
ability, and physiology, remains largely undescribed. In this study, we establish baseline information on how the physiology of a
small, coastal, demersal, elasmobranch species (Mustelis canis, smooth dogfish) is impacted by prolonged, sub lethal
exposure of this widespread and potent contaminant. We assess sublethal effects of different levels of exposure using different
physiological parameters including aerobic scope measured via oxygen consumption, growth rate, body condition, and food
consumption. As a secondary objective we will use a novel approach where we assess cognitive impacts by measuring
learning ability and memory through operant conditioning. Behaviors, such as foraging, prey capture, swimming behavior,
memory, and learning are critical for many species of elasmobranchs yet are likely impacted by exposure to MeHg. Hence,
assessing effects on physiology and behavior after sub-lethal exposure can give us an idea of how important physiological and
cognitive processes may respond to changing ocean contaminant conditions.

Keywords: methlymercury; physiology; cognition; sublethal effects
SI Abstract Ref: 141




Dr Vincent Raoult
School of Environmental and Life Sciences, University of Newcastle, NSW, Australia; School of Natural
Sciences, Macquarie University, North Ryde, NSW Australia

Reliance of young sharks on threatened estuarine habitats for nutrition implies susceptibility to climate
change
1.    Yuri, Niella; School of Natural Sciences, Macquarie University, North Ryde, NSW Australia
2.    Vincent, Raoult; School of Environmental and Life Sciences, University of Newcastle, NSW Australia
3.    Troy, Gaston; School of Environmental and Life Sciences, University of Newcastle, NSW Australia
4.    Kyle, Goodman; School of Natural Sciences, Macquarie University, North Ryde, NSW Australia
5.    Rob, Harcourt; School of Natural Sciences, Macquarie University, North Ryde, NSW Australia
6.    Vic, Peddemors; NSW Department of Primary Industries, Sydney Institute of Marine Science, Mosman, Sydney, New
      South Wales, Australia
7.    Amy, Smoothey; NSW Department of Primary Industries, Sydney Institute of Marine Science, Mosman, Sydney, New
      South Wales, Australia

Climate change and urbanization of coastal areas are impacting estuarine habitats globally. While these regions are important
for the early-life development of many aquatic species, links between habitat use and foraging ecology are not well known.
Thus, it is essential to understand the importance of threatened habitats for animals inhabiting estuaries to promote their
conservation and improve ecosystem management. This study examined the importance of estuarine habitats as feeding
grounds for juvenile bull sharks (Carcharhinus leucas) in the Clarence River, a nursery area where the species is targeted by
commercial fisheries in New South Wales, Australia. Juvenile sharks (n = 54) ranging from one to ∼6.5 years-old, together with
particulate matter and several primary producer species of mangrove and saltmarsh, were sampled for δ13C, δ15N and δ34S
stable isotopes. Bayesian mixing models and a Generalized Additive Mixed Model were used to investigate the contribution of
estuarine habitats to the diet of bull sharks and changes with growth. The importance of resources consumed from within
mangrove habitats for juvenile bull shark resource use was minimal but slightly increased with age, with younger sharks (<2.5
years) relying on prey feeding on sources derived from particulate organic matter, while older juveniles (4–6.5 years) preyed on
species that relied mostly on threatened saltmarsh habitats. Our findings highlight the important linkages between habitats of
conservation concern and species redistribution in response to climate change, particularly as the animals could be moving
towards new areas lacking suitable estuarine habitats.

Keywords: bull sharks; stable isotopes; trophic ecology; shark nursery; stable isotopes

Biology & Life History 3


Flávia F. Petean
Instituto Tecnológico de Chascomús, Consejo Nacional de Investigaciones Científicas y Técnicas, Universidad
Nacional de San Martín, Av. Intendente Marino Km. 8,2 (B7130IWA), Chascomús, Buenos Aires, Argentina

Natural history of the Brazilian marine stingray Hypanus berthalutzae Petean, Naylor & Lima (2020):
knowledge and gaps
1.    Leonardo O. S. Espinosa; Instituto de Oceanografia, Universidade Federal do Rio Grande, Campus Carreiros, Av. Itália
      Km. 08, 96203-900, Rio Grande, RS, Brasil
2.    Flávia F. Petean; Instituto Tecnológico de Chascomús, Consejo Nacional de Investigaciones Científicas y Técnicas,
      Universidad Nacional de San Martín, Av. Intendente Marino Km. 8,2 (B7130IWA), Chascomús, Buenos Aires, Argentina

The coastal marine stingray species Hypanus berthalutzae is endemic to Brazil and occurs south of the Amazon river mouth to
São Paulo state, besides some oceanic islands and reefs. Until its description, H. berthalutzae specimens were identified as H.
americanus and due to its recent recognition, few studies on the natural history of H. berthalutzae have been published using
its current name. However, many previous studies were done with H. americanus: specimens now recognized as H.
berthalutzae. To identify the existing knowledge on the natural history of this stingray, we surveyed online databases for studies
accomplished with Brazilian specimens using a combination of keywords related to its location and species. A total of 123
searches returned 93 documents with information on H. berthalutzae. These were divided into 15 categories according to their
research area, and the commonest ones were Ecology (33%) and Fisheries (24%). Overall, studies report larger females than
males (TL/DW >75 vs. >51) and in a higher sex ratio (female:male, 21.3:1), with nursery areas in Rio Grande do Norte and
Fernando de Noronha. Besides, H. berthalutzae represents approximately 5% of captured batoids in Southeastern Brazil when
sequencing the mitochondrial maker COI, and occurs in depths varying from 0 to 77.5 m. However, we noticed a lack of studies
concerning Reproduction, Physiology, and Evolution of this stingray, areas that should be the focus of future studies since it is
already a Vulnerable species under the IUCN Red List assessment and is currently suffering from a high artisanal fishing
pressure.

Keywords: Lutz’s stingray; ecology; fisheries; evolution; vulnerable

Sizing up the Nusa Penida MPA manta rays
1.    Janis, Argeswara; Marine Megafauna Foundation
2.    Maulana M, Priadhi; Marine Megafauna Foundation
3.    Aulia, Zeintrinanda; Marine Megafauna Foundation
4.    Elitza, Germanov; Marine Megafauna Foundation; Centre for Sustainable Aquatic Ecosystems, Harry Butler Institute,
      Murdoch University, Perth, Western Australia, Australia ; Environmental and Conservation Sciences, Murdoch University,
      Perth, Western Australia, Australia

Manta rays have some of the lowest productivity rates of elasmobranchs and are threatened with extinction. Knowledge gaps in
birth and growth rates, and habitats used by young persist. A previous study identified critical habitats for Mobula alfredi in the
Nusa Penida Marine Protected Area, Indonesia. However, vital demographic data are outstanding, including manta ray sizes
and the maturity status of 47% of females, which lacked visual maturity indicators in the previous study. Here we measured
manta rays using laser and stereophotogrammetry to classify individuals into age groups. We measured 135 individuals, of
which 65.9% were females and 32.6% were males. More than half (52.8%) lacked visual maturity indicators for females, while
males were primarily adults. The average measurement for females was 4.18m (SE土0.06), and; 3.55m (SE土0.05) for males.
Based on the size of maturity assessed elsewhere, 3.2 m for females and 2.7m for males, 98.6% of females and 100% of
males we measured were deemed mature. Manta rays that were classified as mature based on visual observations averaged
4.27m (SE土0.05) for females and 3.55m (SE土0.06) for males. Nusa Penida’s manta rays are more similar in size to those
reported in Mozambique, and Japan, and larger than those in Raja Ampat, Indonesia, Hawaii, and the Maldives. Going forward
we will measure more juvenile individuals and aim to measure them repeatedly using stereophotogrammetry to allow growth-
rate calculations and assist in testing nursery criteria by identifying Young-of-the-Years.

Keywords: manta rays; stereophotogrammetry; laser-photogrammetry; Indonesia; growth rates

Francisco Emanuel Polanco Vásquez
1; Instituto Politécnico Nacional. Centro Interdisciplinario de Ciencias Marinas; 2; Wildlife Conservation Society.

Reproductive biology of the longtail stingray Hypanus longus (Garman, 1880) caught in the Pacifico of
Guatemala.
1.    Francisco, Polanco-Vásquez; Instituto Politécnico Nacional. Centro Interdisciplinario de Ciencias Marinas; Wildlife
      Conservation Society.
2.    Rosa, Ochoa-Báez; Instituto Politécnico Nacional. Centro Interdisciplinario de Ciencias Marinas.
3.    Felipe, Galván-Magaña; Instituto Politécnico Nacional. Centro Interdisciplinario de Ciencias Marinas.
4.    Katherin, Soto-López; Instituto Politécnico Nacional. Centro Interdisciplinario de Ciencias Marinas.
5.    Jose, Moreira-Ramírez; Wildlife Conservation Society.

The longtail stingray Hypanus longus, is distributed from México to Ecuador. At present, it is the most commercially important
ray in south of Mexico and Central America. The goal is to know the reproductive biology of this ray species in order to gather
biological information for application into fisheries management plans. The morphometric data from 2659 organisms (1248
females, 1411 males) were obtained from January 2019 to February 2021. The females had an average size of 83.32 ± 22.34
cm disc width (DW) (41-178 cm DW); while in males the average size was 78.18 ± 10.46 cm DW (47-109 cm DW). There were
no significant differences between the sizes of females and males (Mann-Whitney p=0.2768). The sexual ratio was 0.88H:1M
(χ2=13.12; p<0.05), males were the most numerous group. In terms of reproductive parameters, 495 organisms (219 females
and 276 males) were analyzed. The L50 was 105.42 cm DW for females and 75.73 cm DW for males. Both values are lower
than the sizes previously reported for this species. Also using histology we found that mature males had fully developed sperm;
whilst mature females developed uterine milk-secreting crypts in the trophonemata. The fecundity of females was 1 to 6
embryos. The sexual ratio of the embryos was 0.76H:1M, (χ2=0.83; p>0.05). This species have an asynchronous reproductive
system. This is the first study about the reproductive biology on this species in Southern Mexico and the Central American
Pacific.

Keywords: reproduction; Guatemala Pacific; rays; artisanal fisheries

Laurent Duchatelet
UCLouvain, Marine Biology Laboratory

From whom does this giant glow come? First approach to the bioluminescence of Dalatias licha
1.    Laurent Duchatelet, UCLouvain, Marine Biology Laboratory
2.    Jérôme Mallefet, UCLouvain, Marine Biology Laboratory

Among luminous elasmobranchs, the kitefin shark, Dalatias licha, is a giant reaching up to 1.8 m. Here, through
pharmacological and histological studies, bioluminescence of the largest luminous vertebrate is unveiled. Light organ
(photophore) histology and bioluminescence control mechanisms are compared with known luminous elasmobranchs
(Etmopterus species and S. aliae). Photophore histology revealed a conserved structure among the Dalatiidae family.
Dalatiidae sharks harbor photophores constituted of a single photocyte (light emitting cell) encapsulated in a pigmented layer,
surmounted by an iris-like structure (ILS) and topped by one or several lens cells. Etmopteridae photophores are more
complex. They are composed of a black round shaped pigmented sheath encompassing group of photogenic cells, a lens
formed by 2 to 3 lens cells, and an ILS formed by chromatophores.
In parallel, a similar hormonal control of the light emission is highlighted. Melatonin triggers light production, while alpha-
melanocyte stimulating and adrenocorticotropic hormones inhibit it. For both families, ILS pigment motion is involved in a
mechanical regulation of the light emission. Recently, an extraocular opsin (encephalopsin) was also demonstrated as playing
a role in the light emission control in Etmopteridae. The encephalopsin presence was recently described during the ontogenic
development of the photophore from the dalatiid S. aliae. Hence leading to the search for D. licha extraocular opsin.
By integrating the new data on the largest luminous vertebrate, D. licha, to previous researches, a comparative analysis will be
conducted to highlight a clear bioluminescent structure and control mechanism conservation throughout evolution in luminous
deep-sea sharks.


Keywords: deep-sea sharks; bioluminescence control; Dalatiidae; Etmopteridae; hormones

Baby blues? The reproductive state and nutritional ecology of female blue sharks, Prionace glauca, in
Irish waters.
1.    Jenny Bortoluzzi; Trinity College Dublin, The University of Dublin
2.    Nicholas Payne; Trinity College Dublin, The University of Dublin
3.    Martin J. Kainz; WasserCluster Lunz and Danube University Krems
4.    James Gelslechter; University of North Florida
5.    Lucy Harding; Trinity College Dublin, The University of Dublin
6.    Alison Boyce; Trinity College Dublin, The University of Dublin
7.    Luke Harman; University College Cork
8.    Matthew Cobain; Trinity College Dublin, The University of Dublin
9.    Haley Dolton; Trinity College Dublin, The University of Dublin
10.   Grace McNicholas; Trinity College Dublin, The University of Dublin and the Marine Institute
11.   David Edwards, West Cork Charters
12.   Maria McGuinness; University College Cork
13.   Thomas K. Doyle; University College Cork
14.   Andrew L. Jackson; Trinity College Dublin, The University of Dublin

Despite being one of the most abundant pelagic and most wide ranging shark in the world, much still needs to be learnt about
blue shark biology and ecology. As the most frequently fished oceanic shark, the population is reported to have declined by up
to 70% in the Atlantic, which raises concerns about the future survival of the species. Life history parameters and knowledge of
a species’ ecology are key for management and conservation, particularly when the protection of larger areas is not feasible. In
Irish waters, the population of blue sharks present during the late spring to early autumn is predominantly female but
information about their reproductive status and feeding ecology during this time is lacking. Here, we integrate the results from
sex steroid hormone analysis (progesterone and 17β-oestradiol) in blood plasma to assess reproductive status. In addition,
using omega-3 polyunsaturated fatty acids (PUFA) in both plasma and muscle tissues, we investigate the effect of nutrition and
diet strategies on the reproductive state of female blue sharks. We will also present data on hydrogen stable isotopes of PUFA
to indicate the ability of blue sharks to perform endogenous conversion of dietary PUFA into important physiological omega-3
PUFA.

Keywords: reproductive state; fatty acids; sex steroid hormones; feeding ecology; nutrition

Felicie Dhellemmes
Bimini Biological Field station

What did we learn from six years of shark personality research?
1.    Jean Sebastien, Finger; Bimini Biological Field station
2.    Joffrey Baeyaert; Algarve University
3.    Raquel Braun; Bimini Biological Field station
4.    Liam Dickson; Queen mary University London
5.    Tristan Guttridge; Bimini Biological Field station
6.    Harry Gray; Cardiff University
7.    Nigel Hussey; Windsor University
8.    Matthew Smukall; Bimini Biological Field station
9.    Jens Krause; Leibniz Institute of freshwater Ecology and Inland Fisheries

In behavioural ecology, interest in the study of animal personality (i.e. consistent individual differences in behaviour) has
increased in the last two decades as it is believed to have important ecological and evolutionary consequences. These
consequences are especially pronounced when personality covaries with other behaviours (i.e. behavioural syndrome) or with
life-history (i.e. pace-of-life syndrome). So far, studies of personality on behavioural, and pace-of-life-syndromes have produced
ambiguous outcomes, and the prominence of studies on captive animals in the literature may be a reason for inconclusive
results.
To address this knowledge gap, we tested personality in wild juvenile lemon shark (Negaprion brevirostris) from 2012 to 2018
to investigate the emergence of behavioural and pace-of-life-syndromes under relevant ecological pressures.
We first explored how to quantify meaningful personality traits in our study species. We then investigated (1) whether a
behavioural syndrome existed between two consistent traits and whether the appearance of the syndrome was context
dependent, (2) whether a growth-mortality trade-off was mediated by personality and (3) whether personality could predict the
foraging habitat of sharks and whether this link was context-dependent.
Overall, our research suggest that ecological conditions play a crucial role in the emergence and the shaping of personality and
trait association. This work offers a possible explanation for the ambiguous results of previous studies and highlights the
importance of increasing the focus on wild study systems in future animal personality research. Along with recent revisions of
the personality research framework, this work may help paving the way for future shark-personality research.




                                                                   Sharks International 2022                          Page 22
Keywords: personaliy; pace-of-life; foraging specialization

Diego Fallas-Madrigal
Biomolecular Laboratory, Center for International Programs and Sustainability Studies, Universidad Veritas, San
José, Costa Rica

Research and conservation of the eagle ray (Aetobatus laticeps)
1.    Alejandra, Castelo-Corona Sebastian; Centro Interdisciplinario de Ciencias Marinas, La Paz Baja California Sur, México
2.    Maria del Socorro, Gonzáles-Ramos, Biomolecular Laboratory, Center for International Programs and Sustainability
      Studies, Universidad Veritas, San José, Costa Rica.

The Pacific white-spotted eagle ray Aetobatus laticeps, has been recently separated from the Atlantic A. narinari
based on both morphological and genetic evidence. This species is distributed from the Golf of California to
Ecuador, where different research efforts have been done. Considering the conservation need and lack of
information in this species, we have studied its population, habitat use, and spot pattern variation along its
distribution. By using different methodologies, we have found 132 individuals in Oaxaca (Mexico), and 72 in the
north of Costa Rica. First recorded sizes in Jalisco (Mexico) ranged from 50 to 130 cm in disc width, and
encounter rates were found to be significantly in spring seasons with SST fluctuation of 25°-27°C. Furthermore, a
first assessment of the spot pattern in northern Costa Rica showed 17 distinctive spots, and an additional
putative eagle ray species from Cocos Island with a spot pattern resembling to its sister species, A. ocellatus.
Now in a regional scale we are continuing studying this species with new aims, such as spot pattern change,
diversity pattern along its distribution, site fidelity, population structures, as well as contributing in global efforts to
study the white spotted eagle rays.


Keywords: spotted eagle ray

Noémie Coulon
Laboratoire de Biologie des Organismes et Ecosystèmes Aquatiques (BOREA) MNHN, CNRS, IRD, SU, UCN,

Northeast Atlantic elasmobranchs on the move: different responses to climate change across functional
traits and phylogeny
1.    Noémie, Coulon; Laboratoire de Biologie des Organismes et Ecosystèmes Aquatiques (BOREA) MNHN, CNRS, IRD, SU,
      UCN, UA, 35800, Dinard, France
2.    Sophie, Elliott; Game & Wildlife Conservation Trust, Fordingbridge, Hampshire, SP6 1EF, England
3.    Nils, Teichert; Noémie, Coulon; Laboratoire de Biologie des Organismes et Ecosystèmes Aquatiques (BOREA) MNHN,
      CNRS, IRD, SU, UCN, UA, 35800, Dinard, France
4.    Thomas, Barreau; Service des stations Marine, CRESCO, Dinard, France
5.    Alexandre, Carpentier; Laboratoire de Biologie des Organismes et Ecosystèmes Aquatiques (BOREA) MNHN, CNRS,
      IRD, SU, UCN, UA, Université de Rennes 1, Rennes, France

Ongoing climate change is responsible for shifting the distribution of many marine species and may lead to changes in
community structure and ecosystem functioning, especially where species perform unique functions such as elasmobranchs.
However, this issue has been rarely studied in these species due to the limited amount of occurrence data and may have led to
an underestimation of the underlying threats. It can be circumvented by identifying functional traits that explain range shifts in
elasmobranchs rather than by studying individual species responses. Therefore, our aim was to detect and quantify distribution
changes in demersal elasmobranchs in response to ocean acidification and increasing sea temperature and assess whether
such changes can be attributed to their functional traits. To undertake this study, we collated scientific bottom trawl surveys
data from 1997-2020 across the European continental shelf and employed a multispecies joint distribution model to model their
habitat and understand potential variations in distribution with time according to sea water temperature and pH. The responses
of trophic level, reproductive guild, preferred depth and latitudinal range traits according to these climate change variables were
equally explored. From this analysis we highlight species whose range has shifted over the past two decades. Community
structure variations are described over time and functional groups that are appear to be more vulnerable to future climate
changes identified. Fishing pressure is also tested as a complementary factor affecting elasmobranch distribution over time

Keywords: climate change; elasmobranchs; functional traits; jSDM; shift

Natascha Wosnick
Universidade Federal do Paraná

The effects of arsenic, lead and mercury muscle assimilation on physiological pathways in the Brazilian
Guitarfish, Pseudobatos horkelii
1.    Natascha, Wosnick; Universidade Federal do Paraná
2.    Natalia ,Guimarães; Universidade Federal do Rio de Janeiro
3.    Renata Daldin, Leite; Universidade Federal do Paraná
4.    Ana Paula Chaves, Silva; Universidade de São Paulo
5.    Eloísa Pinheiro, Giareta; Universidade Federal do Paraná
6.    Amanda Pontes, Lopes; Fundação Oswaldo Cruz
7.    Francielli Casanova, Monteiro; Pontifícia Universidade Católica do Rio de Janeiro
8.    Carlos, Massone; Pontifícia Universidade Católica do Rio de Janeiro
9.    Renato, Carreira; Pontifícia Universidade Católica do Rio de Janeiro
10.   Marcelo, Vianna; Universidade Federal do Rio de Janeiro
11.   Rachel Ann, Hauser-Davis; Fundação Oswaldo Cruz


Metal contamination is one of the emerging threats with the potential to negatively affect marine animals, being of particular
relevance to coastal mesopredators with resident behavior due to the expressive influx of anthropogenic origin across their
home range. Currently, most toxicological investigations with elasmobranchs are focused on toxic metals and human
consumption outcomes. Nevertheless, the effects of contamination on the systemic health of exposed animals have been
systematically neglected. That said, this study aimed to evaluate the potential effects of muscle assimilation of arsenic, lead,
and mercury on the anaerobic metabolism and buffering capacity of the Brazilian Guitarfish through the assessment of serum
lactate and pH. Arsenic concentrations ranged from 0.97 to 79.7, lead from undetectable to 0.07, and mercury from 0.03 to
0.49. Surprisingly, a positive correlation between arsenic and pH was detected, and higher pH was observed in more
contaminated animals. Taken together, the results indicate that arsenic, lead and mercury accumulation in the muscle does not
affect anaerobic metabolism nor the buffering capacity of exposed Pseudobatos horkelii. However, it is not possible to rule out
the deleterious effects on other physiological processes, such as aerobic metabolism. Complementary analyses are needed to
elucidate the unexpected correlation between higher arsenic values and the observed alkalosis as well as the higher pH levels
on guitarfishes presenting higher levels of the metals assessed in this investigation.

Keywords: toxicology; physiology; guitarfish; marine pollution

Iliana Araceli Fonseca Ponce
Proyecto Manta Pacific Mexico; Instituto Nacional de México campus Bahía de Banderas

Physical and environmental drivers of oceanic manta ray (Mobula birostris) sightings at an aggregation
site in Bahía de Banderas, Mexico
1.    Iliana Araceli, Fonseca Ponce; Proyecto Manta Pacific Mexico; Instituto Nacional de México campus Bahía de Banderas.
2.    Aldo Alfonso, Zavala Jiménez; Proyecto Manta Pacific Mexico; University of California, Campus Santa Cruz.
3.    Octavio, Aburto Oropeza; Scripps Institution of Oceanography.
4.    Adrián, Maldonado Gasca; Instituto Nacional de México, Campus Bahía de Banderas.
5.    Felipe, Galvan Magaña; Instituto Politécnico Nacional, Centro Interdisciplinario de Ciencias Marinas (CICIMAR-IPN).
6.    Rogelio, González Armas; Centro Interdisciplinario de Ciencias Marinas (CICIMAR-IPN).
7.    Joshua David, Stewart; Marine Mammal Institute, Department of Fisheries, Wildlife, and Conservation Sciences, Hatfield
      Marine Science Center, Oregon State University; The Manta Trust.

In Banderas Bay, Mexico, an important aggregation site of oceanic manta rays occurs adjacent to a major human population
center (Puerto Vallarta) in a region of high vessel traffic and fishing effort. Manta rays in this population have 10 times the per-
capita incidence of injuries from vessel strikes and fishing gear entanglements compared to an offshore population of mantas at
the protected Revillagigedo Islands, only ~300 miles away. To identify the locations and periods of highest risk of
anthropogenic impacts on this population, we have been surveying coastal habitats in Banderas Bay at weekly intervals in
partnership with local fishermen since 2014, collecting in-situ environmental data and recording sightings of manta rays. We
show that the El Niño Southern Oscillation has an extremely strong influence on the occupancy probability of manta rays in
Banderas Bay, and that there is a clear seasonal cycle of visitation to the region. We also show significant relationships
between occupancy and sea surface temperature, lunar phase and tidal range. We found that the presence of manta rays was
positively related to some zooplankton taxonomic groups such as copepods, cladocerans and fish larvae. We hypothesize that
mantas are primarily feeding on mesopelagic prey in a submarine canyon adjacent to the aggregation area and using the
shallow nearshore zone for thermal recovery. This information on seasonal, physical, and environmental drivers of manta ray
occupancy creates opportunities for dynamic management strategies that may prevent and mitigate anthropogenic impacts on
this vulnerable population.

Keywords: zooplankton; filter-feeder; elasmobranch; GAM; ENSO

The blackmouth catshark, Galeus melastomus, as potential bioindicator for Mediterranean deep-sea
environment contamination
1.    Guia Consales; Department of Physical Sciences, Earth and Environment, University of Siena, Via Mattioli 4, Siena
      53100, Italy; Department of Integrative Marine Ecology, Stazione Zoologica Anton Dohrn - Italian National Institute for
      Marine Biology, Ecology and Biotechnology, Genoa Marine Centre, c/o CNR-IAS, Via De Marini, 6 16149 Genoa, Italy
2.    Massimiliano Bottaro; Department of Integrative Marine Ecology (EMI), Genoa Marine Centre (GMC), Stazione Zoologica
      Anton Dohrn–National Institute of Marine Biology, Ecology and Biotechnology, Villa del Principe, Piazza del Principe 4,
      16126 Genoa, Italy.
3.    Cecilia Mancusi; ARPAT - Regional Agency for Environmental Protection of Tuscany, Via Marrani 114, 57126 Livorno,
      Italy.
4.    Alessandra Neri; Consortium for the Inter-University Center of Marine Biology and Applied Ecology “G.Bacci”, Viale
      Nazario Sauro 4, 57128 Livorno, Italy
5.    Maria Pia Mordente; Department of Physical Sciences, Earth and Environment, University of Siena, Via Mattioli 4, Siena
      53100, Italy
6.    Maria Bisignano; Department of Physical Sciences, Earth and Environment, University of Siena, Via Mattioli 4, Siena
      53100, Italy
7.    Letizia Marsili; Department of Physical Sciences, Earth and Environment, University of Siena, Via Mattioli 4, Siena 53100,
      Italy; Inter-University Center of Cetacean Research (CIRCE), Via Mattioli 4, Siena 53100, Italy

The deep-sea is the largest and most productive habitat of the planet, but scarce information regarding pollution is available.
The Mediterranean is one of the most anthropized maritime areas, where old and new generation contamination pose a threat
to the conservation of local biodiversity. For this reason, it is an excellent natural laboratory for studying the impacts of these
chemical compounds in the deep-sea.
As the chondrichthyans are important consumers below 400 m, we studied the presence of organochlorine contaminants-OCs,
from the Ligurian Sea to the Gulf of Naples, in the blackmouth catshark, Galeus melastomus, a widely distributed deep-water
opportunistic species.
Hexachlorobenzene-HCB, polychlorinated biphenyls-PCBs, and dichlorodiphenyltrichloroethane and its metabolites-DDTs,
were determined in the liver and muscle of 73 specimens.



                                                                    Sharks International 2022                           Page 25
PCBs were the most abundant and the use of an enriched op’ formula of DDT was highlighted. As well as the use of Dicofol
especially in the Gulf of Naples, the most anthropized study site.
Despite the higher levels of all the three OC group in the liver compared to those detected in the muscle, our findings suggest
that this tissue could be used as indicator of chronic exposure to pollutants, but to clearly understand the real impacts of
pollutants in the deep sea further studies in others areas is needed.
In this framework, G. melastomus presents interesting features to serve as potential bioindicator for the Mediterranean deep-
sea due to its feeding habits, remarkable abundance and current conservation status (“Least Concern” for the Mediterranean
IUCN Red List).

Keywords: bioindicator; deep-sea; Galeus melastomus; Mediterranean Sea; organochlorine contaminants (OCs)

Marc Aquino Baleyto

Diving deeper into the underlying white shark behaviors at Guadalupe Island, Mexico
1.  Marc, Aquino-Baleytó; Instituto Politécnico Nacional, Centro Interdisciplinario de Ciencias Marinas, La Paz, Mexico
2.  Vianey, Leos-Barajas; University of Toronto, Toronto, ON, Canada
3.  Timo, Adam; University of St Andrews, St Andrews, UK
4.  Mauricio, Hoyos-Padilla; Pelagios-Kakunjá, La Paz, Mexico
5.  Omar, Santana-Morales; Ecocimati AC, Ensenada, Mexico
6.  Felipe, Galván-Magaña; Instituto Politécnico Nacional, Centro Interdisciplinario de Ciencias Marinas, La Paz, Mexico
7.  Rogelio, González-Armas; Instituto Politécnico Nacional, Centro Interdisciplinario de Ciencias Marinas, La Paz, Mexico
8.  Christopher G., Lowe; Department of Biological Sciences, California State University Long Beach, Long Beach, California,
    USA
9. James T., Ketchum; Pelagios-Kakunjá, La Paz, Mexico
10. Héctor, Villalobos; Instituto Politécnico Nacional, Centro Interdisciplinario de Ciencias Marinas, La Paz, Mexico

1. Fine-scale movement patterns are driven by both biotic (hunting, physiological needs) and abiotic (environmental conditions)
factors. The energy balance governs all movement-related strategic decisions.
2. Marine environments can be better understood by considering the vertical component. From 24 acoustic trackings of 10
white sharks in Guadalupe Island, this study linked, for the first time, horizontal and vertical movement data and inferred six
different behavioral states along with movement states, through the use of hidden Markov models, which allowed to draw a
comprehensive picture of white shark behavior.
3. Traveling was the most frequent state of behavior for white sharks, carried out mainly at night and twilight. In contrast, area-
restricted searching was the least used, occurring primarily in daylight hours.
4. Time of day, distance to shore, total shark length, and, to a lesser extent, tide phase affected behavioral states. Chumming
activity reversed, in the short term and in a nonpermanent way, the behavioral pattern to a general diel vertical pattern.

Keywords: Bayesian inference; behavioral states; energy costs; movement strategies; telemetry
SI Abstract Ref: 136




Enrico Gennari
Oceans Research Institute; Rhodes University; South African Institute for Aquatic Biodiversity

Exploring recovery potential for white sharks (Carcharodon carcharias) in South Africa: is prohibition
enough?
1.    Heather Bowlby; Fisheries and Oceans Canada.
2.    Neil Hammerschlag; University of Miami.
3.    Dylan Irion; University of Cape Town.
4.    Enrico Gennari; Oceans Research Institute; South African Institute for Aquatic Biodiversity

It is often difficult to assess whether a prohibition to exploitation ensures effective conservation or recovery of a particular at-
risk, wide-ranging species, especially with the continued presence of different types of pressures within its range. This is the
case for white sharks (Carcharodon carcharias) in South Africa, which have been protected since 1991, yet recent declines at
two aggregation sites have renewed conservation concerns. Empirical data on population dynamics, the magnitude of threats,
and regional trends for white sharks remain largely unavailable, yet there is widespread acceptance that conservation decision-
making should not be delayed because of scientific uncertainty. To facilitate a better understanding of risks for precautionary
management and provide guidance on possible appropriate actions, we used simulation modeling of four life history scenarios
for white sharks contextualizing the potential population response to multiple levels of mortality. Most scenarios indicated that
annual removals in the tens of individuals would substantially limit the potential for and magnitude of any abundance increase
following prohibition in 1991. Average known removals of white sharks occurring in the bather protection program since the
1990s have typically remained higher than these thresholds. If white sharks in southern Africa are characterized by a long
lifespan and/or a small population size, the proportion of trajectories declining or becoming functionally extinct over the next
generation could be substantial. These results highlight the specific challenges associated with the conservation of white
sharks in South Africa, and suggest new mitigation measures may be required to ensure functional protection and population
recovery

Keywords: white shark; conservation; bather protection program; management; South Africa
SI Abstract Ref: 108




                                                                    Sharks International 2022                           Page 27
Adrienne Gooden
Finders University - Southern Shark Ecology Group

Does the increase in shark activity during wildlife tourism lead to higher energy expenditure?
1.    Thomas, Clarke; Flinders University - Southern Shark Ecology Group, Department for Environment and Water
2.    Lauren, Meyer; Flinders University - Southern Shark Ecology Group, Georgia Aquarium
3.    Charlie, Huveneers; Flinders University - Southern Shark Ecology Group

Wildlife tourism is expanding globally, as is the need to quantify its potential impacts on the species targeted. Studies on the
effects of marine tourism on wildlife have focused on documenting behavioural changes, but whether changes affect the energy
budgets and ultimately the survival prospects of these animals is largely unknown. This is particularly acute for large predatory
sharks that are difficult to study but economically and ecologically important. To assess the effects of cage-diving on white
shark energy expenditure, animal-borne cameras and accelerometer loggers were deployed on 18 white sharks (Carcharodon
carcharias) at a cage-diving site (Neptune Islands Group Marine Park, South Australia) and recorded shark activity for 489.7
hours. A random forest machine learning algorithm was used to predict their behaviours based on 38 hours of acceleration data
with behaviours confirmed from the animal-borne cameras. The presence of cage-diving vessels led to white sharks being
more active and spending more time undertaking high-energy swimming (vectorial dynamic body acceleration [VeDBA] 0.04 ±
0.03 g; doubled) and bursts of acceleration (VeDBA 0.44 ± 0.38 g; seven-fold increase) while decreasing low-energy swimming
(1.5-fold). However, once put in the context of daily energy budget and accounting for the duration of cage-diving activities (~6
hours per day), energy expenditure was not substantially affected by wildlife tourism, with the presence of cage-diving vessels
leading to a 0.4 – 0.7 % increase in field metabolic rate. Our findings show that while food-based tourism can influence white
shark behaviour and increase energy expenditure, the overall impact on field metabolic rates and daily energy budget is
minimal.

Keywords: animal-borne camera; accelerometer; metabolic rate; wildlife tourism; machine learning
SI Abstract Ref: 92




Charlie Huveneers
Flinders University

Effects of wildlife tourism on white shark associative behaviour and implications for human safety
1.    Yuri Niella, Flinders University
2.    Hugh Pederson, Innovasea
3.    Charlie Huveneers, Flinders University

While wildlife tourism may impact the animals involved, it plays a critical role in public awareness and conservation.
Understanding changes in animal behaviour in response to tourism activities can inform the trade-offs between negative
impacts and socio-economic benefits. The movements of 133 white sharks were tracked with acoustic telemetry between 2013
and 2021 at the Neptune Islands, Australia. We used three behavioural variables (i.e. daily hours of arrival, time spent near
cage-diving boats, and residency) to investigate whether tourism led to associative behaviour using a combination of k-mean
clustering with Generalised Additive Mixed Models and Analysis of Variance. Significant clusters of individuals were found for
all variables tested. Different trends were observed between clusters, but did not vary within individuals from the same clusters,
suggesting complex individual temperaments. Some sharks tended to arrive earlier to match the arrival of the cage-diving
boats, but the residency and amount of time near cage-diving boats significantly decreased over time for some other sharks.
There are public claims that cage-diving activities may condition white sharks to interact with boats, potentially increasing shark
bite risk. Our results suggest that while some associative behaviour was observed, a loss of response to the stimulus used to
attract sharks, i.e. habituation, might also be occurring. White shark cage-diving is a global industry, dispelling myths about
these iconic animals. Despite complex temperaments across different individuals, long-term impacts of cage-diving activities
affecting white shark behaviour are unlikely.

Keywords: wildlife tourism; cognitive behaviour; associative learning; acoustic tracking; white shark cage-diving

The influence of environmental factors on individual behaviour of bait-attracted white sharks in South
Africa.
1.    Olga, M. Azevedo; Department of Biology, Ecology and Earth Sciences, University of Calabria, Italy.
2.    Ana, M. Correia; Interdisciplinary Centre of Marine and Environmental Research; Department of Biology, Faculty of
      Sciences, University of Porto, Portugal.
3.    Giuseppe, Rijllo; Department of Biology, Ecology and Earth Sciences, University of Calabria, Italy.
4.    Gianni, Giglio; Department of Biology, Ecology and Earth Sciences, University of Calabria, Italy.
5.    Chiara, Romano; Department of Biology, Ecology and Earth Sciences, University of Calabria, Italy.
6.    Francesca, Reinero; The Sharks Studies Centre, Scientific Institute, Italy.
7.    Primo, Micarelli; The Sharks Studies Centre, Scientific Institute, Italy.
8.    Emilio, Sperone; Department of Biology, Ecology and Earth Sciences, University of Calabria, Italy.


Feared by some and loved by others, the white shark (Carcharodon carcharias) is a charismatic species, targeted by different
research studies over the last decades. Advances in biologging tags allowed to track the hidden distribution of these predators.
However, there is still a lack of knowledge about the influence of environmental factors on their behaviour. Indeed, considering
the global distribution of white sharks, it is a challenge to understand the processes influencing their lifecycle, and to assess the
anthropogenic disturbances to their populations. Dyer Island in South Africa is a well-known aggregation area for Carcharodon
carcharias, with an established cage-diving ecotourism industry. This activity was used as a platform of opportunity to collect
data on the sharks’ behaviour. We applied an ethological approach to investigate the influence of environmental factors on the
complexity of individual behaviours. The biotic conditions of the moment had a different impact on the reactions of females and
males towards the bait. Females performed a higher number of behaviours under daylight, lower sea surface temperatures,
short wavelets, clear skies, under La Niña events, and high tides. Males behaved more complexly at dusk, medium sea surface
temperatures, large wavelets, few clouds, hight tides and elevated moonlight. White sharks often swim over recreational waters
or fishing areas and human encounters, fishing mortality, and climate change are pressures that need to be addressed. The
applied ethological approach increases knowledge on how the species responds to environmental factors, therefore informing
policy-makers and supporting its conservation.

Keywords: white shark; environmental factors; behaviour

Sian Liddy
University of Sydney, School of Life and Environmental Sciences

Characterising shark holobionts, the myth of the lone predator
1.    Sian Liddy; University of Sydney
2.    Amy Smoothey; NSW Department of Primary Industries
3.    Jadranka Nappi; University of New South Wales
4.    Sebastian Vadillo Gonzalez; University of Sydney
5.    Torsten Thomas; University of New South Wales
6.    Ezequiel Marzinelli; University of Sydney

Shark populations are declining globally, yet many aspects of their physiology and life history are still poorly understood. Most
of our understanding and management of sharks is based on our knowledge of their biology and interactions with other macro-
organisms. Sharks live, however, in the ocean in a “soup” of microorganisms. Evidence from systems as diverse as plants,
invertebrates and humans shows that microorganisms associated with these hosts play critical roles in their health and
function. Our understanding of host-microbiome interactions in sharks is limited, but such knowledge can help develop more
robust conservation management tools. Here, we characterised the microbiome of Great White, Bull, Port Jackson and Dusky
Whaler sharks in Australia via 16S rRNA gene amplicon sequencing. To understand what influences variation in microbial
community structure within and between shark individuals and species, we assessed bacteria associated to functionally distinct
body regions within each shark. There was strong variation between body regions, as well as between species. We discuss
potential drivers of such variation and the microbial taxa and functions associated with these differences. This is the first step in
understanding the role microbes play in the health and function of sharks.

Keywords: holobiont; microbiome; Carcharodon carcharias; Carcharhinus leucas; Heterodontus portusjacksoni

Li Jin Chai
Marine Research and Conservation Foundation

Malaysia Whale Sharks: distribution, seasonality and threats
1.    Li Jin Chai, Borneo Marine Research Institute, Universiti Malaysia Sabah, Jalan UMS, 88400 Kota Kinabalu Sabah,
      Malaysia; Malaysia Whale Sharks, Kota Kinabalu, Sabah, Malaysia
2.    Gonzalo Araujo, Malaysia Whale Sharks, Kota Kinabalu, Sabah, Malaysia; Marine Research and Conservation
      Foundation, Somerset, UK
3.    Nasrulhakim bin Maidin, Sabah Parks, P.O. Box 10626, 88806 Kota Kinabalu, Sabah, Malaysia
4.    Elvin M. Bavoh, Sabah Parks, P.O. Box 10626, 88806 Kota Kinabalu, Sabah, Malaysia
5.    Ruzlee Jumatin, Sabah Department of Fisheries, 4th Floor, Wisma Pertanian Sabah, Jalan Tasik, 88624 Kota Kinabalu,
      Sabah, Malaysia
6.    B. Mabel Manjaji-Matsumoto, Borneo Marine Research Institute, Universiti Malaysia Sabah, Jalan UMS, 88400 Kota
      Kinabalu Sabah, Malaysia; Malaysia Whale Sharks, Kota Kinabalu, Sabah, Malaysia

Whale sharks (Rhincodon typus) were targeted by fisheries in Asia into the 2000s leading to declines in regional abundance
and its uplisting to ‘Endangered’ in the IUCN Red List. The Philippines hosts one of the largest populations globally, and
Indonesia hosts at least five known aggregation sites. In contrast, little is known about whale sharks in Malaysia. Here, we used
citizen science, survey effort and data mining to understand the species in Malaysia. We recorded 162 whale shark encounters
from news outlets, social media posts and contributions to the global database ‘Sharkbook.ai’ from 1996-2021. Of these, 80
whale sharks were identified using their unique spot patterns ranging 3.5-6.0 m in total length, most of whose sex was
undetermined (83%). This juvenile-dominated population demographic is consistent with neighbouring countries. Using photo-
ID one individual was matched between Pulau Redang and Chumphon in Thailand. Similarly, a whale shark from Philippines
was matched to Pulau Sipadan, Sabah. Both Sipadan and Redang have the highest incidences of whale sharks in Malaysia,
where these sharks appear to be navigating through like that observed elsewhere (e.g. Galapagos). In contrast, whale sharks
encountered along the west coast of Sabah (e.g. Pulau Gaya) appear to be capitalising on zooplankton. Through citizen
science and online reports, we report at least four whale sharks mortalities in Malaysia from anthropogenic causes (plastic
ingestion and direct harvest). Further work is needed to elucidate whale shark movements within and away from Malaysia, as
well as understanding their feeding ecology and persistent threats.

Keywords: Rhincodon typus; endangered; citizen science; Southeast Asia; population demographics
SI Abstract Ref: 320




Harriet Allen
The Shark Trust

The Great Eggcase Hunt - 20 years of citizen science
1.    Harriet, Allen; The Shark Trust
2.    Cat, Gordon; The Shark Trust
3.    Ali, Hood; The Shark Trust

The Great Eggcase Hunt is a citizen science initiative that has been running since 2003. In nearly 20 years, the project has
received >22,000 submissions from ~7,500 people, totalling ~334,000 eggcases from 18 oviparous elasmobranchs around the
British Isles. This dataset provides an opportunity to monitor public engagement with the project, tracking its growth and spread
over both space and time. Analysis of this dataset can be used to pick out patterns, such as the seasonality of public
engagement, and to identify locations that are lacking in records and may require more effort. The project has grown
considerably since its beginning; from 54 submissions totalling 128 eggcases, from 25 locations around the British Isles (mostly
in the Southwest where the Shark Trust is based) in its first year, to ~1,100 submissions totalling >27,500 eggcases, from >700
locations (covering the majority of the British Isles from the Isle of Wight to Orkney) in the first half of 2022 alone. As well as
citizen science engagement, the data from this project allows hotspots for eggcases and potential egg-laying grounds to be
identified, as well as further developing our understanding of eggcase morphology (e.g., the Common Skate complex).
Although established in the British Isles, the Great Eggcase Hunt receives submissions from around the world, including across
Europe and the Mediterranean, as well as Australia, South Africa, and the USA. The project continues to grow, with
identification resources in development for different regions and an app to make the submission process even easier.

Keywords: citizen science; eggcases; oviparous; Rajidae; Scyliorhinidae

Elitza Germanov
Marine Megafauna Foundation, West Palm Beach, Florida, United States of America 1; Centre for Sustainable
Aquatic Ecosystems, Harry Butler Institute, Murdoch University, Perth, Western Australia, Australia 2;
Environmental and Conservation Sciences, Murdoch University, Perth, Western Australia, Australia 3

A decade of manta ray research and conservation in Indonesia
1.    Elitza S. Germanov
2.    Andrea D. Marshall
3.    Simon J. Pierce
4.    Lars Bejder; Marine Mammal Research Program, Hawaii Institute of Marine Biology, University of Hawaii at
1.    Manoa, Honolulu, Hawaii, United States
5.    I. Gede Hendrawan; Faculty of Marine Sciences and Fisheries, Universitas Udayana, Denpasar, Bali, Indonesia
6.    Neil Loneragan; Faculty of Fisheries and Marine Science, Bogor Institute of Agriculture, Bogor, West Java, Indonesia

Manta rays have some of the lowest estimated population growth rates for elasmobranchs, making them susceptible to drastic
declines from human threats. A decade ago, Indonesia simultaneously hosted one of the largest fisheries for mantas, along
with a major manta ray tourism industry. The high value of tourism was a strong argument for protection, but knowledge of
habitat use was necessary to create effective policy. Galvanizing tourism operators and the public, we used citizen science to
address knowledge gaps, identify manta ray hotspots, and provide baseline population data. We showed that manta rays
routinely moved beyond established conservation and tourism areas into areas of high fishing activity, warranting nationwide
species protection. That was then legislated in 2014.

Subsequently, we have provided longer-term characterizations of the habitat use, demographics, and remaining human threats
to manta ray aggregations within the Nusa Penida Marine Protected Area (MPA) and the Komodo National Park (NP). Using a
combination of active researcher and citizen science we established a high degree of site affinity to two sites within the Nusa
Penida MPA. One site was crucial for adult cleaning and mating, and the other for feeding juveniles. Habitat use in Komodo NP
follows a similar pattern, with four key sites identified.

Ongoing threats to manta rays in these areas include entanglement in fishing gear, plastic pollution, and growing tourism.
Future research aims to assess population dynamics and trends, and to characterize additional critical habitats in the region.


Keywords: photo-ID; population structure; movement; site use; coral triangle region

Much ado about stingrays
1.    Jennifer Keeping, University of Glasgow
2.    David Bailey, University of Glasgow
3.    Charlotte Hopkins, University of Hull

This presentation intends to showcase the works completed during my MSc by research projects concerning the stingrays of
southern Mozambique, the research gaps I have identified therein and the preliminary results of my forthcoming PhD projects.
I intend to summarise the findings of the two published manuscripts from my MSc studies; the first detailing the validation of the
computer-aided, photo-identification of the smalleye stingray, Megatrygon microps, which is among the largest and most unique
species of stingrays in the world. The second manuscript describes the trends in the sightings of stingray in southern
Mozambique, using citizen-science collected data to determine seasonality and small-scale habitat movement of stingrays in
the diving region of Tofo, Mozambique.
I am now undergoing PhD research to continue to work on filling the many research gaps that I have identified during the MSc
work. My PhD topics include the use of stable isotope analysis to determine feeding ecology, habitat use and movements of
multiple stingray species in the Inhambane provincial region. These data will compliment the photo-ID and diver observations
already completed, whilst increasing our knowledge surrounding the differing trophic roles of each stingray species, their
importance to the ecosystem and, most notably, reveal more about their movements to the ocean zones that are unavailable
with the satellite and acoustic tags traditionally used in movement telemetry studies.


Keywords: stingray; mozambique; ecology; stable isotope; photo-ID

Georgia Jones

UK recreational shark angler practices, perceptions and attitudes; bridging the gap between scientists
and anglers for the benefit of sharks
1.    Jones, G.C.A.
2.    Murphy, S.
3.    Rudd, H.
4.    Somerfield, P.
5.    Thomas, S.F.
6.    Stewart, B.D.

Recreational anglers are important stakeholders in global fisheries and have capacity to provide valuable data for scientific
research and conservation management of threatened species. Recreational angling for sharks has a long history in the UK,
and is seeing a renewed surge in popularity, but tensions exist between anglers and scientists over mistrust of how data will be
used and issues around some cases of poor shark handling. The result has been a loss of data sharing and a stymied
understanding of UK shark populations, especially for several threatened species that are considered data-poor. Here we
present the results of the first project initiated by a new collaboration ‘Shark Hub UK’, which aims to bring anglers and scientists
together as equals, for sharks. Our questionnaire was completed by 169 anglers from right around the UK and provided novel
insights into the distribution and intensity of recreational angling activities for UK sharks, the handling methods currently
employed and angler attitudes towards shark management and data sharing. The results will guide future shark handling best-
practice guidance for anglers, rebuild trust between anglers and scientists, and highlight avenues for key future research into
UK shark ecology and conservation.

Keywords: recreational angling; best-practice handling; UK sharks; stakeholder engagement

Katie Hindle
A Rocha Kenya

Biodiversity and conservation of elasmobranchs in Watamu Marine National Park, Kenya
1.    Katie, Hindle: A Rocha Kenya.
2.    Eric, Thuranira; A Rocha Kenya.
3.    Peter, Musila; A Rocha Kenya.
4.    Colin, Jackson; A Rocha Kenya.
5.    Stanley, Baya; A Rocha Kenya.
6.    Queen, Harare; A Rocha Kenya.
7.    Robert, Sluka; A Rocha International.

The no-take MPA in Watamu Kenya houses a diversity of IUCN Red List threatened elasmobranchs and serves as a nursery
area for at least one of these species. The surrounding community is growing in its understanding of the intrinsic and natural
capital value of these species through creative educational opportunities both in schools and among artisanal fishers.

Ten elasmobranch species were observed using four methods (BRUVs, Timed Swims, Beach walks, and Diving surveys):
Taeniura lymma, Glaucostegus halavi, Carcharhinus melanopterus, Neotrygon caeruleopunctata, Himantura gerrardi,
Himantura uarnak, Triaenodon obesus, Torpedo sinuspersici, Aetobatus narinari, and Rhincodon typus. The most abundant
species were Carcharhinus melanopterus, Taeniura lymma, and Neotrygon caeruleopunctata. Elasmobranchs were observed
most often over sand habitat (56%) followed by seagrass (33%), and coral (11%).

Larger Blacktip reef sharks (Carcharhinus melanopterus), majority females were seen August – November. Most small pups
appeared along the shallow sandy areas along the length of WMNP’s beach from October onwards with a decline of larger
sharks around this time. Footage of a shark that appears to show afterbirth with a larger male following was recorded in early
November possibly indicating WMNP as an important pupping ground.

Environmental education created awareness among community members. Programs were conducted in 8 schools reaching
320 students and 10 community groups reaching 237 individuals. An artisanal fishing competition was held for the first time
which involved the use of hooks of a specific size to emphasize the importance of sustainable fishing methods and
conservation of elasmobranchs.


Keywords: MPA; nursery; guitarfish; shark; ray

Jennifer Olbers
WILDTRUST

A cross-disciplinary approach to the protection of South Africa’s threatened sharks and rays
1.    Jennifer, Olbers; WILDTRUST
2.    Nikki, Chapman; WILDTRUST
3.    Sandile, Ntuli; WILDTRUST
4.    Summer, Newton; WILDTRUST
5.    Jean, Harris; WILDTRUST
6.    Lauren, van Nijkerk; WILDTRUST
7.    Geremy Cliff; Research Associate of University of KwaZulu-Natal

South Africa is a global hotspot for chondrichthyan diversity while providing important habitats for many threatened species.
Despite a rich history of shark research and management in South Africa, there are knowledge gaps, inadequate statutory
interventions ensuring adequate protection, and a lack of capacity to effectively enforce current legal provisions. Furthermore,
public perceptions about sharks are in general, negative, and based on incorrect information. To improve the protection of
threatened (Vulnerable, Endangered, and Critically Endangered) species, prioritizing South African endemics, the WILDTRUST
through their marine programme, WILDOCEANS, together with the Shark Conservation Fund and many other partners,
implemented a 3-year Shark and Ray Protection Project. A campaign called Shark Attack was launched in March 2020,
highlighting the plight of South Africa’s chondrichthyans and actively attempting to shift negative perceptions and inspire
positive action through its website, social media platforms, and traditional media. The following content are available for
download online: database and annotated checklist comprising all known chondrichthyan species found in South African
waters, detailed status reports on 90 threatened and/or endemic species, a field guide of threatened and endemic species for
law enforcement officers, online species ID workshops, draft recommendations for improved statutory modifications to SA
legislation, shark and ray systematic conservation plan report, with associated R-scripts, highlighting essential habitats for
shark and ray MPA’s or shark sanctuaries, trade toolkit for law enforcement in numerous ports of entry and various information
pack and policy brief for decision-makers. The presentation aims to highlight the successes of this collaborative project.

Evan Prasky
Northeastern University

Quantifying angler perceptions about shark depredation through stakeholder engagement
1.    Evan, Prasky; Northeastern University
2.    Marcus, Drymon; Mississippi State University
3.    Amanda, Jefferson ; Mississippi–Alabama Sea Grant
4.    Mandy, Karnauskas; National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration
5.    Savannah, Swinea; Northeastern University
6.    Steven, Scyphers; University of South Alabama

The Gulf of Mexico (GoM) is home to 31 federally managed reef fish species that are both commercially and recreationally
important for communities in the region. These fish are federally managed through a series of stock assessments and a
scientific understanding of natural lifecycles. Depredation, defined as the partial or complete removal of a captured species by a
non-target species, is a cause of increased mortality and is not well quantified or understood. Mounting voices from anglers in
the community suggest depredation is not only a prevalent and pervasive issue across the Gulf but also is rapidly increasing.
Because of this, fisheries managers cannot effectively capture true population numbers derived from stock assessments.
Depredation is a complex social-ecological issue that occurs between a multitude of species and is not required to be reported.
Therefore, we have turned to anglers across the GoM to gain insights into their perceptions, interactions, and identification of
the main causes and drivers of depredation to better assist in the classification and characterization to increase the accuracy of
fisheries managers’ stock assessments. To do this, we distributed a Gulf-wide survey (n = 1044) that focused on building
cognitive maps to highlight the perceptions anglers have on depredation. We also asked questions that quantified key factors
that have influenced depredation, such as seasonal variation and fishing habits, to better understand the human-wildlife conflict
that depredation is causing. Using the information gathered, we can begin to more accurately assess how depredation impacts
anglers and stock assessments.

Keywords: shark depredation; human-wildlife conflict; cognitive maps; community perceptions; stakeholder attitudes

Sensory Biology, Feeding Habits & Metabolism 1


Robert Priester
Okeanos – University of the Azores

Trophic ecology and coastal habitat use of two sympatric shark species in the Azores archipelago (mid-
Atlantic) assessed with CNS stable isotope analysis
1.    C. Robert, Priester; Okeanos – University of the Azores.
2.    Jan, Dierking; GEOMAR Helmholtz Centre for Ocean Research Kiel.
3.    David, Abecasis; CCMAR – Centre of Marine Sciences, University of Algarve
4.    Pedro, Afonso; IMAR/Okeanos – University of the Azores

The anthropogenic exploitation of marine resources has caused drastic declines of large predatory fishes. Amongst these,
sharks are of major conservation concern due to their high vulnerability to overfishing and role as top predators. The Azores
Islands, Mid Atlantic, are thought to serve as essential fish habitat (EFH - waters necessary to fish for spawning, breeding,
feeding or growth to maturity) for tope (Galeorhinus galeus) and smooth hammerhead (Sphyrna zygaena) sharks, but data to
better assess this role are scarce. To address existing knowledge gaps, here we provide the first comparative assessment of
the trophic ecology, ontogenetic shifts with increasing shark size and niche partitioning of G. galeus and S. zygeana around the
Azores based on simultaneous δ13C, δ15N and δ34S (CNS) stable isotope analysis. Overall, shark isotopic niches, obtained
using a 3D-Bayesian ellipse approach, indicated a strong role of mixed coastal-benthic resources, but with clear differences
between life stages consistent with intra- and interspecific niche partitioning. Whereas tope shark showed a significant
ontogenetic shift to higher trophic levels within the coastal-benthic prey niche, smooth hammerhead sharks exhibited significant
decreases in δ34S, consistent with an increasing reliance on coastal-benthic prey with growing size. The co-occurrence of
juveniles of two shark species of high conservation concern, and the role of coastal-benthic prey in their diet, highlight the
importance of healthy coastal habitats for the conservation of these emblematic predators in the wider Atlantic, and of the
important role of Azores coastal areas as EFH.

Keywords: Simultaneous carbon nitrogen and sulfur stable isotope analysis; 3D niche hypervolume; niche partitioning;
communal shark nursery; ontogenetic shift

Sabrina Riverón
Marine Predator Research Group, School of Natural Sciences, Macquarie University

Morphology influences niche dynamics in two large sympatric sharks from Uruguayan waters
1.    Sabrina, Riverón; Marine Predator Research Group, School of Natural Sciences, Macquarie University.
2.    Vincent, Raoult; School of Environmental and Life Sciences, University of Newcastle; Marine Ecology Group, School of
      Natural Sciences, Macquarie University.
3.    David J., Slip; Taronga Institute of Science and Learning, Taronga Conservation Society Australia.
4.    Federico, Mas; National Directorate of Aquatic Resources (DINARA).
5.    Martín, Laporta; National Directorate of Aquatic Resources (DINARA), Atlantic Fisheries Management Unit; Campus
      DoMar, International Campus of Excellence, University of Santiago de Compostela.
6.    Inés, Pereyra; National Directorate of Aquatic Resources (DINARA), Atlantic Fisheries Management Unit.
7.    Santiago, Silveira; National Directorate of Aquatic Resources (DINARA), Atlantic Fisheries Management Unit.
8.    Robert G., Harcourt; Marine Predator Research Group, School of Natural Sciences, Macquarie University.

Prey availability in an environment does not ensure that prey will be consumed by a predator, as they must also be detected,
captured and handled. Predator morphology imposes limitations on prey selection due to biomechanical constraints, making
some prey functionally inaccessible and thereby shaping predators’ trophic niches. We assessed how two important
components of morphology (tooth shape and body length) influence prey selectivity and trophic niche in two large-bodied
sympatric sharks with contrasting feeding modes. The first species capture prey using spear-shaped, grasping teeth
(Carcharias taurus), while the second has multi-cuspid cutting teeth used to serrate larger prey (Notorynchus cepedianus).
Stomach content analysis and isotopic values of δ13C, δ34S and δ15N from muscle and liver were used to characterize
trophic niche and prey selection. As gape-limited C. taurus grew, their consumption of teleosts decreased inversely to
chondrichthyans. By contrast, non-gape limited N. cepedianus consumed teleosts and chondrichthyans in similar proportions,
along with marine mammals, but with no clear relationship to body size. As body length increased, both species consumed prey
from higher trophic levels (higher δ15N), but N. cepedianus accessed prey at relatively higher trophic levels. δ13C and δ34S
remained substantially unchanged with body length since gape and dentition are less limiting for these tracers. Morphological
characters play an essential role in predator prey selection and may be the primary mechanism facilitating resource partitioning
in marine predators. Their inclusion in ecological studies will help understand prey choice and how it shapes trophodynamics in
marine ecosystems.

Keywords: marine predator; trophic ecology; stable isotopes; stomach contents; Southwestern Atlantic

Erin Spencer
Florida International University

Using biologgers to estimate energy expenditure and consumption rates of great hammerhead sharks
(Sphyrna mokarran)
1.    Erin Spencer, Florida International University
2.    Lisa Hoopes, Georgia Aquarium
3.    Gil Iosilevskii, Technion
4.    Yannis Papastamatiou, Florida International University

Top predators play an important role in ecosystem food webs, and quantifying predators’ energetic needs is key to
understanding their rate of consumption on lower tropic levels. Metabolic costs are the largest components of an organism’s
energy budget, and measuring an organism’s metabolic rate in the field provides an estimate of how much an organism must
consume. We used biologgers to record depth, temperature, speed, tri-axial acceleration and tri-axial magnetism of great
hammerhead sharks (Sphyrna mokarran) tagged off of the Atlantic and Gulf coasts of Florida. We then used previously-
established hydrodynamic models of great hammerheads derived from wind tunnel experiments to convert measures of swim
speed from sampled sharks into energy consumption rates. Preliminary analysis of four sharks indicate sampled individuals
would need to consume an average of approximately 1.27 lbs of food per day to account for metabolic costs, varying with
speed and size of the animal. These results further our understanding of the ecological role of great hammerheads, a critically
endangered predator, by allowing us to calculate how much prey they may be removing from local ecosystems.

Keywords: biologging; energetics; great hammerhead sharks; biologgers

Brianna Cahill
Harbor Branch Oceanographic Institute, Florida Atlantic University

Breaking bags and crunching clams: Assessing whitespotted eagle ray interactions with shellfish
aquaculture gear
1. Brianna, Cahill; Harbor Branch Oceanographic Institute, Florida Atlantic University.
2. Kayla, McCulloch; Rosenstiel School of Marine and Atmospheric Science, University of Miami.
3. Breanna, DeGroot; Harbor Branch Oceanographic Institute, Florida Atlantic University.
4. Kim, Bassos-Hull; Mote Marine Laboratory.
5, Matthew, Ajemian; Harbor Branch Oceanographic Institute, Florida Atlantic University.

Shallow coastal waters are commonly used in shellfish aquaculture for “grow-out” of bivalves like the Northern Quahog
(Mercenaria mercenaria). These grow-out locations have substantially higher clam densities than the surrounding environment
and potentially attract molluscivores. Inspired by clammer reports of predator-inflicted damage to grow-out gear, we assessed
the capacity of a highly mobile predator (whitespotted eagle ray Aetobatus narinari) to detect and interact with clams housed
within a suite of industry standard antipredator materials. Experiments were conducted in mesocosms where rays were
exposed to unprotected clams (control), clams inside polyester mesh clam bags (dipped in a latex net coating and non-dipped),
and under high density polyethylene (HDPE) or chicken wire cover netting. Gear interactions were quantified from video
footage throughout the course of the experiment and clam mortality was assessed after its completion. Rays interacted with
antipredator gear significantly more than control plots, with particularly long durations noted for clam bags covered with HDPE
netting. While rays were capable of consuming clams through bags, antipredator treatments reduced clam mortality by 4-10x
compared to control plots. Double-layered (i.e., bags with cover netting) treatments exhibited the lowest clam mortality
(0.6±0.1%), highlighting the utility of this type of protection in shellfish aquaculture.


Keywords: Batoid; shellfisheries; behavioral ecology
