From data deficient to big data in shark conservation
1.  Francesco Ferretti; Department of Fish and Wildlife Conservation, Virginia Tech, Blacksburg, Virginia, USA
2.  Jeremy Jenrette; Department of Fish and Wildlife Conservation, Virginia Tech, Blacksburg, Virginia, USA
3.  Filippo Bargnesi; Polytechnic University of Marche, Ancona, Italy
4.  Stefano Moro; Stazione Zoologica Anton Dohrn, Rome, Italy
5.  Zac Liu; Hopkins Marine Station of Stanford University, Pacific Grove California, USA
6.  Cheryl Butner; Hopkins Marine Station of Stanford University, Pacific Grove California, USA
7.  Edward Fox; Department of Computer Science, Virginia Tech, Blacksburg, Virginia, USA
8.  Steven Haddock; Monterey Bay Aquarium Research Institute, Moss Landing, California, USA
9.  Trevor Hastie; Department of Statistics and Department of Biomedical Data Science, Stanford University, Palo Alto,
    California, USA
10. Salvador Jorgensen; University of California Santa Cruz, Santa Cruz, California, USA
11. Fiorenza Micheli; Hopkins Marine Station of Stanford University, Pacific Grove California, USA

Sharks are declining rapidly because of their vulnerable life histories and the direct and indirect impacts of fishing and habitat
modification. For this reason, they are among the most endangered animals in the ocean. Meanwhile, they are also among the
most poorly quantified groups of vertebrates on the planet, as basic information on abundance, distribution, and life-history is
lacking for most species. Hence there is an urgent need to collect and analyze data on sharks to increase our understanding of
their ecology, develop effective conservation actions, and reverse their loss. Here we show how crowdsourcing, web data-
mining, and machine learning techniques can fill this critical information gap. We are building the largest database of shark
occurrence records by implementing sharkPulse, a modular crowd-sourcing platform that combines smartphone and web
applications, social network and website crawling, and image analysis to fill knowledge gaps in global shark populations. We
will illustrate the architecture of this platform and show how hundreds of thousands of online images can be sourced and
transformed into shark sightings. We explore the opportunities from crowdsourcing, artificial intelligence, big-data approaches,
and social network mining to quickly and cost-effectively fill major knowledge gaps in shark distribution and characterize their
status and drivers of decline. We also list a series of challenges that need to be addressed to efficiently source this information
continuously and produce efficient and real-time monitoring of global shark populations.

Keywords: citizen science; web data-mining; machine learning; big data; global shark populations
SI Abstract Ref: 337




Jeremy Jenrette
Virginia Polytechnic Institute and State University

Shark detection and classification with machine learning
Jenrette, Jeremy., ZY-C, Liu., Chimote, Pranav., Fox, Edward., Hastie, Trevor., and Ferretti, Francesco

Suitable shark conservation depends on well-informed population assessments. Direct methods such as scientific surveys and
fisheries monitoring are adequate for defining population statuses, but species-specific indices of abundance and distribution
coming from these sources are rare for most shark species. We can rapidly fill these information gaps by boosting media-based
remote monitoring efforts with machine learning and automation.
We created a database of 53,345 shark images covering 219 species of sharks, and packaged object-detection and image
classification models into a Shark Detector bundle. The Shark Detector recognizes and classifies sharks from videos and
images using transfer learning and convolutional neural networks (CNNs). We applied these models to common data-
generation approaches of sharks: collecting occurrence records from photographs taken by the public or citizen scientists,
processing baited remote camera footage and online videos, and data-mining Instagram. We examined the accuracy of each
model and tested genus and species prediction correctness as a result of training data quantity.
The Shark Detector can classify 47 species pertaining to 26 genera. It sorted heterogeneous datasets of images sourced from
Instagram with 91% accuracy and classified species with 70% accuracy. It located sharks in baited remote footage and
YouTube videos with 89% accuracy, and classified located subjects to the species level with 69% accuracy. All data-generation
methods were processed without manual interaction.
As media-based remote monitoring appears to dominate methods for observing sharks in nature, we developed an open-
source Shark Detector to facilitate common identification applications. Prediction accuracy of the software pipeline increases as
more images are added to the training dataset. We provide public access to the software on our GitHub page.

Keywords: data-mining; image classification; Instagram; machine learning; sharks

Kathryn Anne Ayres
CICIMAR-IPN; Pelagios Kakunja

Seasonal aggregations and movements of blacktip sharks (Carcharhinus limbatus) at Cabo Pulmo
National Park, Gulf of California, Mexico
1.    Kathryn, Ayres; CICIMAR-IPN.
2.    James, Ketchum; Pelagios Kakunja.
3.    Rogelio, González Armas; CICIMAR-IPN.
4.    Felipe, Galván Magaña; CICIMAR-IPN.
5.    Alex, Hearn; Universidad San Francisco de Quito.
6.    Fernando, Elorriaga Verplancken; CICIMAR-IPN.
7.    Edgar Mauricio, Hoyos-Padilla; Pelagios Kakunja.
8.    Stephen, Kajiura; Florida Atlantic University.


Blacktip shark (Carcharhinus limbatus) aggregations can be observed along the coastline of Cabo Pulmo National Park
(CPNP), a no-take marine protected area in the Gulf of California. Unoccupied Aerial Vehicle (UAV) surveys were completed bi-
weekly (every two weeks) over a stretch of sandy habitat for three consecutive years between January 2019 and December
2021. C. limbatus abundance ranged between 1 and 1229 sharks (282 ± 43, mean ± SE) in winter months (December – April),
when sea surface temperature (SST) was below 25 °C. Time of day, year and photoperiod were also significant predictors (p <
0.01) of abundance according to the Generalized Additive Model (GAM). Results from sharks tagged with acoustic (n = 20) and
satellite tags (n = 4) indicated a northerly migration in late spring, before returning to CPNP in winter.

Keywords: UAV; aerial survey; acoustic telemetry; satellite tracking; Generalized Additive Model

Threatened Species – Spotlight on Angel Sharks, Sawfish & Rhinorays 4


Charlotte Pike
Zoological Society of London | Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands

Establishing a method to identify and prioritise areas for protection of Angelshark (Squatina squatina) in
the Canary Islands.
1.    Maria Belén Caro; Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands
2.    Hector Toledo; Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands
3.    David Jiménez Alvarado; Universidad de Las Palmas de Gran Canaria
4.    Eva Meyers; Zoological Research Museum Alexander Koenig & Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands
5.    Joanna Barker; Zoological Society of London & Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands
6.    Michael Sealey; Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands
7.    Charlotte Pike; Zoological Society of London & Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands
8.    Matthew Gollock; Zoological Society of London


The Angelshark (Squatina squatina) was formerly common across the East Atlantic and Mediterranean Seas, but has
undergone dramatic declines over the last 50 years. Despite this reduction, the Canary Islands remain a uniquely large
stronghold for the species, where Angelsharks can still be observed frequently. Protection of Angelsharks in the Canary Islands
is therefore of vital importance to ensure the survival of the species, as threats such as habitat loss and accidental capture in
fisheries still impact Angelsharks in these waters.
In 2019, Angelsharks were listed in the Spanish Endangered Species List for Canary Islands waters, under the category “In
danger of extinction”. In accordance with this listing, it was required that a Recovery Plan be developed, including the
delineation of areas relevant to the conservation of the Angelsharks (ARCAs), where specific measures should be
implemented.
In order to identify ARCAs in the Canary Islands, Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands (ASP:CI) completed geospatial analysis
of Angelshark distribution using available data, from both scientific surveys and citizen science sightings. Using this data,
alongside current knowledge of Angelshark ecology, ASP:CI were able to determine specific criteria to delineate ARCAs in the
Canary Islands. We will present these methods whilst discussing the limitations related to designating critical areas for data
limited species, and highlighting the importance of translating scientific evidence into policy action.
This work, led by ASP:CI, was funded by Shark Conservation Fund and through a contract with the Canary Islands
Government, co-financed by the FEDER 2014-2020 Operational Program.

Keywords: Angelshark; distribution; mapping; spatial protection; management
SI Abstract Ref: 332




Veronika Biskis
University of the Sunshine Coast

Sawfish spotters fill data gaps critical for protection in Queensland, Australia
1.    Veronika Biskis, University of the Sunshine Coast.
2.    Dr Kathy Townsend, University of the Sunshine Coast
3.    Dr Barbara Wueringer, Sharks And Rays Australia

In Australia, effective management of critically endangered sawfishes is a difficult task, in part due to remaining habitat being
extremely remote and expensive to reach. Since the federal recovery plan was put in place in 2015, field investigation in
Queensland has been limited to a select few river systems, not allowing for a full-scale species distribution assessment.
In 2017, Sharks And Rays Australia launched a public sightings campaign targeted at recreational fishers to fill knowledge gaps
in population abundance and spatial ecology. Since then, over 950 submissions have been received Australia-wide from
members of the public. Data from Queensland encounters reported to have taken place between 1998 and 2022 were
analysed, representing equal temporal distribution before and after federal protection.
Sighting locations were analysed for association with commercial and recreational fishing pressure and environmental
protection measures. Size and year of capture were shown to be negatively correlated, even over a relatively short time span of
20 years. Condition of the animal also varied with species, size and method of capture, indicating a varied impact on sawfishes
through different fishing methods.
This study provides first insights into key habitat for different life history stages on the Queensland coast since becoming
federally protected. Additionally, findings highlight the importance of citizen science as a reliable data source, and a platform for
the direct involvement of the recreational fishing community in protecting critically endangered species.

Keywords: sawfish; citizen science; fisheries; distribution; conservation

                                                                     Sharks International 2022                          Page 80
Michael James Sealey
Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands

The value of citizen science “Count the Angels” events towards the study of Angelsharks (Squatina
squatina) in the Canary Islands.
1.    Michael James, Sealey; Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands
2.    Leire, Gabiola Hernández; Universidad de Alicante
3.    Joanna, Barker; Zoological Society of London & Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands
4.    Charlotte, Pike; Zoological Society of London & Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands
5.    Héctor, Toledo; Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands
6.    Belén, Caro; Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands
7.    Eva, Meyers; Zoological Research Museum Alexander Koenig & Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands
8.    David, Jiménez-Alvarado; Universidad de Las Palmas de Gran Canaria & Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands
9.    Alexander Lehnen; Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands


As the popularity of recreational scuba diving grows, so does the will of scuba divers to contribute towards the protection and
conservation of the marine environment. This growth has been accompanied by the establishment of a citizen science
movement aimed to engage and recruit scuba divers in data collection for research. This innovative tool has been used in the
Canary Islands to gather data on the Critically Endangered Angelshark (Squatina squatina) through an annual event named
Count the Angels (CTA), which has been completed for four years (2018-2021).

The CTA event is organised under the direction and supervision of the Angel Shark Project: Canary Islands. During four days,
participating dive centres and clubs carry out two coordinated dives at scheduled hours and register specific data, including
Angelshark sightings. The last CTA event was held from the 4 to the 7 of December 2021, with a total of 37 participating dive
centres and clubs, 107 dives registered and a total of 166 Angelsharks recorded throughout five of the Canary Islands. The
collected data has allowed different analysis including shark size and sex ratio, behaviour patterns, encounter probability and
abundance estimates for the different islands.

This unique and growing event is an example of how citizen science can be a valuable tool not only to support Angelshark
research, but also as a mechanism to create awareness and engage scuba divers towards the conservation of a Critically
Endangered species.

CTA events were funded by Shark Conservation Fund, Disney Conservation Fund and WWF Netherlands.


Keywords: Angelshark; canary islands; citizen science; scuba divers

Rima Jabado
Elasmo Project, Dubai, United Arab Emirates

Rhino rays – uncovering the drivers behind their exploitation in data-poor areas
1.  Rima W. Jabado, Elasmo Project, Dubai, United Arab Emirates
2.  Niang Alioune, Institut Mauritanien de Recherches Océanographiques et de Pêches (IMROP), Mauritania
3.  Mamadou Ba, Parc National du Banc d’Arguin (PNBA), Mauritania
4.  Luc B. Badji, Institut Universitaire de pêche et d’Aquaculture (IUPA), Dakar, Sénégal
5.  Alissa Barnes, Elasmo Project, Dubai, United Arab Emirates
6.  Helene Buchholzer, Blue Resources Trust, Colombo, Sri Lanka
7.  Daniel Fernando, Blue Resources Trust, Colombo, Sri Lanka
8.  Ramajeyam Gobiraj, Blue Resources Trust, Colombo, Sri Lanka
9.  Divya Karnad, Department of Environmental Studies, Ashoka University, Sonipat, Haryana, India
10. Buddhi Maheshika, Blue Resources Trust, Colombo, Sri Lanka
11. Sushmita Mukherji, CSIRO-UTAS Quantitative Marine Sciences PhD Program, Institute for Marine and Antarctic Studies,
    University of Tasmania, Hobart, Tasmania, Australia
12. Akshay Tanna, Blue Resources Trust, Colombo, Sri Lanka
13. Sahan Thilakaratna, Blue Resources Trust, Colombo, Sri Lanka


Rhino rays are facing a global conservation crisis. These 67 species (families Pristidae (Sawfishes), Glaucostegidae (Giant
Guitarfishes), Rhinidae (Wedgefishes), Rhinobatidae (Guitarfishes), and Trygonorrhinidae (Banjo Rays)) have limited
productivity making them extremely susceptible to overexploitation. Rhino rays face an uncertain future with 68.7% considered
threatened with extinction according to the IUCN Red List of Threatened Species. We focus on understanding the status and
uncovering the drivers behind their exploitation in four data-poor countries: India, Mauritania, Senegal, and Sri Lanka. These
species richness or endemicity hotspots are data-poor in terms of fisheries, have some of the poorest coastal communities, and
species’ distributions overlap with intense and increasing fishing pressure. Data were collected by interviewing fishers and
traders to record historical knowledge and current perceptions, as well as landing site surveys to collect data on species and



                                                                   Sharks International 2022                         Page 81
assess how fisheries impact them. Results suggest that species exploitation is not always driven by the fin trade but also by
demand for their meat, often consumed locally. Landing site surveys indicate a low abundance of species while fishers describe
historical high abundances, indicative of potential localized extinctions going unnoticed. The complexities in understanding
these fisheries at the local scale highlight that conventional fisheries management measures are unlikely to yield results,
especially when governance is weak in terms of enforcement and policy implementation. Adaptive local actions are needed
with solutions based on fisher engagement to ensure their livelihoods and nutritional needs are taken into consideration while
identifying potential mitigation measures or solutions to reduce demand.

Keywords: conservation; threatened species; guitarfish; wedgefish; Rhinopristiformes

Amanda Pontes Lopes
Laboratory of Environmental Health Assessment and Promotion, Instituto Oswaldo Cruz/Fiocruz, Av. Brazil, 4.365,
